\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(\Psi_{\sigma},M,F)\\
d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})<\epsilon\end{subarray}}\;\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}(|R_{r}^{i}-R^{j}_{r}-y|\leq 1)<Cr^{-d/2}\big(1+r^{-1/2}|x_{i}-x_{j}-y|\big)^{-K}.

We remark that this bound is fairly sharp; e.g., for a non-degenerate random walk on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, it is sharply obtained from the local central limit theorem that the density at time
r
has
L^{\infty}
norm bounded above by
Cr^{-d/2}
, and the above estimate generalizes this fact to more complicated Markov chains. Thus the above theorem is fairly intuitive, essentially saying that any Markov chain that does not “get stuck” near the boundaries of the Weyl chamber and resembles a random walk far from those boundaries still satisfies a similar bound.

We will often apply Theorem
4.7
to bound expectations of certain functionals of
R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}
. In particular, we will make extensive use of the following result:

Corollary 4.8
.

Let
F:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
be a decreasing function of exponential decay at infinity. Fix
\sigma>0
,
M>0
,
K>0
, and
d\geq 2
. Let
H:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
be decreasing and satisfy
H(x)\leq Ae^{-bx}
where
A,b>0
. Let
\boldsymbol{b}_{0}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(\Psi_{\sigma},M,F)
be centered and
\delta
-repulsive. Then there exists
\epsilon=\epsilon(F,\sigma,M,\delta,d)>0
and
C=C(F,\sigma,M,\delta,K,A,b,d)>0
such that uniformly over
\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}
,
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
r\geq 0
and all
f:I\to[0,\infty)
satisfying
|f(x)|\leq H(|x|)
one has

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(\Psi_{\sigma},M,F)\\
d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})<\epsilon\end{subarray}}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[f(R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-y)]<C\cdot Ab^{-d}\cdot r^{-\frac{d}{2}}\big(1+r^{-1/2}|x_{i}-x_{j}-y|\big)^{-K}.

In most applications,
H
will be fixed, so we will use the form of the bound with the right side given by
Cr^{-d/2}(1+r^{-1/2}|x_{i}-x_{j}-y|)^{-K}
, disregarding
A,b
.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[f(R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-y)]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[H(|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-y|)]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{a\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}H(|a|)\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}(|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-y-a|\leq 1)

We can use Theorem
4.7
(more precisely, Equation (
4.4
)) to obtain
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}(|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-y-a|\leq 1)\leq Cr^{-d/2}\cdot(1+r^{-1/2}|x_{i}-x_{j}-y-a|)^{-2K},
uniformly over all the desired parameters. We therefore obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(\Psi_{\sigma},M,F)\\
d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})<\epsilon\end{subarray}}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[f(R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-y)]

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(\Psi_{\sigma},M,F)\\
d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})<\epsilon\end{subarray}}\sum_{a\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}^{\infty}H(|a|)\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}(|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-y-a|\leq 1)

\displaystyle\leq Cr^{-d/2}\sum_{a\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}H(|a|)(1+r^{-1/2}|x_{i}-x_{j}-y-a|)^{-2K}.

On one hand,
H
decays exponentially which gives the upper bound of
\frac{A}{b}\cdot\big(1+r^{-1/2}|x_{i}-x_{j}-y|\big)^{-2K}
. On the other hand, the
-K
power in the sum is bounded above by
1
, thus the sum is also bounded by
\sum_{a\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}H(|a|)\leq C\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\ell^{d-1}H(\ell)\leq C\frac{A\cdot d!}{b^{d}}.
Now just use
\min\{\frac{A}{b},\frac{A}{b^{d}}\}=Ab^{-d}.

∎

4.4.
Existence of invariant measures

Definition 4.9
.

We say that a Borel measure
\pi
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
has
constant growth at infinity
if

\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\pi(\{x:|x-y|\leq M\})\leq CM^{d}

where
C
is independent of
M.

If
\pi
is a measure of constant growth of infinity on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then any measurable function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
|f(x)|\leq H(|x|)
is integrable as long as
H:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is non-increasing and satisfies
\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\ell^{d-1}H(\ell)<\infty
or equivalently
\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}H(\ell^{1/d})<\infty.
This is fairly straightforward to show. Examples of such functions include any
f
satisfying
|f(x)|\leq C|x|^{-d-\epsilon}
for
|x|>1
. This will be a very important and useful fact later.

Theorem 4.10

(Invariant measure)
.

Assume that one has an SRI chain
\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(\Psi_{\sigma},M,F)
with
k=2
. Furthermore assume that
\boldsymbol{b}
has the property that
\boldsymbol{b}(\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})=\boldsymbol{b}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}^{\prime})
whenever
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=\mathbf{x}+(a,a)
and
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}=\mathbf{y}+(a,a)
for some
a\in I
.

Define the associated difference kernel
\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,A):=\int_{I^{k}}\mathbf{1}_{\{y_{1}-y_{2}\in A\}}\boldsymbol{b}((x,0),\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}),
for Borel sets
A\subset I
. Furthermore assume that
\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}
is “Strong Feller” in the sense that
x\mapsto\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\bullet)
is continuous in total variation norm, and furthermore that for all
x\in I
and all open sets
U\subset I
there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}^{m}(x,U)>0
. Then the Markov kernel
\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}
has a unique (up to scalar multiple) invariant measure
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
on
I
that has constant growth at infinity.

This theorem will be proven in Appendix
C
.

Note that
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
is not a probability measure or even a finite measure—typically it will look like Lebesgue measure or counting measure far from the origin, but will have some impurities near the origin. In particular, the origin will often have extra mass to account for the extra time spent there by the process
P_{\mathbf{dif}}
. Since the invariant measure is only unique up to scalar multiple, we will need to fix a normalization of the measure
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
.

Definition 4.11
.

Define the function

\mathfrak{f}:=(P_{\mathbf{dif}}-\mathrm{Id})u\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\text{ where }\;\;\;\;u(x):=\begin{cases}1+|x|,&d=1\\
\log(1+|x|),&d=2\\
-(1+|x|)^{2-d},&d\geq 3.\end{cases}

We can think of
u(x)
as an approximate (up to rescaling by a constant plus some lower order terms) fundamental solution for the generator
(P_{\mathbf{dif}}-\mathrm{Id})
. It follows from (
D.7
) and the constant growth condition of
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
that
\int_{I}\mathfrak{f}\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}<\infty.
This allows us to define the following:

Definition 4.12
.

We define the
unit normalization
of
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
as the one in which
\int_{I}\mathfrak{f}\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}=1,
where
\mathfrak{f}
is as in Definition
4.11
. We further define the dimension dependent constants
c_{1}=1,c_{2}=2\pi,
and
c_{d}=d(d-2)/\Gamma(1+\frac{d}{2})
for
d\geq 3
.

For the definition to make sense, we must show that
\int_{I}\mathfrak{f}\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}\neq 0
, which will be clear from e.g. (
4.7
) and (
4.8
). Informally, the unit normalization is the one such that
2c_{d}\cdot\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
would agree with Lebesgue measure at infinity if
I=\mathbb{R}^{d}
, or counting measure at infinity if
I=\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. Henceforth, all integrals against
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
should be understood as being under the unit normalization.

Remark 4.13

(Comparison with noise coefficients in
[
BRAS06
]
and
[
DDP24b
]
)
.

We discuss how our noise coefficient in
d=1
matches with the coefficient obtained in
[
BRAS06
]
for finite range
1
-d random walks in random environments. This discussion gives another interpretation of the noise coefficient and unit normalization in terms of the potential kernel (or Green’s function if we extended this to the case
d\geq 3)
for
P_{\mathbf{dif}}
. In
[
BRAS06
]
they consider a model where
I=\mathbb{Z}
and the kernels
K_{n}(x,\cdot)
are i.i.d. in
x
and
n
. Furthermore, the kernels have finite range, meaning that
K_{n}(x,y)\neq 0
for only finitely many
y
, and they are aperiodic. For simplicity we will assume that
p=1
.

Following the notation in
[
BRAS06
]
, let
q(x,y)=P_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,y)
, i.e. the transition probabilities for the difference of the two walkers sampled from the two-point motion, and let
\overline{q}(x,y)
denote the transition probabilities for the difference of two independent random walks each sampled from the one-point motion.

In
d=1
, the noise coefficient in Theorem
1.13
is given by

\displaystyle\gamma_{\mathrm{ext}}^{2}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{z}\bigg[\sum_{x,y}(x-y)^{2}\mu(x)\mu(y)-\sum_{a}(a-z)^{2}q(z,a)\bigg]\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\bigg[\sum_{x,y}(x-y)^{2}\mu(x)\mu(y)-\sum_{a}a^{2}q(0,a)\bigg]\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(0).

Here
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
is the invariant measure of the Markov kernel
q
, under its unit normalization. The second equality is due to the fact that for
z\neq 0
,
q(z,a)=\overline{q}(z,a)=\sum_{k}\mu(k)\mu(k+z-a)
. The inner sum can then be shown to equal zero. This can be further simplified by noting that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left[\sum_{x,y}(x-y)^{2}\mu(x)\mu(y)-\sum_{a}a^{2}q(0,a)\right]=\text{Var}\left(\sum_{x}xK_{1}(0,x)\right).

In the language of
[
BRAS06
]
, this right-hand side is denoted by
\sigma_{D}^{2}
. Further define
D=\sum_{x}xK_{1}(0,x)
and let

\sigma_{a}^{2}:=\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}(x-D)^{2}\mu(x).
By Assumption
1.2
Item (
3
), we will assume here (WLOG) that
\sigma_{a}^{2}=1
.

Let
G_{n}(x,y)
and
\overline{G}_{n}(x,y)
be the
n
-step Green’s functions for
q
and
\overline{q}
respectively. Then define the potential kernels

(4.5)

a(x):=\lim_{n\to\infty}\{G_{n}(0,0)-G_{n}(x,0)\};\ \ \ \ \ \ \overline{a}(x):=\lim_{n\to\infty}\{\overline{G}_{n}(0,0)-\overline{G}_{n}(x,0)\}.

These potential kernels are fundamental solutions to their respective generators, i.e.

(q-\mathrm{Id})a(x)=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{1}_{\{x=0\}};\ \ \ \ \ \ (\overline{q}-\mathrm{Id})\overline{a}(x)=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{1}_{\{x=0\}}.

Next define

\beta:=\sum_{x}q(0,x)\overline{a}(x)=\frac{\overline{a}(x)}{a(x)}.

This equivalence is explained in
[
BRAS06
]
. In
[
BRAS06
]
, the noise coefficient is then given by
\frac{\sigma_{D}^{2}}{\beta}
so it remains to show that
\beta^{-1}=\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(0).
As shown in
[
LL10
, Theorem 4.4.8]
, we have that
\overline{a}(x)=\frac{|x|}{2\sigma_{a}^{2}}+C+O(e^{-\beta x})
for some constants
C
and
\beta
. It follows that

\int_{I}(q-\mathrm{Id})u\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}=2\int_{I}(q-\mathrm{Id})\overline{a}\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}

as the
O(e^{-\beta|x|})
term of the integral vanishes due to
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
being an invariant measure.

Finally, we have defined the unit normalization so that

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=\int_{I}(q-\mathrm{Id})u\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}=2\sigma_{a}^{2}\int_{I}(q-\mathrm{Id})\overline{a}\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}

\displaystyle=2\sigma_{a}^{2}\beta\int_{I}(q-\mathrm{Id})a\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}=\beta\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(0),

where the last line follows from (
4.5
). This concludes the proof.

In
[
DDP24b
]
, we considered a special case of the above model, which is the
d=1
case of the nearest-neighbor random walk in random environment described in Example
1.4
. For that model, we defined
\sigma^{2}:=\frac{1}{4}\sigma_{D}^{2}
. We then showed that
\gamma_{\mathrm{ext}}^{2}=\frac{8\sigma^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}=\frac{2\sigma_{D}^{2}}{1-4\sigma^{2}}
. This denominator of
1-4\sigma^{2}
is precisely equal to
\beta=\frac{1}{\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(0)}
and represents how likely the two walkers in the two-point motion are to stick together when they are at the same location. Note that the extra factor of
2
in the numerator is due to the periodicity of the nearest-neighbor random walk.

For further discussion of the noise coefficient for these discrete random walks in random environments, see
[
HDCC24
,
HDCC25
]
.

4.5.
Limit theorems for additive functionals

We now consider additive functionals of the difference chain
R_{r}^{1}-R^{2}_{r}
. The asymptotic behavior of such functionals is highly dependent on the dimension that we are working in as well as how close together the two walks are at the initial time.

We will first recall some classical results for simple symmetric random walks that will illustrate the dimension-dependent behavior that occurs for SRI chains as well.
Consider independent simple symmetric random walks
R^{1}_{r},R^{2}_{r}
. First suppose that
R^{1}_{0}=R^{2}_{0}
, i.e., the two walks start out at the same position. Then as described in the introduction, we have the following limit laws (in distribution) as we take
N\to\infty
:

(1)

In
d=1
,
N^{-1/2}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt}\mathbf{1}_{\{R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r}=0\}}\Rightarrow L^{B}_{0}(t)
, where
L^{B}_{0}(t)
is the local time at
0
of a Brownian motion
B
of rate
2
. This was first proven in
[
CH49
]
.

(2)

In
d=2
,
(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt}\mathbf{1}_{\{R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r}=0\}}\Rightarrow Z
, where
Z
is an exponential random variable with rate
\pi
. This result was first proven in
[
ET60
]
.

(3)

Finally, in
d\geq 3
,
\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{1}_{\{R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r}=0\}}
is a geometric random variable, with rate given by the return probability to 0.

More generally, for any random walk on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with increments of mean zero and identity covariance matrix, we can consider the asymptotic behavior of
\sum_{r=0}^{N}f(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})
for any nonnegative bounded function
f:\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}f(x)<\infty
, and we would get the following:

(1)

In
d=1
,
N^{-1/2}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt}f(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})\Rightarrow(\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}f(x))\cdot L^{B}_{0}(t)
.

(2)

In
d=2
,
(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt}f(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})\Rightarrow(\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}f(x))\cdot Z,
with
Z
exponentially distributed and not depending on
f
or
t
.

(3)

In
d\geq 3
,
\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}f(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})
is a random variable depending both on
f
and the model.

These types of results are part of the general theory for asymptotic behaviors of additive functionals of Markov chains called the Darling-Kac theorem
[
DK57
]
, which shows that under suitable renormalization, the limiting distribution for a large class of additive functionals of a Markov process is given by a Mitag-Leffler distribution. Both the folded normal distribution (the distribution of Brownian local time at zero for fixed
t
) and the exponential distribution are special cases of the Mitag-Leffler distribution. See also
[
KR53
]
for analogous result for 2d Brownian motion.

Next, we consider the case where the random walkers start out far apart. In particular, we consider the case when
|R^{1}_{0}-R^{2}_{0}|=O(\sqrt{N})
. In
d=1
, we still have

N^{-1/2}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt}\mathbf{1}_{\{R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r}=0\}}\Rightarrow L^{B}_{0}(t),

where the only difference is that Brownian motion
B
may not start at 0.

In
d\geq 2
, things change dramatically, as these additive functionals are now dominated by the (very small) probability that the two walkers meet up at least once before time
Nt
. We must normalize appropriately if we hope to see a nontrivial limit. The expected number of collisions between the random walkers is given by the
d
-dimensional Green’s function whose behavior is of order
|R^{1}_{0}-R^{2}_{0}|^{2-d}=N^{\frac{2-d}{2}}
. Therefore,
N^{\frac{2-d}{2}}
should be the correct normalization if we expect to see something nonzero in the limit.

However, by Donsker’s theorem, we still have that

N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt}\mathbf{1}_{\{R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r}=0\}}
converges a.s. to
0
,
because a
d
-dimensional Brownian motion with
d\geq 2
starting macroscopically away from
0
will remain bounded away from
0

on the interval
[0,t]
. While the almost sure limit is zero, we still obtain a nontrivial limit in expectation.
If the starting location is
(x^{1}_{N},x^{2}_{N})
with
N^{-1/2}(x^{1}_{N}-x^{2}_{N})\to x\neq 0
, then we have

\lim_{N\to\infty}N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\mathbb{E}_{(x^{1}_{N},x^{2}_{N})}\bigg[\sum_{r=0}^{Nt}\mathbf{1}_{\{R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r}=0\}}\bigg]=c_{d}\int_{0}^{t}G(2s,x)ds.

Here
c_{d}
are the dimension dependent constants from Defintion
4.11
, and
G
is the standard heat kernel on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

For SRI chains, we will essentially have the same set of results, with
\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}f(x)
replaced everywhere with
\int f\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
, which captures the local structure of the SRI chain near the origin. This is the subject of the next several theorems. We begin by stating an assumption that will be used throughout the remainder of this subsection.

Assumption 4.14
.

Fix
\sigma>0
,
M>0
,
d\geq 1
and a decreasing function
F:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
of exponential decay at infinity. Consider a sequence
\boldsymbol{b}_{N}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(\Psi_{\sigma},M,F)
with
k=2
, satisfying
\boldsymbol{b}_{N}(\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})=\boldsymbol{b}_{N}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}^{\prime})
whenever
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=\mathbf{x}+(a,a)
and
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}=\mathbf{y}+(a,a)
. Assume that
\boldsymbol{b}_{N}
converges as
N\to\infty
to some limiting SRI chain
\boldsymbol{b}
, which is
\delta
-repulsive for some
\delta>0
. We do not assume that
\boldsymbol{b}
is centered, but we impose that
\boldsymbol{b}
satisfies
d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b}_{N},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})\leq\epsilon
for some centered chain
\boldsymbol{b}_{0}
where
\epsilon
is as in Theorem
4.7
. This is required in order for the anti-concentration estimates of Theorem
4.7
to hold, and these estimates will be crucial in the forthecoming proofs. We also assume that the limiting chain
\boldsymbol{b}
satisfies the strong Feller conditions in Theorem
4.10
.

Consider any sequence of functions
f_{N}:I\to\mathbb{R}
such that
|f_{N}(x)|\leq H(|x|)
for some decreasing
H:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
of exponential decay at infinity. Assume
f_{N}\to f
uniformly as
N\to\infty
. Let
s_{N}:=\int_{I}u\;\nu_{N}(\mathrm{d}u)
, where
\nu_{N}
is the base measure of
\boldsymbol{b}_{N}
. Also assume (without loss of generality) that the covariance matrix of the base measure
\nu
of
\boldsymbol{b}
is
\mathrm{Id}_{d\times d}
. Let
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
be the invariant measure of
\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}
under its unit normalization.

Remark 4.15
.

The exponential decay assumption on
F
is stronger than is necessary. It could be relaxed to a power decay of sufficient degree depending on
d
, but this would clutter the notation and require additional proof details, without adding much value to the main results, and we therefore do not pursue it.

Theorem 4.16
.

Let
d\geq 1
and consider the setting of Assumption
4.14
.
For any
t\geq 0
, and any
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have that

(4.6)

\lim_{N\to\infty}N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\big[\phi(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}r)\big)f_{N}(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})]\\
=\bigg(\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}G(2s,x_{1}-x_{2})G(2s,y)\phi(\tfrac{1}{2}(y+x_{1}+x_{2}))\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}s\bigg)c_{d}\int_{I}f\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}

whenever
N^{-1/2}\mathbf{x}_{N}\to\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2d},
where
x_{1}\neq x_{2}
. Here
G
appearing on the right side is the standard heat kernel on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, that is
G(t,y)=(2\pi t)^{-d/2}e^{-|y|^{2}/(2t)}.
Furthermore,
c_{d}
and
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
are as in Definition
4.12
.

When
N^{-1/2}\mathbf{x}_{N}\to\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2d},
where
x_{1}\neq x_{2}
, we say that
x^{1}_{N}
and
x_{N}^{2}
are
well-separated
. We now address the case where
x_{N}^{1}
and
x_{N}^{2}
are close to each other, rather than well-separated as in Theorem
4.16
.

Theorem 4.17
.

Assume Assumption
4.14
.

(1)

If
d=1
then Theorem
4.16
remains valid even when
x_{1}=x_{2},
under the same assumptions.

(2)

If
d=2
then in the same setting as Theorem
4.16
, if
\mathbf{x}_{N}
is any sequence in
I^{k}
such that
x^{1}_{N}-x^{2}_{N}
remains bounded as
N\to\infty
, then we have that

(4.7)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{\log N}\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[f_{N}(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})\bigg]=\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}f\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}.

The above two theorems will be proven in Appendix
D
, but we sketch the important ideas of the proofs here. The main idea is that by using the Krylov-Bogoliubov trick, all subsequential limits of these additive functionals can be shown to be equal to some constant multiple of the unique invariant measure for the limiting Markov chain
\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}
, yielding the term
\int_{I}f\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
in each of the results. We then identify the constant uniquely, so that the entire sequence converges to some unique multiple of the invariant measure. To nail down this constant, we compute the value of the limit for a specific choice of
f
. In particular, we take
f=\mathfrak{f}
as in Definition
4.11
. This yields the coefficient
\big(\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}G(2s,x_{1}-x_{2})G(2s,y)\phi(\tfrac{1}{2}(y+x_{1}+x_{2}))\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}s\big)c_{d}
in Theorem
4.16
and the simpler coefficient
\frac{1}{2}
in Theorem
4.17
.

We explain here how one can obtain the coefficient
\frac{1}{2}
as the proof is illustrative. For an explanation of how the coefficient
\big(\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}G(2s,x_{1}-x_{2})G(2s,y)\phi(\tfrac{1}{2}(y+x_{1}+x_{2}))\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}s\big)c_{d}
arises in
d=1
, see Remark
4.18
.

The invariance principle of Theorem
4.6
implies that for
\mathfrak{f}=(P_{\mathbf{dif}}-\mathrm{Id})u
where
u(x)=\log(1+|x|)
,

\displaystyle(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{N-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[\mathfrak{f}(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})]

\displaystyle=(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{N-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[\log(1+|R^{i}_{r+1}-R^{j}_{r+1}|)-\log(1+|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}|)]

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[\log(1+|R^{1}_{N}-R^{2}_{N}|)]-\log(1+|x^{i}_{N}-x^{j}_{N}|)}{\log N}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[\log(N^{-1/2}+N^{-1/2}|R^{1}_{N}-R^{2}_{N}|)]-\log(1+|x^{i}_{N}-x^{j}_{N}|)}{\log N}

(4.8)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle N\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}\frac{1}{2}.

In the last line, we used the invariance principle and the assumption that
|x^{i}_{N}-x^{j}_{N}|
remains bounded to conclude that the numerator remains bounded. Finally, note that
\frac{1}{2}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}\mathfrak{f}\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
when
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
is under the unit normalization.

Remark 4.18
.

For
d=1
, there is an alternative approach to proving Theorem
4.16
(and the
d=1
case of Theorem
4.17
) in which one can use the theory of local times to take the limit of the expectations, using the invariance principle. This uses the approach of
[
DDP24a
,
DDP24b
,
Par24
]
. For instance, assuming all particles start from 0, we have that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt}\mathbf{E}_{(0,0)}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\big[\phi(N^{-1/2}

\displaystyle(R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}r)\big)f_{N}(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})]=\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(f)\mathbf{E}_{BM^{\otimes 2}}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\phi(X_{s})L_{0}^{X-Y}(\mathrm{d}s)\right]

\displaystyle=\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(f)\mathbf{E}_{BM^{\otimes 2}}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\phi\left(\frac{X_{s}+Y_{s}}{2}\right)L_{0}^{X-Y}(\mathrm{d}s)\right]

\displaystyle=\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(f)\int_{0}^{t}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{R}}G\big(\tfrac{s}{2},x\big)\phi(x)\mathrm{d}x\bigg]G(2s,0)\mathrm{d}s.

The second equality is due to the fact that on the support of the local time measure
L_{0}^{X-Y}(\mathrm{d}s)
, we have
X_{s}=Y_{s}
. The reason we want to change
X\to\frac{X+Y}{2}
is so that it becomes independent of
X-Y
. Doing a further change of variables
x\to\frac{x}{2}
matches this expression with the right-hand side of (
4.6
) and completes the proof.

However, such an approach does not work in higher dimensions. Indeed if
d\geq 2
, then the family of processes
\big(N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{s=N\varepsilon}^{Nt}f(R^{i}_{s}-R^{j}_{s})\big)_{t\geq\varepsilon}
viewed under the measures
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}
do not satisfy any nice
L^{q}
moment bounds for
any

q>1
. Indeed if
|x_{N}^{i}-x_{N}^{j}|
is of order
N^{1/2}
, the process itself converges in
C[0,T]
to the zero process (by the invariance principle of Theorem
4.6
), however the expectation converges to a finite and positive value, while the second moments and beyond blow up to infinity. This is in sharp contrast to
d=1
where the process converges to the local time and satisfies moment bounds of every order.

Remark 4.19
.

In the above convergence theorems, the main difficulty comes from the fact that
d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{N})
can tend to zero extremely slowly (as slowly as one desires). Indeed if
d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{N})
tends to zero at a fast rate of
O(N^{-1})
, then many of the proofs of the appendix can be substantially simplified by a direct coupling with the centered chain. However such a case is irrelevant to us, since all of the interesting cases of models we actually study will have much slower convergence rates. For example in
d=2
, by Theorem
1.15
the most interesting case is when it goes to 0 like
1/\sqrt{\log N}
.

The following theorem is an extension of the Erdös-Taylor theorem for 2d SRI chains and will be most important for the
\varsigma_{N}
at the critical scale in
d=2
(that is, the third bullet point of Theorem
1.15
).

Theorem 4.20

(Erdös-Taylor theorem for SRI chains in
d=2
)
.

Let
d=2
and assume Assumption
4.14
. If
\mathbf{x}_{N}
is any sequence in
I^{2}
such that
x^{1}_{N}-x^{2}_{N}
remains bounded as
N\to\infty
, then
(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{N}f_{N}(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})
converges in law (viewed under
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}
) as
N\to\infty
to an exponential random variable of rate
2/\big(\int f\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}\big)
.

Furthermore, for any
t>\varepsilon>0
the random variable
\sum_{r=N\varepsilon}^{Nt}f_{N}(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})
goes to 0 in probability, thus
(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt}f_{N}(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r}),
viewed as a process in the variable
t
, converges in f.d.d.’s to a constant process equal to the same exponential random variable.

Remark 4.21
.

This theorem can be thought of as an Erdös-Taylor theorem for sequences of SRI chains. The original Erdös-Taylor theorem
[
ET60
]
proves that the occupation times of a 2d simple symmetric random walk are asymptotically exponential when rescaled by
\log N
. A series of recent papers
[
LZ23
,
LZ24
]
extends the Erdös-Taylor theorem to the pairwise collisions of a family of i.i.d. simple symmetric random walks on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

We include the proof of Theorem
4.20
in this section because it is extremely instructive for the proofs in later sections.

Proof.

Note that the last sentence is immediate from the invariance principle of Theorem
4.6
, and the fact that the law of Brownian motion in
d=2
stays positive distance from the origin on any time interval
[\varepsilon,t]
.

It suffices to prove the statement for the case where
f_{N}=f
for all
N
. We can then bound the error terms involving
f_{N}-f
using Corollary
4.8
, noting e.g. that
|f_{N}(x)-f(x)|\leq H_{N}(|x|)
for some functions of the form
H_{N}\downarrow 0
with
H_{N}
having some uniform exponential decay bound. In that case
b
can be taken to be fixed and
A\to 0
, thus
Ab^{-d}\to 0
.

We now prove the first statement. We will prove convergence of the integer moments of
(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{N}f(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})
to the correct sequence of values, and one may easily show that this is enough. Thus we need to show that for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
:

(4.9)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[\bigg(\frac{1}{\log N}\sum_{r=0}^{N}f(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})\bigg)^{m}\bigg]=m!2^{-m}\bigg(\int_{I}f\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}\bigg)^{m}.

Without loss of generality, we will assume that
f
is compactly supported. This suffices by an approximation argument because for general nonnegative
f
of exponential decay, one may take a sequence
f^{(\ell)}\uparrow f
with each
f^{(\ell)}
of compact support. Note that again by taking the factor
A
appearing in the right side of Corollary
4.8
to zero,

\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\sup_{N\geq 1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{N}|f^{(\ell)}-f|(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})\bigg]=0.

Clearly any limit in
L^{1}
of exponential random variables is still exponential, with rate given by the limit of the rate, thus justifying the reduction to
f
of compact support.

Let us now proceed by induction on
m\in\mathbb{N}
. The base case for
m=1
just follows from Theorem
4.17
.
The inductive step simplifies due to the compact support assumption on
f
. The method that we use to complete the inductive step will henceforth be called the
renewal trick.
Suppose that the support of
f
is contained in a ball of radius
K
about the origin. Suppose the claim holds up to
m-1
. Then there exists some decreasing deterministic sequence
c_{m-1,f}(N)
with
\lim_{N\to\infty}c_{m-1,f}(N)\to 0
such that

\sup_{\mathbf{x}:|x_{i}-x_{j}|\leq K}\bigg|\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[\bigg(\frac{1}{\log N}\sum_{s=0}^{N}f(R^{1}_{s}-R^{2}_{s})\bigg)^{m-1}\bigg]-(m-1)!\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}f\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}\bigg)^{m-1}\bigg|\leq c_{m-1,f}(N).

We can expand

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[\bigg(\frac{1}{\log N}\sum_{r=0}^{N}f(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})\bigg)^{m}\bigg]

\displaystyle=m!(\log N)^{-m}\sum_{0\leq s_{1}<...<s_{m}\leq N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}f(R^{1}_{s_{\ell}}-R^{2}_{s_{\ell}})\bigg]

\displaystyle\;\;+(\log N)^{-m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq s_{1},...,s_{m}\leq N\\
\text{repeated index}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}f(R^{1}_{s_{\ell}}-R^{2}_{s_{\ell}})\bigg].

One may check that the terms with repeated indices are negligible as they yield a term of order
(\log N)^{-1}
by the inductive hypothesis. As for the first term with no repeated indices, we can write it using the Markov property as

\displaystyle m!(\log

\displaystyle N)^{-m}\sum_{0\leq s_{1}<...<s_{m}\leq N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}f(R^{1}_{s_{\ell}}-R^{2}_{s_{\ell}})\bigg]=

\displaystyle=\frac{m!}{\log N}\sum_{s_{1}=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[f(R^{1}_{s_{1}}-R^{2}_{s_{1}})\cdot\frac{g_{N}(s_{1})^{m-1}}{(m-1)!}\mathbf{E}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}_{\mathbf{R}_{s_{1}}}\bigg[\bigg(\frac{1}{\log(N-s_{1})}\sum_{s=0}^{N-s_{1}}f(R^{1}_{s}-R^{2}_{s})\bigg)^{m-1}\bigg]\bigg]+O(1/\log N)

where
g_{N}(s):=\frac{\log(N-s)}{\log N}
and the
O(1/\log N)
term again consists of collection of terms that have repeated indices and are negligible in the limit. By the inductive hypothesis and compact support of
f
, we know that

\displaystyle f(R^{1}_{s_{1}}-R^{2}_{s_{1}})\bigg|\bigg(\frac{1}{\log(N-s_{1})}\sum_{s=0}^{N-s_{1}}\mathbf{E}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}_{\mathbf{R}_{s_{1}}}[f(R^{1}_{s}-R^{2}_{s})]

\displaystyle\bigg)^{m-1}-(m-1)!\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}f\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}\bigg)^{m-1}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq c_{m-1,f}(N-s_{1})\|f\|_{\infty}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\{|R^{1}_{s_{1}}-R^{2}_{s_{1}}|\leq K\}},

where the right side is a deterministic quantity.

Note that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{\log N}\sum_{s=0}^{N}c_{m-1,f}(N-s)\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[\mathbf{1}_{\{|R^{1}_{s}-R^{2}_{s}|\leq K\}}]=0.

This follows from splitting the sum in half and observing that by Corollary
4.8
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\log N}\sum_{s=0}^{N/2}c_{m-1,f}(N-s)\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[\mathbf{1}_{\{|R^{1}_{s}-R^{2}_{s}|\leq K\}}]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\log N}\sum_{s=0}^{N/2}\frac{c_{m-1,f}(N/2)}{1\wedge s}

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot c_{m-1,f}(N/2)

and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\log N}\sum_{s=N/2}^{N}c_{m-1,f}(N-s)\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[\mathbf{1}_{\{|R^{1}_{s}-R^{2}_{s}|\leq K\}}]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\log N}\sum_{s=N/2}^{N}\frac{1}{s}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C\log 2}{\log N}.

Both of these expressions tend to
0
as
N\to\infty
.

Putting the above together, it remains to show that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{\log N}\sum_{s_{1}=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[f(R^{1}_{s_{1}}-R^{2}_{s_{1}})\cdot g_{N}(s_{1})^{m-1}\bigg]=\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}f\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}.

We note that
g_{N}(s)\in[0,1]
so that

|g_{N}(s)^{m-1}-1|\leq m|g_{N}(s)-1|\leq Cm\cdot\frac{s}{N\log N}.

We therefore have

\frac{1}{\log N}\sum_{s_{1}=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[f(R^{1}_{s_{1}}-R^{2}_{s_{1}})\cdot g_{N}(s_{1})^{m-1}\bigg]\\
\leq\frac{1}{\log N}\sum_{s_{1}=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[f(R^{1}_{s_{1}}-R^{2}_{s_{1}})\bigg]+\frac{Cm}{N(\log N)^{2}}\sum_{s_{1}=0}^{N}s\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[f(R^{1}_{s_{1}}-R^{2}_{s_{1}})\bigg].

The first term on the right-hand side converges to
\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}f\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
by the
m=1
case of this theorem, and the second term converges to
0
by Corollary
4.8
.
∎

Remark 4.22
.

Note that the reason this proof technique fails in
d=1
is due to the crucial fact noted in Remark
1.22
that while in
d=1
, the limit of the discrete local time up to time
Nt
depends on
t
, the exponential limit in
d=2
does not depend on
t
. This allowed us to apply the inductive step to
\sum_{s=0}^{N-s_{1}}\mathbf{E}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}_{\mathbf{R}_{s_{1}}}[f(R^{1}_{s}-R^{2}_{s})]
as
s_{1}
is of order
N
in the sum.

We now prove a version of the last theorem with a test function appearing in the sum, which will be important later.

Theorem 4.23

(Backwards-time propagation of test functions in
d=2
)
.

Let
d=2
. Assume
\mathbf{x}_{N}=(x^{1}_{N},x^{2}_{N})
is any sequence such that
N^{-1/2}x_{N}^{1}\to x_{1}
and
x^{1}_{N}-x^{2}_{N}
remains bounded, and consider the same setting as Assumption
4.14
.
Consider smooth bounded functions
\phi_{1},...,\phi_{m}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
with globally bounded and continuous first and second partial derivatives, and consider
f^{1},...,f^{m}:I\to\mathbb{R}
of exponential decay at infinity. Then

(4.10)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[\frac{1}{(\log N)^{m}}\sum_{0\leq r_{1}<\ldots<r_{m}\leq Nt}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}f^{\ell}(R^{1}_{r_{\ell}}-R^{2}_{r_{\ell}})\phi_{\ell}(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r_{\ell}}-s_{N}r_{\ell}))\bigg]=\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}\bigg(\phi_{\ell}(x_{1})\int_{I}f^{\ell}\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}\bigg)

The claim remains true if we replace
(f^{1},...,f^{m})
on the left side by some sequence
(f^{1}_{N},...,f^{m}_{N})
converging uniformly on compact sets as
N\to\infty
to some
(f^{1},...,f^{m})
, as long as
\sup_{N}|f^{j}_{N}|
decays exponentially fast at infinity for
j=1,...,m
.

The reason we call this backwards propagation in time is because we begin with a test function
\phi
whose input value is the Markov chain evaluated at some large time
r
, and then this input value gets propagated backwards in time to obtain the test function evaluated at time zero in the limit, i.e., the expression
\phi(x_{1})
appearing on the right-hand side of (
4.10
).

In
d=1
, this type of backward-time propagation fails completely, and the joint limit will be a nontrivial one given by a local time of Brownian motion and some Lebesgue-Stieltjes integral against the local time measure. The point is that in higher dimensions, all of the information is already concentrated at the initial time, as we already observed in Theorem
4.20
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
m
. The induction step follows exactly as in the proof of Theorem
4.20
, using the Markov property and applying the same exact renewal trick. It only remains to prove the
m=1
case which is the base case for the induction argument. For the
m=1
case, we will show that

(4.11)

(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[f(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})\cdot\bigg(\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}r)\big)-\phi(N^{-1/2}x_{1}^{N})\bigg)\bigg]\to 0.

We remark that this is not true if one were to try to bring an absolute value inside the sum, so we need to be more precise.

To prove the above limit, fix the test function
\phi
and consider the sequence of (signed) measures
\gamma_{N}(f):=(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\big[f(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})\cdot\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}r)\big)\big]
as defined above. On one hand, it is simple to show that any subsequential limit of
\gamma_{N}
must be an invariant measure (see e.g.,
Step 4
in Appendix D), thus by Theorem
4.10
a multiple of
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
. If we can uniquely identify the constant as being equal to
\frac{1}{2}\cdot\phi(x_{1})
, this would uniquely pin down every subsequential limit of
\gamma_{N}
. To do that, we just need to pick some specific
f
and then explicitly calculate the limit of the expectation.

In the proof of Theorem
4.20
we picked the specific
f
given by
\mathfrak{f}=(P_{\mathbf{dif}}-\mathrm{Id})u(\mathbf{x})
where
u(\mathbf{x}):=\log(1+|x_{1}-x_{2}|)
, and here we will pick the same
f
. The calculation for this will be based on “discrete Ito calculus” and is very similar to ideas used later in Appendix C. Henceforth abbreviate
V:=R^{1}-R^{2}
. We have by the Markov Property that

\displaystyle\gamma_{N}

\displaystyle\big((P_{\mathbf{dif}}-\mathrm{Id})u\big)=(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[\bigg(\log(1+|V_{r+1}|)-\log(1+|V_{r}|)\bigg)\cdot\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}r)\big)\bigg]

\displaystyle=(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[\bigg(\log(N^{-1/2}+N^{-1/2}|V_{r+1}|)-\log(N^{-1/2}+N^{-1/2}|V_{r}|)\bigg)\cdot\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}r)\big)\bigg].

Summing this expression by parts, we get that

\displaystyle\gamma_{N}\big((P_{\mathbf{dif}}-\mathrm{Id})u\big)=(\log N)^{-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[\log(N^{-1/2}+N^{-1/2}|V_{N+1}|)\phi(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{N+1}-s_{N}(N+1)))\bigg]

\displaystyle-(\log N)^{-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[\log(N^{-1/2}+N^{-1/2}|x^{1}_{N}-x^{2}_{N}|)\phi(N^{-1/2}x_{1}^{N})\bigg]

\displaystyle\;\;-(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[\log(N^{-1/2}+N^{-1/2}|V_{r}|)\cdot\bigg(\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r+1}-s_{N}(r+1)\big)-\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}r)\big)\bigg)\bigg]

\displaystyle+(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[\bigg(\log(1+|V_{r+1}|)-\log(1+|V_{r}|)\bigg)\bigg(\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r+1}-s_{N}(r+1)\big)-\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}r)\big)\bigg)\bigg].

We now take the limits of each of these four terms.

Term 1:
By the invariance principle of Theorem
4.6
, the expression
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[\log(N^{-1/2}+N^{-1/2}|V_{N+1}|)]
approaches a finite value as
N\to\infty
and hence

(\log N)^{-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[\log(N^{-1/2}+N^{-1/2}|V_{N+1}|)\phi(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{N+1}-s_{N}(N+1)))\bigg]\to 0.

Term 2:
Using the assumption that
x^{1}_{N}-x^{2}_{N}
remains bounded and the fact that
N^{-1/2}X^{1}_{N}\to x_{1}
, we have

-(\log N)^{-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[\log(N^{-1/2}+N^{-1/2}|x^{1}_{N}-x^{2}_{N}|)\phi(N^{-1/2}x_{1}^{N})\bigg]\to\frac{1}{2}\phi(x_{1}).

Term 3:

Taylor expand
\phi
to get

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r+1}-s_{N}(r+1)\big)-\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}r)\big)

\displaystyle=N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r+1}-R^{1}_{r}-s_{N})\bullet\nabla\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}r)\big)+O(1/N).

The
O(1/N)
term can be disregarded since there are only
N
summands and the sum above includes a further multiplying factor of
1/\log N
. On the other hand the first term is also inconsequential since the SRI property yields
|\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[R^{1}_{r+1}-R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}|\mathcal{F}_{r}]|\leq\tilde{f}(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})
for some rapidly decreasing function
\tilde{f}:I\to\mathbb{R}
, thus by Theorem
4.7
and the extra multiplying factor of
N^{-1/2}
one obtains the vanishing in the associated term above.

Term 4:
Note that

\displaystyle|\log(1+|V_{r+1}|)-\log(1+|V_{r}|)|

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\frac{1}{1+|V_{r}|}+\frac{1}{1+|V_{r+1}|}\bigg)|V_{r+1}-V_{r}|

\displaystyle\leq N^{-1/2}\bigg(\frac{1}{N^{-1/2}(1+|V_{r}|)}+\frac{1}{N^{-1/2}(1+|V_{r+1}|)}\bigg)\sum_{j=1,2}|R^{j}_{r+1}-R^{j}_{r}-s_{N}|.

Likewise

|\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r+1}-s_{N}(r+1)\big)-\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}r)\big)|\leq CN^{-1/2}\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}|R^{1}_{r+1}-R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}|.

Thus Term 4 is bounded above by something of the form

C(\log N)^{-1}N^{-1}\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[\bigg(\frac{1}{N^{-1/2}(1+|V_{r}|)}+\frac{1}{N^{-1/2}(1+|V_{r+1}|)}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{j=1,2}|R^{j}_{r+1}-R^{j}_{r}-s_{N}|^{2}\bigg].

Consider conjugate exponents
q^{\prime},q
with
q^{\prime}<2
, and use the Young’s inequality
ab\leq\frac{1}{p}a^{q^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{q}b^{q}
, then note that the last expression is bounded above by

C(\log N)^{-1}N^{-1}\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[\bigg(\frac{1}{N^{-1/2}(1+|V_{r}|)}+\frac{1}{N^{-1/2}(1+|V_{r+1}|)}\bigg)^{q^{\prime}}+\sum_{j=1,2}|R^{j}_{r+1}-R^{j}_{r}-s_{N}|^{2q}\bigg].

The power
q^{\prime}
term can be shown to vanish like
O(1/\log N)
using the invariance principle of Theorem
4.6
(and the anticoncentration estimate of Theorem
4.7
to establish the uniform integrability, noting
q^{\prime}<2
), while the power
2q
term vanishes thanks to uniform boundedness of the
(2q)^{th}
moments in the definition of SRI chains which ensures that
\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\big[\sum_{j=1,2}|R^{j}_{r+1}-R^{j}_{r}-s_{N}|^{2q}\big]\leq CN
for some uniform constant
C
. Dividing this sum by
N\log N
takes this term to
0
.

Combining the above four term-wise limits, we finally conclude that
\gamma_{N}\big((P_{\mathbf{dif}}-\mathrm{Id})u\big)\to\frac{1}{2}\phi(x_{1}).
As this is also the limit of
(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\big[\mathfrak{f}(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})\cdot\phi(N^{-1/2}x_{1}^{N})\big]
by Theorem
4.17
, we conclude the proof of (
4.11
).
∎

Example 4.24

(Triviality of the exponential field)
.

Let
d=2
and assume Assumption
4.14
.
Assume
N^{-1/2}x_{N}^{1}\to x_{1}
and
x^{1}_{N}-x^{2}_{N}
remains bounded. Then for any
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, the pair

(\log N)^{-1}\;\;\cdot\;\;\bigg(\sum_{r=0}^{N}f(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})\;\;\;\;,\;\;\;\;\sum_{r=0}^{N}f(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})\cdot\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}r)\big)\bigg)

when sampled from the chain
\boldsymbol{b}_{N}
converges in law to
(Z,\phi(x_{1})Z)
where
Z
is an exponential random variable of rate
2/\big(\int f\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}\big).
This follows immediately from Theorem
4.23
which immediately yields

(4.12)

(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{N}f(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})\cdot\bigg(\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}r)\big)-\phi(N^{-1/2}x_{1}^{N})\bigg)\to 0

in
L^{2}
under the measures
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}
, in the sense that the sequence of second moments goes to 0.

Theorem 4.25

(Backwards-time propagation of test functions in
d\geq 3
)
.

Let
d\geq 3
. Assume
\mathbf{x}_{N}=(x^{1}_{N},x^{2}_{N})
is any sequence such that
N^{-1/2}x_{N}^{1}\to x_{1}
and
x^{1}_{N}-x^{2}_{N}
converges to
y\in I
(without any normalization), and consider the same setting as Assumption
4.14
.
Consider smooth bounded functions
\phi_{1},...,\phi_{m}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
with globally bounded and continuous first and second partial derivatives, and consider
f^{1},...,f^{m}:I\to\mathbb{R}
continuous of exponential decay at infinity. Then

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}

\displaystyle\bigg[\sum_{0\leq r_{1}<\ldots<r_{m}\leq Nt}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}f^{\ell}(R^{1}_{r_{\ell}}-R^{2}_{r_{\ell}})\phi_{\ell}(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r_{\ell}}-s_{N}r_{\ell}))\bigg]

(4.13)

\displaystyle=\phi_{1}(x_{1})\cdots\phi_{m}(x_{1})\sum_{0\leq r_{1}<...<r_{m}<\infty}\;\mathbf{E}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathrm{dif}}}_{y}\big[f^{1}(X_{r_{1}})\cdots f^{m}(X_{r_{m}})].

The claim remains true if we replace
(f^{1},...,f^{m})
on the left side by some sequence
(f^{1}_{N},...,f^{m}_{N})
equicontinuous and converging uniformly on compact sets as
N\to\infty
to some
(f^{1},...,f^{m})
, as long as
\sup_{N}|f^{j}_{N}|
decays exponentially fast at infinity for
j=1,...,m
.

Unlike the
d=2
case above, we remark that there is no logarithmic factor here, but more importantly the terms do not decouple as a product of individual terms in the limit, instead nesting inside one another due to the transience and the Markov property. Thus summarizing, we see that for
d=1
, the backwards propagation simply fails, while for
d=2
and
d\geq 3
it remains true but takes on somewhat different forms.

Proof.

Again, we proceed by induction on
m
. The inductive step follows from the same renewal trick we used in the proof of Theorem
4.20
, so we just need to prove the base case
m=1
.

For the
m=1
case, we must show that

(4.14)

\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[f(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})\cdot\bigg(\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}r)\big)-\phi(N^{-1/2}x_{1}^{N})\bigg)\bigg]\to 0.

Fix the test function
\phi
and consider the sequence of signed measures
\gamma_{N}(f):=\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\big[f(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})\cdot\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}r)\big)\big]
. By the usual arguments (see
Step 4
in Appendix D), any subsequential limit of
\gamma_{N}
must be a multiple of
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
. We will show that this multiple equals
u(y)\phi(x_{1})
, which uniquely pins down every subsequential limit of
\gamma_{N}
. As usual, we will show this by considering the specific choice
\mathfrak{f}=(P_{\mathbf{dif}}-\mathrm{Id})u
with
u(x):=(1+|x|)^{2-d}
. Recall that
V:=R^{1}-R^{2}
. We have by the Markov Property that

\displaystyle\gamma_{N}

\displaystyle\big((P_{\mathbf{dif}}-\mathrm{Id})u\big)=\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[\bigg(u(V_{r+1})-u(V_{r})\bigg)\cdot\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}r)\big)\bigg].

Summing this expression by parts, we get that

\displaystyle\gamma_{N}\big((P_{\mathbf{dif}}-\mathrm{Id})u\big)=\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[u(V_{N+1})\phi(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{N+1}-s_{N}(N+1)))\bigg]

\displaystyle-\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[u(x^{1}_{N}-x^{2}_{N})\phi(N^{-1/2}x_{N}^{1})\bigg]

\displaystyle\;\;-\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[u(V_{r})\cdot\bigg(\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r+1}-s_{N}(r+1)\big)-\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}r)\big)\bigg)\bigg]

\displaystyle+\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[\bigg(u(V_{r+1})-u(V_{r})\bigg)\bigg(\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r+1}-s_{N}(r+1)\big)-\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}r)\big)\bigg)\bigg].

We now take the limits of each of these four terms.

Term 1:
By the invariance principle of Theorem
4.6
, the expression
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[u(V_{N+1})]=\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[(1+|V_{N+1}|)^{2-d}]
approaches
0
as
N\to\infty
.

Term 2:
Using the assumption that
x^{1}_{N}-x^{2}_{N}\to y
and the fact that
N^{-1/2}x^{1}_{N}\to x_{1}
, we have

\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[u(x^{1}_{N}-x^{2}_{N})\phi(N^{-1/2}x_{N}^{1})\bigg]\to u(y)\phi(x_{1}).

Term 3:

Taylor expand
\phi
to get

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r+1}-s_{N}(r+1)\big)-\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}r)\big)

\displaystyle=N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r+1}-R^{1}_{r}-s_{N})\bullet\nabla\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}r)\big)+O(1/N).

The
O(1/N)
term goes to
0
since by Theorem
4.6
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{r=1}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\big[u(V_{r})\big]\leq C\frac{1}{N}\sum_{r=1}^{N}r^{1-\frac{d}{2}}\to 0.

The first term is also inconsequential since the SRI property yields
|\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[R^{1}_{r+1}-R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}|\mathcal{F}_{r}]|\leq\tilde{f}(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})
for some rapidly decreasing function
\tilde{f}:I\to\mathbb{R}
, thus by Theorem
4.7
and the extra multiplying factor of
N^{-1/2}
one obtains the vanishing in the associated term above.

Term 4:

We have

|\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r+1}-s_{N}(r+1)\big)-\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}r)\big)|\leq CN^{-1/2}\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}|R^{1}_{r+1}-R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}|.

Thus Term 4 is bounded above by

CN^{-1/2}\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[\bigg(\frac{1}{(1+|V_{r}|)^{d-2}}-\frac{1}{(1+|V_{r+1}|)^{d-2}}\bigg)\cdot|R^{1}_{r+1}-R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}|\bigg].

Consider conjugate exponents
q^{\prime},q
with
q^{\prime}<2
, and use Hölder’s inequality to obtain

CN^{-1/2}\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[\bigg|\frac{1}{(1+|V_{r}|)^{d-2}}-\frac{1}{(1+|V_{r+1}|)^{d-2}}\bigg|^{q^{\prime}}\bigg]^{1/q^{\prime}}\cdot\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[|R^{1}_{r+1}-R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}|^{q^{\prime}}\bigg]^{1/q^{\prime}}.

Due to uniform boundedness of the
q^{th}
moments in the definition of SRI chains which ensures that
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\big[|R^{1}_{r+1}-R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}|^{q}\big]\leq C
for some uniform constant
C
.

The remaining sum can be shown to vanish using the invariance principle of Theorem
4.6
(and the anti-concentration estimate of Theorem
4.7
to establish the uniform integrability, noting
q^{\prime}<2
), while the power
2q
term vanishes thanks to uniform boundedness of the
(2q)^{th}
moments in the definition of SRI chains which ensures that
\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\big[\sum_{j=1,2}|R^{j}_{r+1}-R^{j}_{r}-s_{N}|^{2q}\big]\leq CN
for some uniform constant
C
. Dividing this sum by
N\log N
takes this term to
0
.

Combining the above four term-wise limits, we finally conclude that
\gamma_{N}\big((P_{\mathbf{dif}}-\mathrm{Id})u\big)\to-u(y)\phi(x_{1}).
As this is also the limit of
\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\big[\mathfrak{f}(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})\cdot\phi(N^{-1/2}x_{1}^{N})\big]
, we conclude the proof of (
4.14
).
∎

Example 4.26
.

Let
d\geq 3
and assume Assumption
4.14
. Further assume that
f
is continuous. Assume
N^{-1/2}x_{N}^{1}\to x_{1}
and
x^{1}_{N}-x^{2}_{N}
converges to
y\in I
. Then for any
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we have that

(4.15)

\sum_{r=0}^{N}f(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})\cdot\bigg(\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}r)\big)-\phi(N^{-1/2}x_{1}^{N})\bigg)\to 0.

in
L^{2}
under the measures
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}
. This is immediate from Theorem
4.25
. In particular the sequence

\sum_{r=0}^{N}f(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})\cdot\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}r)\big)

converges in law as
N\to\infty
to a random variable distributed as
\phi(x_{1})\cdot\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}f(X_{r})
where
X
is the limiting centered chain representing the difference of the two coordinates
R^{1},R^{2},
with
X_{0}=y
.

4.6.
Proof of SRI property for the tilted
k
-point Markov chains

In this section, we will show that the tilted measures
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}
that we defined in Section
2
are SRI chains.

Proposition 4.27
.

The tilted measures
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}
are SRI chains in the sense of Definition
4.1
. More precisely, we have
\boldsymbol{p}^{(k)}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(\Psi_{\sigma},M,F)
with
F=\mathrm{F_{dec}}
as in Assumption
1.2
, and
\boldsymbol{p}^{(k)}
is centered and
\delta
-repulsive for some
\delta>0
.

Moreover,
d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{p}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{q}^{(k)}_{\beta})\leq C|\beta|,
thus there exists
\beta_{0}>0
such that for
|\beta|<\beta_{0}
we have
\boldsymbol{q}^{(k)}_{\beta}\in\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{SRI}}_{k\times d}(\Psi_{\sigma/2},2M,F^{1/2})
and
\boldsymbol{q}^{(k)}_{\beta}
is
(\delta/2)
-repulsive.

Furthermore, for any sequence
\beta^{N}\to v
with
|v|<\beta_{0}
the conditions in Assumption
4.14
are satisfied by the sequence
\boldsymbol{q}_{\beta^{N}}^{(2)}.

Proof.

The reason why
\boldsymbol{p}^{(k)}
is centered is that the marginal law of each coordinate of the associated Markov chain is simply a random walk with increment distribution
\mu
.

The reason why
\boldsymbol{p}^{(k)}
is
\delta
-repulsive is because of the final item in Assumption
1.2
. Next, one has by definition that

\|\boldsymbol{p}^{(k)}(\mathbf{x},\bullet)-\boldsymbol{q}^{(k)}_{\beta}(\mathbf{x},\bullet)\|_{TV}=\int_{I^{k}}\bigg|e^{\beta\bullet\sum_{i=1}^{k}(y_{i}-x_{i})-\log\int_{I^{k}}e^{\beta\bullet\sum_{i=1}^{k}(a_{i}-x_{i})}\boldsymbol{p}^{(k)}(\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{a})}\;-\;1\bigg|\boldsymbol{p}^{(k)}(\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}).

Taylor expand everything in
\beta
, then note that the zeroth order terms cancel since it is 1, thus for small
\beta
the first order term is the first nontrivial term. Factor out
\beta
in this term and take the norm, then use Lemma
2.9
to bound the remaining integral by an absolute constant, and we arrive at the bound
d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{p}^{(k)},\boldsymbol{q}^{(k)}_{\beta})\leq C|\beta|.
The fact that
\boldsymbol{q}^{(k)}_{\beta}\in\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{SRI}}_{k\times d}(\Psi_{\sigma/2},2M,F^{1/2})
follows from e.g. Lemmas
2.9
and Lemma
A.2
. Next, we need to make sure that the regularity conditions of Theorem

To prove the repulsivity statement, note more generally that there exists
\epsilon_{0}=\epsilon_{0}(\sigma,M,\delta)>0
, so that
\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(\Psi_{\sigma},M,F)
and
d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})<\epsilon_{0}
together imply that
\boldsymbol{b}
is
(\delta/2)
-repulsive. To prove this, notice (for example by Lemma
A.2
) that

\bigg|\int_{I^{k}}|y_{i}-y_{j}-(x_{i}-x_{j})|\big(\boldsymbol{b}-\boldsymbol{b}_{0})(\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})\bigg|\leq C\cdot d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})^{1/2},

for some
C=C(q,M)>0.
Thus taking
\epsilon_{0}:=\delta^{2}/(4C)
gives the claim.

To prove the final statement, note that the strong Feller condition on the limiting chain is already provided by Assumption
1.2
Item (6).
∎

Corollary 4.28
.

Let
d\geq 2
. Let
H:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
be decreasing with
H(x)\leq Ae^{-bx}
. There exists
C,\beta_{0}>0
such that uniformly over all
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and all
f:I\to[0,\infty)
satisfying
|f(x)|\leq H(|x|)
one has

\sup_{\beta:|\beta|\leq\beta_{0}}\sup_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in I^{k}}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}[f(R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-y)]<Cr^{-\frac{d}{2}}\cdot Ab^{-d}\cdot\big(1+r^{-1/2}|x_{i}-x_{j}-y|\big)^{-K}.

The constant
C
only depends on
H
through
A,b
.

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
4.27
and Corollary
4.8
. The exponential decay assumption on
\mathrm{F_{dec}}
ensures that the conditions of Corollary
4.8
are satisfied.
∎

The following lemma allows us to apply Corollary
4.28
in conjunction with the Markov property to bound expectations of products of functions of exponential decay.

Lemma 4.29
.

Let
d\geq 2
.
Suppose that
F:\mathbb{[}0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is a decreasing function of exponential decay at infinity. Let
K>0
. Then there exists some
\epsilon>0
and a constant
C=C(K,F)>0
such that uniformly over
|\varsigma|<\epsilon
,
a_{1},a_{2}\in I
, and all
0=r_{0}<r_{1}\leq\cdots\leq r_{m}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}^{(\varsigma,2)}_{(a_{1},a_{2})}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{m}F(|R^{1}_{r_{j}}-R^{2}_{r_{j}}|)\bigg]\leq C\left(1+r_{1}^{-1/2}|a_{1}-a_{2}|\right)^{-K}\prod_{j=1}^{m}1\wedge(r_{j}-r_{j-1})^{-d/2}.

In particular, for fixed
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
we have that

(4.16)

\sum_{1\leq s_{1}\leq...\leq s_{m-1}\leq r}\mathbf{E}^{(\varsigma,2)}_{(a_{1},a_{2})}\bigg[F(|R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r}|)\prod_{j=1}^{m}F(|R^{1}_{r_{j}}-R^{2}_{r_{j}}|)\bigg]\leq\frac{C^{m}}{\omega_{r}(d)^{m-1}}r^{-d/2}(1+r^{-1/2}|a_{1}-a_{2}|\big)^{-K}.

One might think of
F
here as a place-holder for functions like
\mathrm{F_{dec}}
from Assumption
1.2
, as well as for particular important functions like
\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\sigma,\beta},\boldsymbol{u}_{v}
from Section
2
.

Proof.

The second bound follows readily from the first one. To prove the first one, consider
1<k\leq m
. If
r_{k}\neq r_{k-1}
, it follows from Corollary
4.28
that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}^{(\varsigma,2)}_{(a_{1},a_{2})}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{k}F(|R^{1}_{r_{j}}-R^{2}_{r_{j}}|)\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}^{(\varsigma,2)}_{(a_{1},a_{2})}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}F(|R^{1}_{r_{j}}-R^{2}_{r_{j}}|)\mathbf{E}^{(\varsigma,2)}_{(a_{1},a_{2})}\left[F(|R^{1}_{r_{k}}-R^{2}_{r_{k}}|)|\mathcal{F}_{r_{k-1}}\right]\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq C(r_{k}-r_{k-1})^{-d/2}\mathbf{E}^{(\varsigma,2)}_{(a_{1},a_{2})}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}F(|R^{1}_{r_{j}}-R^{2}_{r_{j}}|)\bigg].

On the other hand, if
r_{k}=r_{k-1}
, we can absorb the
k
th term of the product into the
{k-1}
st term so that the
(k-1)^{st}
term is
F^{2}(|R^{1}_{r_{k-1}}-R^{2}_{r_{k-1}}|)
. We can then repeat the above argument for the index
r_{k-1}
, as
F^{2}
is still a function of exponential decay, so Corollary
4.28
still applies.

Therefore, iterating over the product, we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}^{(\varsigma,2)}_{(a_{1},a_{2})}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{m}F(|R^{1}_{r_{j}}-R^{2}_{r_{j}}|)f(R^{1}_{s}-R^{2}_{s})\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq C\prod_{j=2}^{m+1}1\wedge(r_{j}-r_{j-1})^{-d/2}\mathbf{E}^{(\varsigma_{N},2)}_{(a_{1},a_{2})}\bigg[F(|R_{r_{1}}^{1}-R^{2}_{r_{1}}|)\bigg].

Finally, since
r_{1}\neq 0
, we can apply Theorem
4.7
to the remaining expectation, taking into account the initial positions of the two particles to obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}^{(\varsigma,2)}_{(a_{1},a_{2})}\bigg[F(|R_{r_{1}}^{1}-R^{2}_{r_{1}}|)\bigg]\leq Cr_{1}^{-d/2}\left(1+r_{1}^{-1/2}|a_{1}-a_{2}|\right)^{-K}.

∎

The following corollary illustrates how we will apply the previous results.

Corollary 4.30

(Markov property allows us to upgrade first-moment bounds to exponential moment bounds)
.

Fix
t\geq 0
. Recall
\psi_{N}(p,d)
from Assumption
1.9
, and define

\omega_{N}(d):=(N^{-1}\psi_{N}(p,d))^{2p}.

Consider
f:I\to[0,\infty)
of exponential decay at infinity. Then there exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
and
C=C(d,k,f)>0
such that we have the exponential moment bound

\sup_{N\geq 1}\sup_{|\beta|<\epsilon_{0}}\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}[e^{\frac{1}{C}\omega_{N}(d)\sum_{r=1}^{Nt}f(R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r})}]<\infty.

Before the proof, we remark that this is the crucial estimate that explains why the
\psi_{N}(p,d)
has the form that it has.

Proof.

Taylor expand the exponential inside the expectation, and commute the infinite sum with the expectation. We will obtain an infinite sum over
m\in\mathbb{N}
of terms of the form

\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{\omega_{N}(d)^{m}}{C^{m}m!}\sum_{1\leq r_{1}\leq...\leq r_{m}\leq Nt}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}\bigg[\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}f(R^{i}_{r_{\ell}}-R^{j}_{r_{\ell}})\bigg].

Corollary
4.29
implies that this last sum is upper bounded by

\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{\omega_{N}(d)^{m}}{C^{m}m!}\sum_{1\leq r_{1}\leq...\leq r_{m}\leq Nt}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}1\wedge(r_{\ell}-r_{\ell-1})^{-d/2}.

Note that in dimension
d
, this inner sum is precisely of order
m!\omega_{N}(d)^{m}
, so that this entire expression is bounded by

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{(C^{\prime})^{m}}{C^{m}}.

Now choose
C<C^{\prime}
and the series converges, completing the proof.
∎

5.
Limit formulas and estimates for the quadratic variations

The quadratic variation field (QVF) from (
3.13
) will be extremely useful when proving tightness in a certain topology and for identifying the limit points. In particular, it will help us to obtain the correct noise coefficient for the limiting fields.

5.1.
The limit formulas

Definition 5.1
.

Given
a_{1},a_{2}\in I
, we define
Q^{f}_{N}(t,\phi;a_{1},a_{2}):=Q^{f}_{N}(t,\phi;\delta_{a_{1}}\otimes\delta_{a_{2}})
, where
Q_{N}^{f}
is given by (
3.13
). In other words, the parameters
a_{1},a_{2}\in I
denote the starting locations of the two random walkers
R^{1},R^{2}
.

Note the identity

(5.1)

\mathbb{E}\big[Q_{N}^{f}(t,\phi;\nu_{1}\otimes\nu_{2})\big]=\frac{\int_{I^{2}}e^{\varsigma_{N}\bullet(a_{1}+a_{2})}\mathbb{E}\big[Q_{N}^{f}(t,\phi;a_{1},a_{2})\big]\nu_{1}(\mathrm{d}a_{1})\nu_{2}(\mathrm{d}a_{2})}{\int_{I^{2}}e^{\varsigma_{N}\bullet(a_{1}+a_{2})}\nu_{1}(\mathrm{d}a_{1})\nu_{2}(\mathrm{d}a_{2})},

which will be important later. This identity is immediate from the definition of
Q_{N}
in (
3.13
) and the form of the constants
C_{N,t,x,\nu}
from Definition
1.10
, in particular their dependence on
\nu.

Assumption 5.2
.

Let
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. Let
f_{N}:I\to\mathbb{R}
be any sequence of continuous functions such that
\sup_{N}|f_{N}(x)|\leq F(|x|)
for some function
F:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
of exponential decay. Furthermore assume that
f_{N}
converge uniformly as
N\to\infty
to some
f:I\to\mathbb{R}
. Consider sequences
a_{i}^{N}\in I\;(i=1,2)
such that
(N^{-1/2}a^{N}_{1},N^{-1/2}a^{N}_{2})\to(a_{1},a_{2})
respectively as
N\to\infty
. If
d\geq 2
, we further assume that
a_{1}^{N}
and
a_{2}^{N}
are well-separated in the sense that the limits satisfy
a_{1}\neq a_{2}
.

With this assumption, we will now prove some limit formulas for this quadratic variation field in each dimension. These quadratic variation formulas will be the key to identifying limit points later in Section
6
.

Fix
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Notice that

(5.2)

\mathbb{E}[Q^{f_{N}}_{N}(t,\phi;a_{1},a_{2})]=\\
N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt}\mathbf{E}_{(a_{1}^{N},a_{2}^{N})}^{(0,2)}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{2}C_{N,N^{-1}s,N^{-1/2}(R^{j}_{s}-a_{j}^{N}-N^{-1}d_{N}s)}\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{s}-N^{-1}d_{N}s)\big)\cdot f_{N}(R^{1}_{s}-R^{2}_{s})\bigg].

We would like to take the
N\to\infty
limit of this expectation.
Recall from Theorem
1.20
that we have

\boldsymbol{u}_{\varsigma_{N}}(x_{1}-x_{2})=\log\int_{I^{2}}e^{\varsigma_{N}\bullet\sum_{j=1,2}(y_{j}-x_{j})}\boldsymbol{p}^{(2)}(\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})-2\log M(\varsigma_{N}).

Using (
2.5
) to rewrite (
5.2
) in terms of the tilted measures introduced in Definition
2.7
, we obtain

N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt}\mathbf{E}^{(\varsigma_{N},2)}_{(a_{1}^{N},a_{2}^{N})}\bigg[e^{\sum_{r=0}^{s-1}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varsigma_{N}}(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})}\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{s}-N^{-1}d_{N}s)\big)\cdot f(R^{1}_{s}-R^{2}_{s})\bigg].

Finally, we Taylor expand the exponential into an infinite series and we obtain an infinite series of the form

(5.3)

N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{m!}\mathbf{E}^{(\varsigma_{N},2)}_{(a_{1}^{N},a_{2}^{N})}\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{r=0}^{s-1}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varsigma_{N}}(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})\bigg)^{m}\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{s}-N^{-1}d_{N}s)\big)\cdot f(R^{1}_{s}-R^{2}_{s})\bigg].

The limit of (
5.3
) as
N\to\infty
depends heavily on the dimension as shown in the following propositions. The proofs will involve taking the
N\to\infty
limit for each fixed
m
and then using dominated convergence to sum over
m
. The dominated convergence will be justified in Proposition
5.8
.

Proposition 5.3

(Limit formula for the QVF in
d=1
)
.

Let
d=1.
Assume that we are in the setting of Assumption
5.2
. Then for all
t>0
, if
N^{1/4p}\varsigma_{N}\to 0
then

(5.4)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\;\mathbb{E}\big[Q_{N}^{f_{N}}(t,\phi;a^{N}_{1},a^{N}_{2})\big]=\int_{I}f\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}\cdot\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}G(2s,a_{1}-a_{2})G(2s,y)\phi(\tfrac{1}{2}(y+a_{1}+a_{2}))\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}s.

The case where
d=1
and
N^{1/4p}\varsigma_{N}\to{\boldsymbol{v}}\neq 0
was addressed in
[
Par24
]
and leads to a more complicated limit, but this regime is not covered in this paper.

Proposition 5.4

(Limit formula for the QVF in
d=2
)
.

Let
d=2.
Assume that we are in the setting of Assumption
5.2
. Then there exists
\epsilon_{\mathrm{thr}}>0
(possibly depending on
F
) such that for all
t>0
and
{\boldsymbol{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
|{\boldsymbol{v}}|<\epsilon_{\mathrm{thr}}
, if
(\log N)^{1/2p}\varsigma_{N}\to{\boldsymbol{v}}
then

(5.5)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\;\mathbb{E}\big[Q_{N}^{f_{N}}(t,\phi;a^{N}_{1},a^{N}_{2})\big]=\frac{\int_{I}f\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}}{1-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{\mathrm{ext}}({\boldsymbol{v}})^{2}}\cdot 2\pi\cdot\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}G(2s,a_{1}-a_{2})G(2s,y)\phi(\tfrac{1}{2}(y+a_{1}+a_{2}))\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}s.

Proposition 5.5

(Limit formula for the QVF in
d\geq 3
)
.

Let
d\geq 3
. Assume that we are in the setting of Assumption
5.2
. Then there exists
\epsilon_{\mathrm{thr}}>0
(possibly depending on
F
and
d
) and a function
\Theta_{\mathrm{eff}}^{2}(f,\bullet):B_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(0,\epsilon_{\mathrm{thr}})\to\mathbb{R}
equal to
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(f)
at
{\boldsymbol{v}}=0
, such that for all
t>0
and
{\boldsymbol{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
|{\boldsymbol{v}}|<\epsilon_{\mathrm{thr}}
, if
\varsigma_{N}\to{\boldsymbol{v}}
then

(5.6)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\;\mathbb{E}\big[Q_{N}^{f_{N}}(t,\phi;a^{N}_{1},a^{N}_{2})\big]=\Theta_{\mathrm{eff}}^{2}(f;{\boldsymbol{v}})\cdot c_{d}\cdot\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}G^{(\boldsymbol{v})}(2s,a_{1}-a_{2})G^{(\boldsymbol{v})}(2s,y)\phi(\tfrac{1}{2}(y+a_{1}+a_{2}))\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}s,

where
G^{(\boldsymbol{v})}(t,x)
is the heat kernel with respect to the operator
\mathrm{div}(H_{{\boldsymbol{v}}}\nabla)
, where
H_{{\boldsymbol{v}}}
is the Hessian matrix of
\log M
at
{\boldsymbol{v}}
, and where
M
is the moment generating function of
\mu
as in (
1.10
).
The explicit expression for
\Theta_{\mathrm{eff}}^{2}(f;{\boldsymbol{v}})
is given by

\int_{I}\left[f(y)+\left(e^{\boldsymbol{u}_{\boldsymbol{v}}(y)}-1\right)\mathbf{E}_{y}^{(\boldsymbol{v},\mathrm{diff})}\left[\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{1\leq s_{1}\leq...\leq s_{n}<\infty}g_{\mathrm{count}}(\vec{s})\boldsymbol{u}_{\boldsymbol{v}}(X_{s_{1}})\cdots\boldsymbol{u}_{\boldsymbol{v}}(X_{s_{n}})\left(\sum_{s=s_{n}+1}^{\infty}f(X_{s})\right)\right]\right]\pi_{\boldsymbol{v}}^{\mathrm{inv}}(\mathrm{d}y).

Here
\pi_{v}^{\mathrm{inv}}
is the invariant measure for the difference process (see Proposition
2.8
) of the tilted two-point chain
\boldsymbol{q}^{(2)}_{v}
from (
2.3
), and

(5.7)

g_{\mathrm{count}}(\vec{r}):=\prod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\left(\#\{i:r_{i}=n\}\right)!}.

In the particular special case of
f=\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{v}:=e^{\boldsymbol{u}_{v}}-1,

we have the simpler form which matches with Theorem
1.20
:

(5.8)

\Theta_{\mathrm{eff}}^{2}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{v};v)=\int_{I}\big(e^{\boldsymbol{u}_{v}(y)}-1\big)\mathbf{E}_{y}^{(v,\mathrm{diff})}\bigg[e^{\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{u}_{v}(X_{s})}\bigg]\pi_{v}^{\mathrm{inv}}(\mathrm{d}y).

Remark 5.6
.

Note that the above quadratic variation formulas are computed for general functions
f_{N}
, which will be useful for certain upper bounds.
However, to identify the limiting SPDEs, we will ultimately be choosing

f_{N}=\boldsymbol{\eta}_{r_{1},r_{2}},f_{N}=\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{\frac{\varsigma_{N}}{|\varsigma_{N}|}}
or
f_{N}=\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{\varsigma_{N}}

as explained in Proposition
3.6
. The choice
f_{N}=\boldsymbol{\eta}_{r_{1},r_{2}}

will correspond to
Regime A
, and will be addressed later. In the case where

f_{N}=\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{\frac{\varsigma_{N}}{|\varsigma_{N}|}}

and
\frac{\varsigma_{N}}{|\varsigma_{N}|}\to\frac{\boldsymbol{v}}{|\boldsymbol{v}|}
,
we have that
f_{N}\to f:=\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{\frac{\boldsymbol{v}}{|\boldsymbol{v}|}}
.
Therefore
\int_{I}f\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}=\frac{\gamma_{\mathrm{ext}}({\boldsymbol{v}})^{2}}{|\boldsymbol{v}|^{2p}}
.
This choice is what ultimately gives us the noise coefficient in
Regime B
and
Regime C
. Finally, the case where
f_{N}=\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{\varsigma_{N}}
corresponds with
Regime D
and was addressed in (
5.8
).

5.2.
Proofs of the limit formulas

A key idea to prove the limit formulas will be to write exponential functionals of the relevant Markov processes as infinite series, then commute the infinite series with the limit
N\to\infty
. To do this, we will need various uniform estimates independent of the initial data as well as
N
.

Proposition 5.7

(Bounds for Uniform integrability)
.

Fix
f:I\to\mathbb{R}
of exponential decay at infinity.

(1)

We have a bound uniform over all
N\geq 1
, all initial conditions
a_{1},a_{2}\in I
and all
{\boldsymbol{v}}
in some neighborhood of the origin of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
:

(5.9)

\left|N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt}\frac{1}{m!}\mathbf{E}^{({\boldsymbol{v}},2)}_{(a_{1},a_{2})}\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{r=0}^{s-1}\boldsymbol{u}_{{\boldsymbol{v}}}(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})\bigg)^{m}\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{s}-N^{-1}d_{N}s)\big)\cdot f(R^{1}_{s}-R^{2}_{s})\bigg]\right|\\
\leq\begin{cases}C^{m}\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}|{\boldsymbol{v}}|^{2pm}\frac{(Nt)^{\frac{m}{2}}}{(m/2)!}&d=1\\
C^{m}\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}|{\boldsymbol{v}}|^{2pm}(\log Nt)^{2pm}\left[1+\big|\log\left(\frac{Nt}{1+|a_{1}-a_{2}|^{2}}\right)\big|\right]&d=2\\
C^{m}\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}|{\boldsymbol{v}}|^{2pm}\big(N^{-1/2}(1+|a_{1}-a_{2}|)\big)^{2-d}&d\geq 3\\
\end{cases}

Here
C
is an absolute constant not depending on
v,t,m,\phi,a_{1},a_{2}
.

(2)

Summing (
5.9
) over
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we have a bound uniform over all
N\geq 1
, all initial conditions
a_{1},a_{2}\in I
and all
{\boldsymbol{v}}
in some neighborhood of the origin of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
:

(5.10)

\left|N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt}\mathbf{E}^{({\boldsymbol{v}},2)}_{(a_{1},a_{2})}\bigg[e^{\sum_{r=0}^{s-1}\boldsymbol{u}_{{\boldsymbol{v}}}(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})}\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{s}-N^{-1}d_{N}s)\big)\cdot f(R^{1}_{s}-R^{2}_{s})\bigg]\right|\\
\leq\begin{cases}C\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}e^{N|{\boldsymbol{v}}|^{4p}t}&d=1\\
\frac{C\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}}{1-C|{\boldsymbol{v}}|^{2p}\log Nt}\left[1+\big|\log\left(\frac{Nt}{1+|a_{1}-a_{2}|^{2}}\right)\big|\right]&d=2\\
\frac{C\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}}{1-C|{\boldsymbol{v}}|^{2p}}\left(N^{-1/2}(1+|a_{1}-a_{2}|)\right)^{2-d}&d\geq 3\\
\end{cases}

The right side should be understood to be infinite if the denominator is non-positive. Here
C
is an absolute constant not depending on
v,t,m,\phi,a_{1},a_{2}
.

These bounds are quite sharp and they are illuminating, because their specific forms show where and why the critical scaling arises in each separate dimension. Notice that in
d=1
, the bounds on the right side is independent of the initial condition
(a_{1},a_{2})
, whereas in
d\geq 3
the bound on the right side is independent of
t
. Meanwhile in
d=2
, the bound depends on both
t
and
(a_{1},a_{2})
.

Proof.

Since
\phi
is a bounded function, we can just upper bound it by
\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}
which respects the bounds, so we can just assume
\phi\equiv 1
henceforth. Now since Item (1) implies Item (2) by summing over
m
, we focus on proving Item (1).

We can then rewrite the remaining terms on the left-hand side as follows:

\displaystyle CN^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt}\frac{1}{m!}\mathbf{E}^{({\boldsymbol{v}},2)}_{(a_{1},a_{2})}\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{r=0}^{s-1}\boldsymbol{u}_{{\boldsymbol{v}}}(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})\bigg)^{m}f(R^{1}_{s}-R^{2}_{s})\bigg]

\displaystyle=CN^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt}\sum_{0\leq r_{1}\leq\ldots\leq r_{m}\leq s-1}g_{\mathrm{count}}(\vec{r})\mathbf{E}^{({\boldsymbol{v}},2)}_{(a_{1},a_{2})}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{m}\boldsymbol{u}_{{\boldsymbol{v}}}(R^{1}_{r_{j}}-R^{2}_{r_{j}})f(R^{1}_{s}-R^{2}_{s})\bigg]

where
g_{\mathrm{count}}
is as in (
5.7
).
We will use the bound
g_{\mathrm{count}}(\vec{r})\leq 1
to omit the term
g_{\mathrm{count}}(\vec{r})
going forward. It follows from Propositions
2.12
,
2.16
, and
2.17
that for
|{\boldsymbol{v}}|<z_{0}/8
we have

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{u}_{{\boldsymbol{v}}}(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})|

\displaystyle\leq C\mathrm{F_{dec}}(|(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})|)^{1/2}|{\boldsymbol{v}}|^{2p}.

Abbreviating
F(x):=\max\{|f(x)|,\mathrm{F_{dec}}(|x|)^{1/2}\}
, it remains to show that the quantity

(5.11)

\displaystyle C^{m}|{\boldsymbol{v}}|^{2pm}\cdot N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\ \sum_{s=0}^{Nt}\sum_{0\leq r_{1}\leq\ldots\leq r_{m}\leq s-1}\mathbf{E}^{({\boldsymbol{v}},2)}_{(a_{1},a_{2})}\bigg[F(R^{1}_{s}-R^{2}_{s})\prod_{j=1}^{m+1}F(R^{1}_{r_{j}}-R^{2}_{r_{j}})\bigg],

can be upper-bounded by the right side of (
5.9
). The key to proving this will of course be (
4.16
).

We break this proof into cases based on dimension.

d=2
case.
Using (
4.16
) we see that

(
5.11
)

\displaystyle\leq C^{m}|{\boldsymbol{v}}|^{2pm}(\log(Nt))^{m}\sum_{s=1}^{Nt}s^{-1}(1+s^{-1/2}|a_{1}-a_{2}|)^{-K}

\displaystyle\leq C^{m}|{\boldsymbol{v}}|^{2pm}(\log(Nt))^{m}\cdot[1+|\log(Nt/(1+|a_{1}-a_{2}|^{2}))|],

where the last bound follows by taking any
K>2
so that those terms with
s
of order less than
|a_{1}-a_{2}|^{2}
become negligible in the sum.

d\geq 3
case.
Using (
4.16
) we see that

(
5.11
)

\displaystyle\leq C^{m}|{\boldsymbol{v}}|^{2pm}N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}s^{-d/2}(1+s^{-1/2}|a_{1}-a_{2}|)^{-K}

\displaystyle\leq C^{m}|{\boldsymbol{v}}|^{2pm}N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\cdot(1+|a_{1}-a_{2}|)^{2-d}.

where the last bound follows by taking any
K>d
so that those terms with
s
of order less than
|a_{1}-a_{2}|^{2}
become negligible in the sum.

d=1
case.
This case is somewhat different from the case of
d\geq 2
as (
4.16
) has only been proved for
d\geq 2
. However, for
d=1
the desired bound for (
5.11
) is instead implied by (
B.4
) of the appendix (noting that
\sqrt{m!}
and
(m/2)!
are of the same order up to some constant
C^{m}
), completing the proof.
∎

As an easy corollary, the following proposition will allow us to apply the dominated convergence theorem to interchange the limit in
N
and the sum in
m
in (
5.3
). Recall
\omega_{N}
from (
4.3
).

Corollary 5.8

(Bounds for dominated convergence)
.

Assume that we are in the setting of Assumption
5.2
. Then there exists some
C,\epsilon>0
such that for each
m\geq 0
, each
N\geq 0
and every
|{\boldsymbol{v}}|<\epsilon
, we have

(5.12)

\displaystyle\left|N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt}\frac{1}{m!}\mathbf{E}^{(\omega_{N}^{1/2p}{\boldsymbol{v}},2)}_{(a_{1}^{N},a_{2}^{N})}\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{r=0}^{s-1}u_{\omega_{N}^{1/2p}{\boldsymbol{v}}}(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})\bigg)^{m}\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{s}-N^{-1}d_{N}s)\big)\cdot f(R^{1}_{s}-R^{2}_{s})\bigg]\right|\leq C^{m+1}|{\boldsymbol{v}}|^{2pm}.

In
d=1
the right side can be improved to
C^{m+1}|{\boldsymbol{v}}|^{2pm}/(m/2)!
.

By choosing
|{\boldsymbol{v}}|
small enough, the right-hand side of (
5.12
) is summable. Furthermore, we see that if
{\boldsymbol{v}}=0
, then only the
m=0
term is nonzero.

Proof.

This is immediate from the previous proposition, specializing to the case where
(a_{1},a_{2})=(a_{1}^{N},a_{2}^{N})
and using their well-separatedness to argue that
|a^{1}_{N}-a^{2}_{N}|\geq cN^{1/2}
, and also replacing
v
by
\omega_{N}^{1/2p}v
.
∎

We now turn to proving Propositions
5.3
,
5.4
and
5.5
. Theorem
4.16
will be the key to calculating the relevant limits, therefore we introduce a shorthand notation for the quantities appearing there. Let

\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(f):=\int_{I}f\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}},

where
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
is the invariant measure for the difference process of the two-point motion
\boldsymbol{p}^{(2)}
, and has its unit normalization. Also let

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{(\boldsymbol{v})}(t,\phi;a_{1},a_{2}):=c_{d}\cdot\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}G^{(\boldsymbol{v})}(2s,a_{1}-a_{2})G^{(\boldsymbol{v})}(2s,y)\phi(\tfrac{1}{2}(y+a_{1}+a_{2}))\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}s,

where
G^{(\boldsymbol{v})}
is the heat kernel with respect to the operator
\mathrm{div}(H_{{\boldsymbol{v}}}\nabla)
. We will finally use the notation
\mathcal{G}(t,\phi;x_{1},x_{2})=\mathcal{G}^{(0)}(t,\phi;x_{1},x_{2})
to denote the case where
G^{(0)}=G
is the standard heat kernel without any tilting.

Proof of Proposition
5.3
.

Thanks to the anticoncentration estimate of Theorem
4.7
, we can assume all
f_{N}\equiv f
, as the difference is easily bounded and shown to vanish by e.g. Corollary
4.8
.

We can apply Proposition
5.7
to interchange the sum in
m
in (
5.2
) with the limit in
N
. Also note that it follows from Proposition
5.7
that for
m>0
, the
m
th term converges to
0
as
N\to\infty
since we are in the case
{\boldsymbol{v}}=0
. Therefore only the
m=0
term of the series contributes in the limit. The limit of this term is computed via Theorem
4.16
, giving the desired result.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.4
.

Again, thanks to the anticoncentration estimate of Theorem
4.7
, we can assume all
f_{N}\equiv f
, as the difference is easily bounded and shown to vanish.

It follows from Proposition
2.16
that we can Taylor expand
\boldsymbol{u}_{\varsigma_{N}}
in the
{\varsigma_{N}}
-variable to obtain

\boldsymbol{u}_{\varsigma_{N}}(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})=\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{\varsigma_{N}}(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})+\mathscr{R}_{\varsigma_{N}}(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r}),

where
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{\varsigma_{N}}(x_{1}-x_{2})
is a polynomial of degree
2p
in the
\varsigma_{N}
-variable and where we have that
\sup_{x\in I}|\mathscr{R}_{\varsigma_{N}}(x)|\leq C|\varsigma_{N}|^{2p+1}
.

Using this and interchanging the sum over
m
with the sum over
s
, the
m^{th}
term of the series in (
5.3
) becomes

\sum_{s=0}^{Nt}\sum_{0\leq r_{1}<...<r_{m}\leq s-1}\mathbf{E}^{(\varsigma_{N},2)}_{(a_{1}^{N},a_{2}^{N})}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{m}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{\varsigma_{N}}(R_{r_{j}}^{1}-R^{2}_{r_{j}})\cdot\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{s}-N^{-1}d_{N}s)\big)\cdot f(R^{1}_{s}-R^{2}_{s})\bigg]+O(1/\log N)

where the
O(1/\log N)
term is uniform over all variables and consists of collection of terms that either have repeated indices, or depend on the remainder term
\mathscr{R}_{N}
, and are thus negligible in the limit.

Let
{v}_{N}:=(\log N)^{\frac{1}{2p}}\varsigma_{N}
. We will now show that for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
one has

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}

\displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{Nt}\frac{1}{(\log N)^{m}}\sum_{0\leq r_{1}<...<r_{m}\leq s-1}\mathbf{E}^{(\varsigma_{N},2)}_{(a_{1}^{N},a_{2}^{N})}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{m}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{v_{N}}(R_{r_{j}}^{1}-R^{2}_{r_{j}})\cdot\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{s}-N^{-1}d_{N}s)\big)\cdot f(R^{1}_{s}-R^{2}_{s})\bigg]

(5.13)

\displaystyle=2^{-m}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{v})^{m}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(f)\cdot\mathcal{G}(t,\phi;a_{1},a_{2}).

Recall the defintion of
\gamma_{\mathrm{ext}}(\boldsymbol{v})
from (
1.17
). Observe that
\gamma_{\mathrm{ext}}(\boldsymbol{v})=\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{v})
. Then summing (
5.13
) over
m
and using the dominated convergence theorem to commute the sum over
m
with the limit as
N\to\infty
will yield the claim as long as the limiting vector
{\boldsymbol{v}}
satisfies
|{\boldsymbol{v}}|<\epsilon_{\mathrm{thr}}
where
\epsilon_{\mathrm{thr}}
is chosen so that
C\epsilon_{\mathrm{thr}}^{2p}<1
with
C
as appearing on the right side in Corollary
5.8
. This last condition ensures that the left-hand-side of (
5.12
) is summable in
m
, even with a supremum over all
N
so that dominated convergence may be applied.

Let us first consider
m=0
. This corresponds to finding the limit of

\sum_{s=0}^{Nt}\mathbf{E}^{(\varsigma_{N},2)}_{(a_{1}^{N},a_{2}^{N})}\bigg[\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{s}-N^{-1}d_{N}s)\big)\cdot f(R^{1}_{s}-R^{2}_{s})\bigg].

We can apply Theorem
4.16
