, since the tilted Markov kernels
\boldsymbol{q}^{(k)}_{\beta}
have the SRI property as noted in Proposition
4.27
to obtain that this converges to

\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(f)\cdot\mathcal{G}(t,\phi;a_{1},a_{2})

as desired.

Next we deal with the case
m\geq 1
.
We rearrange the indices on the left-hand side of (
5.13
) and applying the Markov property, we get that it equals

(5.14)

\sum_{r_{1}=0}^{Nt-1}\mathbf{E}^{(\varsigma_{N},2)}_{(a_{1}^{N},a_{2}^{N})}\Bigg[\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{v_{N}}(R^{1}_{r_{1}}-R^{2}_{r_{1}})\\
\cdot\mathbf{E}^{(\varsigma_{N},2)}_{(R^{1}_{r_{1}},R^{2}_{r_{1}})}\bigg[\frac{1}{(\log N)^{m}}\sum_{1\leq s_{1}<...<s_{m}\leq Nt}\prod_{j=1}^{m-1}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{v_{N}}(R^{1}_{s_{j}}-R^{2}_{s_{j}})\cdot f(R^{1}_{s_{m}}-R^{2}_{s_{m}})\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{s_{m}}-N^{-1}d_{N}s_{m})\big)\bigg]\Bigg]

Here we take the convention that the empty sum is equal to
1
and that
s_{0}=0
.
The main idea to proceed is now to apply the expectation limit formula of Theorem
4.16
in conjunction with the backwards-in-time propagation of test functions from Theorem
4.23
.

Fix
T>0
. We now claim that thanks to the backwards-time-propagation from Theorem
4.23
, one has

(5.15)

\lim_{N\to\infty}N\cdot\sup_{u\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}\cap[0,T]}\mathbf{E}^{(\varsigma_{N},2)}_{(a^{1}_{N},a^{2}_{N})}\Bigg[\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{v_{N}}(R^{1}_{Nu}-R^{2}_{Nu})\;\;\cdot\\
\bigg|\mathbf{E}^{(\varsigma_{N},2)}_{(R^{1}_{Nu},R^{2}_{Nu})}\bigg\{\frac{1}{(\log N)^{m}}\sum_{1\leq s_{1}<...<s_{m}\leq Nt}\prod_{j=1}^{m-1}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{v_{N}}(R^{1}_{s_{j}}-R^{2}_{s_{j}})\cdot f(R^{1}_{s_{m}}-R^{2}_{s_{m}})\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{s_{m}}-N^{-1}d_{N}s_{m})\big)\bigg\}\\
-\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{Nu}-N^{-1}d_{N}Nu)\big)\cdot 2^{-m}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(f)\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{v})^{m-1}\bigg|\Bigg]=0.

In other words, we can replace the inner expectation of (
5.14
) with the much simpler quantity given by some constant multiple of
\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r_{1}}-N^{-1}d_{N}r_{1})\big),
where the constant is precisely
2^{-m}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(f)\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{v})^{m-1}
. The proof of (
5.15
) follows readily from the backwards propagation of Theorem
4.23
, and for completeness we will sketch the argument at the end of this proof. For now we assume it is true. Then we may divide by
N
in (
5.15
), and sum over all
u\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}\cap[0,t]
. This means that
N\cdot\sup_{u\in(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z})\cap[0,T]}
can be replaced by
\sum_{u\in(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z})\cap[0,T]}
in the expression
5.15
, and the limit is still 0. Finally we can use this replacement to calculate the original limit (
5.14
). Indeed, it just remains to take the limit of

(5.16)

2^{-m}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(f)\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{v})^{m-1}\sum_{r_{1}=0}^{Nt-1}\mathbf{E}^{(\varsigma_{N},2)}_{(a_{1}^{N},a_{2}^{N})}\Bigg[\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{v_{N}}(R^{1}_{r_{1}}-R^{2}_{r_{1}})\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r_{1}}-N^{-1}d_{N}r_{1})\big)\Bigg].

We can now use Theorem
4.16
to immediately obtain that this converges to

\displaystyle 2^{-m}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{v})^{m}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(f)\cdot\mathcal{G}(t,\phi;a_{1},a_{2}),

as desired.

Proof of (
5.15
).
Consider any sequence
u_{N}\in(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\cap[0,T]
, with
\liminf_{N}u_{N}>0
. Define the change of measure
\tilde{\mathbf{E}}_{N}[F]:=N\cdot\mathbf{E}^{(\varsigma_{N},2)}_{(a^{1}_{N},a^{2}_{N})}\big[|\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{v_{N}}(R^{1}_{Nu_{N}}-R^{2}_{Nu_{N}})|\cdot F\big].
Such
\tilde{\mathbf{E}}_{N}
may not be probability measures, but their mass is bounded uniformly in
N
by the anticoncentration estimate of Theorem
4.7
(since
N^{d/2}=N
if
d=2
), so we can assume without loss of generality that they are probability measures. Now the same anticoncentration estimate and the fact that the initial states
a^{1}_{N},a^{2}_{N}
are assumed to be well-separated, one easily obtains
\tilde{\mathbf{E}}_{N}[R^{1}_{Nu_{N}}]\leq CN^{1/2}
, so that
N^{-1/2}R^{1}_{Nu_{N}}
is tight under
\tilde{\mathbf{E}}_{N}.

Similarly
|R^{1}_{Nu_{N}}-R^{2}_{Nu_{N}}|
is tight under
\tilde{\mathbf{E}}_{N}
since
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{v_{N}}
decays rapidly. This tightness ensures that the random walkers are close together at time
Nu_{N}
so that we can apply Theorem
4.23
with
\phi_{\ell}\equiv 1
for
1\leq\ell\leq m-1
, and
\phi_{m}:=\phi
. This yields that under
\tilde{\mathbf{E}}_{N}
, one has convergence in law as
N\to\infty
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}^{(\varsigma_{N},2)}_{(R^{1}_{Nu_{N}},R^{2}_{Nu_{N}})}\bigg\{\frac{1}{(\log N)^{m}}\sum_{1\leq s_{1}<...<s_{m}\leq Nt}\prod_{j=1}^{m-1}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{v_{N}}(R^{1}_{s_{j}}-R^{2}_{s_{j}})\cdot f(R^{1}_{s_{m}}-R^{2}_{s_{m}})\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{s_{m}}-N^{-1}d_{N}s_{m})\big)\bigg\}

\displaystyle-\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{Nu_{N}}-d_{N}u_{N})\big)\cdot 2^{-m}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{v})^{m-1}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(f)\to 0.

From here, (
5.15
) follows readily by e.g., bounded convergence.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.5
.

As usual, thanks to the anticoncentration estimate of Theorem
4.7
, we can assume all
f_{N}\equiv f
, as the difference is easily bounded and shown to vanish.

Interchanging the sum over
m
with the sum over
s
, the
m^{th}
term of the series in (
5.3
) becomes

(5.17)

N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt}\sum_{0\leq r_{1}\leq...\leq r_{m}\leq s-1}g_{\mathrm{count}}(\vec{r})\mathbf{E}^{(\varsigma_{N},2)}_{(a_{1}^{N},a_{2}^{N})}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{m}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varsigma_{N}}(R^{1}_{r_{j}}-R^{2}_{r_{j}})\cdot\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{s}-N^{-1}d_{N}s)\big)\cdot f(R^{1}_{s}-R^{2}_{s})\bigg].

For each
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we would like to take the limit of this expression as
N\to\infty
. Since
\varsigma_{N}\to v
as
N\to\infty
, notice that
\boldsymbol{u}_{\varsigma_{N}}
converges to
\boldsymbol{u}_{v}
, the function defined in Theorem
1.20
. Similarly,
\boldsymbol{q}^{(2)}_{\varsigma_{N}}\to\boldsymbol{q}_{{\boldsymbol{v}}}^{(2)}
as defined in Theorem
1.20
. The expectation with respect to the difference between the coordinates of this limiting chain is denoted by
\mathbf{E}_{y}^{(v,\mathrm{diff})}.
Unlike the proof of Proposition
5.4
, the terms with repeated indices will
not
be irrelevant here since
\varsigma_{N}
is not necessarily going to
0
here.

By Theorem
4.16
, the limit of the
m=0
term is

\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(f)\cdot\mathcal{G}^{(\boldsymbol{v})}(t,\phi;a_{1},a_{2}).

Here
\pi_{v}^{\mathrm{inv}}
is the invariant measure for
\boldsymbol{q}_{{\boldsymbol{v}}}^{(2)}
, and it has its unit normalization as in Theorem
4.16
. Similarly, the heat kernel
G^{(v)}
is with respect to the operator
\mathrm{div}(H_{{\boldsymbol{v}}}\nabla)

where
H_{{\boldsymbol{v}}}
is the Hessian matrix of
\log M
at
{\boldsymbol{v}}
, where
M
is the moment generating function of
\mu
as in (
1.10
).

Next we consider the case
m\geq 1
. As in the proof of Proposition
5.4
, we will assume without loss of generality that
\mathrm{F_{dec}}(\cdot)
is compactly supported so that the sequence of functions
\boldsymbol{u}_{\varsigma_{N}}(\cdot)
has uniformly bounded support in
N
. This ensures that at time
r_{1}
, the distance
|R^{1}_{r_{1}}-R^{2}_{r_{1}}|
is uniformly bounded in
N
on the event where
\boldsymbol{u}_{\varsigma_{N}}(R^{1}_{r_{1}}-R^{2}_{r_{1}})>0
. We can then use Theorem
4.25
to propagate the function
\phi
backwards in time to time
r_{1}
in the expression (
5.17
). The details are similar to the proof of Proposition
5.4
for the
d=2
case, and follow the same lines as the proof of (
5.15
) above (using Theorem
4.25
as opposed to Theorem
4.23
). The effect of this backwards propagation is that we can replace
\phi(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{s}-N^{-1}d_{N}s))
in (
5.17
) with the quantity
\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r_{1}}-N^{-1}d_{N}r_{1})\big)
, without changing the value of the
N\to\infty
limit. More precisely, we just need to compute the limit of

\displaystyle N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt}\sum_{0\leq r_{1}\leq...\leq r_{m}\leq s-1}g_{\mathrm{count}}(\vec{r})\mathbf{E}^{(\varsigma_{N},2)}_{(a_{1}^{N},a_{2}^{N})}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{m}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varsigma_{N}}(\mathbf{R}_{r_{j}})\cdot\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r_{1}}-N^{-1}d_{N}r_{1})\big)\cdot f(R^{1}_{s}-R^{2}_{s})\bigg].

Rearranging the indices and applying the Markov property, this equals

N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{r_{1}=0}^{Nt-1}\mathbf{E}^{(\varsigma_{N},2)}_{(a_{1}^{N},a_{2}^{N})}\Bigg[\sum_{l=1}^{m}\frac{1}{l!}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varsigma_{N}}(R^{1}_{r_{1}}-R^{2}_{r_{1}})^{l}\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r_{1}}-N^{-1}d_{N}r_{1})\big)\\
\cdot\mathbf{E}^{(\varsigma_{N},2)}_{(R^{1}_{r_{1}},R^{2}_{r_{1}})}\left[\sum_{1\leq s_{1}\leq...\leq s_{m-l}\leq Nt-1}g_{\mathrm{count}}(\vec{s})\prod_{j=1}^{m-l}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varsigma_{N}}(\mathbf{R}_{s_{j}})\cdot\sum_{s=s_{m-l}+1}^{Nt}f(R^{1}_{s}-R^{2}_{s})\right]\Bigg]

As usual, we take the convention that the empty sum is equal to
1
and that
s_{0}=0
.

Applying Theorem
4.16
, we immediately obtain that this converges to

(5.18)

\mathcal{G}^{(\boldsymbol{v})}(t,\phi;a_{1},a_{2})\cdot\\
\int_{I}\sum_{l=1}^{m}\frac{1}{l!}\boldsymbol{u}_{\boldsymbol{v}}(y)^{l}\mathbf{E}_{y}^{(\boldsymbol{v},\mathrm{diff})}\bigg[\sum_{1\leq s_{1}\leq...\leq s_{m-l}<\infty}g_{\mathrm{count}}(\vec{s})\boldsymbol{u}_{\boldsymbol{v}}(X_{s_{1}})\cdots\boldsymbol{u}_{\boldsymbol{v}}(X_{s_{m-l}})\sum_{s=s_{m-l}+1}^{\infty}f(X_{s})\bigg]\pi_{\boldsymbol{v}}^{\mathrm{inv}}(\mathrm{d}y).

Finally, if the limiting vector
{\boldsymbol{v}}
satisfies
|{\boldsymbol{v}}|<\epsilon_{\mathrm{thr}}
where
\epsilon_{\mathrm{thr}}
is chosen so that
C|{\boldsymbol{v}}|^{2p}<1
in Proposition
5.8
, then the left-hand-side of (
5.12
) is absolutely summable in
m
(even with a supremum over
N
). We can therefore use the dominated convergence theorem to compute (
5.3
) by commuting the sum in
m
with the limit as
N\to\infty
. This then reduces to summing the expression (
5.18
) over all
m\geq 0
. Letting
n=m-l
, this yields the limiting value of

\mathcal{G}^{(\boldsymbol{v})}(t,\phi;a_{1},a_{2})\cdot\\
\int_{I}\left[f(y)+\left(e^{\boldsymbol{u}_{v}(y)}-1\right)\mathbf{E}_{y}^{(v,\mathrm{diff})}\left[\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{1\leq s_{1}\leq...\leq s_{n}<\infty}g_{\mathrm{count}}(\vec{s})\boldsymbol{u}_{v}(X_{s_{1}})\cdots\boldsymbol{u}_{v}(X_{s_{n}})\left(\sum_{s=s_{n}+1}^{\infty}f(X_{s})\right)\right]\right]\pi_{v}^{\mathrm{inv}}(\mathrm{d}y).

The second factor is precisely
\Theta(f,v)
as defined in the statement of the proposition.

Specialization to the specific case
f=\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{v}
.
Proposition
5.5
gives the limit of the quadratic variation field for a general function
f
. We now plug in
f(y)=\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{v}(y)=e^{\boldsymbol{u}_{v}(y)}-1=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\boldsymbol{u}_{v}(X_{s})^{k}.
We can now further simplify the term involving
f
as follows:

\displaystyle f(y)+\left(e^{\boldsymbol{u}_{v}(y)}-1\right)\mathbf{E}_{y}^{(v,\mathrm{diff})}\bigg[\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{1\leq s_{1}\leq...\leq s_{n}<\infty}g_{\mathrm{count}}(\vec{s})\boldsymbol{u}_{v}(X_{s_{1}})\cdots\boldsymbol{u}_{v}(X_{s_{n}})\sum_{s=s_{n}+1}^{\infty}f(X_{s})\bigg]

\displaystyle=\left(e^{\boldsymbol{u}_{v}(y)}-1\right)\left[1+\mathbf{E}_{y}^{(v,\mathrm{diff})}\bigg[\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{1\leq s_{1}\leq...\leq s_{n}<\infty}g_{\mathrm{count}}(\vec{s})\boldsymbol{u}_{v}(X_{s_{1}})\cdots\boldsymbol{u}_{v}(X_{s_{n}})\sum_{s=s_{n}+1}^{\infty}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\boldsymbol{u}_{v}(X_{s})^{k}\right)\bigg]\right]

\displaystyle=\left(e^{\boldsymbol{u}_{v}(y)}-1\right)\left[1+\mathbf{E}_{y}^{(v,\mathrm{diff})}\bigg[\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{1\leq s_{1}\leq...\leq s_{n}<s_{n+1}=\cdots=s_{n+k}<\infty}g_{\mathrm{count}}(\vec{s})\boldsymbol{u}_{v}(X_{s_{1}})\cdots\boldsymbol{u}_{v}(X_{s_{n+k}})\bigg]\right].

In the last line, we absorbed the
\frac{1}{k!}
into the function
g_{\mathrm{count}}(\vec{s})
. Finally, note that we are now summing over all possible multi-indices
s
such that
s_{1}\geq 1
. Therefore, this equals

\displaystyle\left(e^{\boldsymbol{u}_{v}(y)}-1\right)\mathbf{E}_{y}^{(v,\mathrm{diff})}\bigg[e^{\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{u}_{v}(X_{s})}\bigg].

Putting everything together, we arrive at

\mathcal{G}^{(\boldsymbol{v})}(t,\phi;a_{1},a_{2})\cdot\int_{I}\left(e^{\boldsymbol{u}_{v}(y)}-1\right)\mathbf{E}_{y}^{(v,\mathrm{diff})}\bigg[e^{\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{u}_{v}(X_{s})}\bigg]\pi_{v}^{\mathrm{inv}}(\mathrm{d}y).

∎

Remark 5.9

(The important difference between
d=1
and
d\geq 2
when looking at the critical location scale)
.

We explain here why we cannot use these techniques to study the
d=1
case in the critical scale
|\varsigma_{N}|\sim\psi_{N}(p,d)
. This is because the renewal trick of Theorem
4.20
that was used to prove the limit theorem for
Q_{N}
in
d\geq 2
no longer works. This is due to the existence of a
nontrivial
limit in distribution for the intersection processes in
d=1
(the limit being the Brownian local time process), which does not happen for
d\geq 2
. As indicated by Theorem
1.13
, this is related to the fact that the fluctuations of the quenched density
P^{\omega}(t,x)
are
not
Gaussian in
d=1
, for
x
at the critical scale. Letting
Q^{\infty}
denote a limit point of
Q_{N}
as
N\to\infty
, and letting
\mathcal{F}_{s}
denote the canonical filtration on
C([0,T],\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})),
this boils down to the fact that at the critical scale
|\varsigma_{N}|\sim\psi_{N}(p,d)
, the following are true: if
d\geq 2
, then from Propositions
5.4
and
5.5
, the quantity
\mathbb{E}[Q^{\infty}(t,\phi)-Q^{\infty}(s,\phi)|\mathcal{F}_{s}]
is a deterministic quantity; but if
d=1
then
\mathbb{E}[Q^{\infty}(t,\phi)-Q^{\infty}(s,\phi)|\mathcal{F}_{s}]
is a genuinely random quantity (see
[
Par24
, Proposition 5.3]
).

Up until now, we have studied the quadratic variation field with initial conditions given by
\delta_{a_{1}^{N}}\otimes\delta_{a_{2}^{N}}.
We now consider the quadratic variation field with initial conditions given by
\nu^{\otimes 2}_{N}.

Theorem 5.10

(The key estimate with general initial data in all dimensions)
.

Consider any (microscopic) sequence of measures
\nu_{N}
on
I
for which the associated (macroscopic) sequence of initial conditions
\mathfrak{H}^{N}(0,\bullet)
as defined in (
1.11
) is a good sequence in the sense of Definition
1.12
, converging weakly as measures on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to a measure
\mathfrak{H}_{0}
. Let
Q_{N}(t,\bullet;\nu_{N}^{\otimes 2})
be as in (
3.13
). Then, we have the limits:

•

If
d=1
and
N^{1/4p}\varsigma_{N}\to 0
then we have

\lim_{N\to\infty}\;\mathbb{E}\big[Q_{N}^{f}(t,\phi;\nu_{N}^{\otimes 2})\big]=\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(f)\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big((G_{s}*\mathfrak{H}_{0})(a)\big)^{2}\phi(a)\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}s.

•

If
d=2
and
(\log N)^{1/2p}\varsigma_{N}\to{\boldsymbol{v}}
with
|\boldsymbol{v}|
sufficiently small, then we have

\lim_{N\to\infty}\;\mathbb{E}\big[Q_{N}^{f}(t,\phi;\nu_{N}^{\otimes 2})\big]=\frac{\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(f)}{1-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{\mathrm{ext}}({\boldsymbol{v}})^{2}}\cdot 2\pi\cdot\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big((G_{s}*\mathfrak{H}_{0})(a)\big)^{2}\phi(a)\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}s.

•

if
d\geq 3
and if
\varsigma_{N}\to{\boldsymbol{v}}
with
|\boldsymbol{v}|
sufficiently small, then

\lim_{N\to\infty}\;\mathbb{E}\big[Q_{N}^{f}(t,\phi;\nu_{N}^{\otimes 2})\big]=\Theta_{\mathrm{eff}}^{2}(f;{\boldsymbol{v}})\cdot c_{d}\cdot\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big((G_{s}^{({\boldsymbol{v}})}*\mathfrak{H}_{0})(a)\big)^{2}\phi(a)\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}s,

where
\Theta_{\mathrm{eff}}^{2}(f,{\boldsymbol{v}})
is as in Proposition
5.5
.

We remark that the integrals on the right side are in fact convergent due to
\mathfrak{H}_{0}^{N}
being a good sequence as in Definition
1.12
, which is not obvious but will be shown as an artifact of the proof.

Proof.

The main idea is to integrate all of the expressions from Propositions
5.3

-

5.5
over the initial conditions
(a_{1}^{N},a_{2}^{N}),
and use the assumption of goodness to obtain a vanishing bound on the part of the integral where
a^{N}_{1},a^{N}_{2}
are close to each other, using Proposition
5.7
.

Recall from (
5.1
) that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[Q_{N}^{f}(t,\phi;\nu_{N}^{\otimes 2})\big]

\displaystyle=\frac{\int_{I^{2}}e^{\varsigma_{N}\bullet(a_{1}+a_{2})}\mathbb{E}\big[Q_{N}^{f}(t,\phi;a_{1},a_{2})\big]\nu_{N}^{\otimes 2}(\mathrm{d}a_{1},\mathrm{d}a_{2})}{\int_{I^{2}}e^{\varsigma_{N}\bullet(a_{1}+a_{2})}\nu_{N}^{\otimes 2}(\mathrm{d}a_{1},\mathrm{d}a_{2})}

\displaystyle=\int_{I^{2}}\mathbb{E}\big[Q_{N}^{f}(t,\phi;a_{1},a_{2})\big](\nu_{N}^{\varsigma_{N}})^{\otimes 2}(\mathrm{d}a_{1},\mathrm{d}a_{2}),

where
\nu^{\varsigma_{N}}_{N}(\mathrm{d}x):=e^{\varsigma_{N}\bullet x-\log\int_{I}e^{\varsigma_{N}\bullet a}\nu_{N}(\mathrm{d}a)}\nu_{N}(\mathrm{d}x)
are the tilted initial states. We can reinterpret this as
a nested expectation given by

(5.19)

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}\big[Q_{N}^{f}(t,\phi;\mathfrak{a}^{N}_{1},\mathfrak{a}^{N}_{2})|\mathfrak{a}_{1}^{N},\mathfrak{a}_{2}^{N}\big]\big]

where
\mathfrak{a}_{i}^{N}
are independent
I
-valued random variables, each distributed as
\nu_{N}^{\varsigma_{N}}
(and also independent of the kernels
K_{i}
). The
distributional limit as
N\to\infty
of the conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}[Q_{N}^{f}(t,\phi;\mathfrak{a}_{1}^{N},\mathfrak{a}_{2}^{N})|\mathfrak{a}_{1}^{N},\mathfrak{a}_{2}^{N}]
is given precisely by the expressions in Propositions
5.3

-

5.5
, where
(a_{1},a_{2})
there are replaced by
(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\mathfrak{a}_{2})
which are distributed as
\mathfrak{H}_{0}^{\otimes 2}
. This is because
\mathfrak{H}_{0}^{\otimes 2}
is the limiting law of
N^{-1/2}(\mathfrak{a}_{1}^{N},\mathfrak{a}_{2}^{N})
, simply by the assumptions in the statement of the theorem.

We claim that the sequence
\mathbb{E}[Q_{N}^{f}(t,\phi;\mathfrak{a}_{1}^{N},\mathfrak{a}_{2}^{N})|\mathfrak{a}_{1}^{N},\mathfrak{a}_{2}^{N}]
is uniformly integrable, which will be justified further below. Thus we can interchange the outer expectation in (
5.19
) with the distributional limit at
N\to\infty
as calculated by Propositions
5.3

-

5.5
. Thus, the limit as
N\to\infty
of (
5.19
) would given by the
(f,v)
-dependent prefactor which depends on the dimension (e.g.
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(f)
, or
\frac{\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(f)}{1-\gamma_{\mathrm{ext}}(v)^{2}}\cdot 2\pi
, or
\Theta_{\mathrm{eff}}(f;v)\cdot c_{d}
), in turn multiplied by

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\mathcal{G}(t,\phi,a_{1},a_{2})\mathfrak{H}_{0}^{\otimes 2}(\mathrm{d}a_{1},\mathrm{d}a_{2})=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}G(2s,x)G(2s,a_{1}-a_{2})\phi(\tfrac{1}{2}(x+a_{1}+a_{2}))\mathfrak{H}_{0}^{\otimes 2}(\mathrm{d}a_{1},\mathrm{d}a_{2})\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}s,

where
G
should be replaced by
G^{(v)}
if
d\geq 3
and
v\neq 0
(i.e.,
Regime D
). Substitute
x\to x-a_{1}-a_{2}
, and we obtain that the last integral can be rewritten as

\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}G(2s,x-a_{1}-a_{2})G(2s,a_{1}-a_{2})\mathfrak{H}_{0}^{\otimes 2}(\mathrm{d}a_{1},\mathrm{d}a_{2})\cdot\phi(\tfrac{1}{2}x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}s.

Making the change of variables
x\to\frac{1}{2}x
, we obtain that the last expression is the same as

(5.20)

\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}G(\tfrac{s}{2},x-\tfrac{1}{2}(a_{1}+a_{2}))G(2s,a_{1}-a_{2})\mathfrak{H}_{0}^{\otimes 2}(\mathrm{d}a_{1},\mathrm{d}a_{2})\cdot\phi(x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}s.

Finally, use the identity
G(\frac{s}{2},x-\frac{1}{2}(a_{1}+a_{2}))G(2s,a_{1}-a_{2})=G(s,x-a_{1})G(s,x-a_{2}),
and notice (for
Regime D
) that this identity also remains true for the
v
-tilted heat kernel, because
G^{(v)}(s,x)=G(s,H_{v}^{-1/2}x)
. Thus we obtain that the above integral equals

\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(G_{s}*\mathfrak{H}_{0}(x))^{2}\cdot\phi(x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}s,

and likewise for
Regime D
but with
G
replaced by
G^{(v)}
.
It is not entirely obvious a priori that this integral converges, as one only has
G_{s}*\mathfrak{H}_{0}(a)^{2}\leq t^{2\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}-2}
which is not necessarily an integrable singularity for
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}\in(0,1/2)
. However, using (
5.20
),
the convergence follows by first performing the integral over
x
first, and noting that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}G(\frac{s}{2},x-\frac{1}{2}(a_{1}+a_{2}))\phi(x)\mathrm{d}x\leq C
for some absolute constant
C
not depending on
s
, then noting that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}G(2s,a_{1}-a_{2})\mathfrak{H}_{0}^{\otimes 2}(\mathrm{d}a_{1},\mathrm{d}a_{2})\leq Cs^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}-1}
thanks to the good sequence property from Definition
1.12
.

Justification of the uniform integrability.
Definition
1.12
will be crucial here. To first give some rough idea of how we need to apply that definition, notice first that if we have some fixed finite measure
\mathfrak{H}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
of Holder class
C^{2(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}-2)}
and exponential tails, then for
d\geq 2
we can form the convolution
\int_{I}|x-y|^{2-d}\mathfrak{H}(\mathrm{d}y)
by writing
|x|^{2-d}\leq\phi(x)+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}2^{n(d-2)}\psi(2^{n}x)
for some smooth bounded
\phi\geq 0
and compactly supported
\psi\geq 0
. Performing the sum shows that the convolution is (just barely) well defined: writing
\delta_{n}(x):=2^{nd}\psi(2^{n}x)
one sees that
\int|x|^{2-d}\mathfrak{H}(\mathrm{d}x)\leq\sum_{n}2^{-2n}|(\delta_{n},\mathfrak{H})|\leq\sum_{n}2^{-2\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}n}<\infty.
To rigorously apply these ideas, we are going to break up the proof into cases depending on the dimension.

d=1
case.
This is the easiest case. By Proposition
5.7
, one has
\sup_{a_{1},a_{2},N}\mathbb{E}[Q^{f}_{N}(t,\phi,a_{1},a_{2})]<\infty
for each fixed
t>0
, which immediately implies the required uniform integrability, in fact the expectation with the randomized initial conditions
\mathfrak{a}_{N}^{1},\mathfrak{a}_{N}^{2}
will just converge by the bounded convergence theorem.

d\geq 3
case.
By Proposition
5.7
, the definition of
Q_{N}
, and
|\varsigma_{N}|<\epsilon_{\mathrm{thr}}
we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Q_{N}^{f}(t,\phi;a_{1},a_{2})]

\displaystyle\leq N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{E}^{(\varsigma_{N},2)}_{(a_{1},a_{2})}[e^{\sum_{r=0}^{s-1}\boldsymbol{u}_{v}(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})}f(R^{1}_{s}-R^{2}_{s})]

\displaystyle\leq CN^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}\cdot(1+|a_{1}-a_{2}|)^{2-d}.

Now, we are going to prove uniform integrability by proving that there exists some
q>1
such that
\sup_{N}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}[Q_{N}^{f}(t,\phi;\mathfrak{a}^{N}_{1},\mathfrak{a}^{N}_{2})|\mathfrak{a}_{1}^{N},\mathfrak{a}_{2}^{N}]^{q}\big]<\infty.
It suffices to show that for
q>1
sufficiently small one has that

\displaystyle\sup_{N}\int_{I^{2}}\big(N^{-1/2}(1+|x-y|)\big)^{q(2-d)}\nu_{N}^{\varsigma_{N}}(\mathrm{d}x)\nu_{N}^{\varsigma_{N}}(\mathrm{d}y)<\infty,

where
\nu^{\varsigma_{N}}_{N}(\mathrm{d}x):=e^{\varsigma_{N}\bullet x-\log\int_{I}e^{\varsigma_{N}\bullet a}\nu_{N}(\mathrm{d}a)}\nu_{N}(\mathrm{d}x)
are the tilted initial states. Make the change of variable
(x,y)=N^{1/2}(a,b)
, and recall (from e.g.(
1.11
)) that
\mathfrak{H}_{0}^{N}(N^{-1/2}\mathrm{d}x)=\nu_{N}^{\varsigma_{N}}(\mathrm{d}x)
. Thus we get that

\displaystyle\int_{I^{2}}\big(N^{-1/2}(1+|x-y|)\big)^{q(2-d)}\nu_{N}^{\varsigma_{N}}(\mathrm{d}x)\nu_{N}^{\varsigma_{N}}(\mathrm{d}y)

\displaystyle=\int_{I^{2}}\big(N^{-1/2}+|a-b|\big)^{q(2-d)}\mathfrak{H}_{0}^{N}(\mathrm{d}a)\mathfrak{H}_{0}^{N}(\mathrm{d}b)

\displaystyle=\int_{I}\bigg[\int_{I}\big(N^{-1/2}+|a-b|\big)^{q(2-d)}\mathfrak{H}_{0}^{N}(\mathrm{d}a)\bigg]\mathfrak{H}_{0}^{N}(\mathrm{d}b)

(5.21)

\displaystyle\leq C\sup_{b}\int_{I}\big(N^{-1/2}+|a-b|\big)^{q(2-d)}\mathfrak{H}_{0}^{N}(\mathrm{d}a),

where we used the fact that
\mathfrak{H}_{0}^{N}
has uniformly bounded total mass thanks to the first bound in Definition
1.12
. Now as long as
q<1+\frac{2\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}}{d-2}
we will show that this last quantity is bounded independently of
N
thanks to the second bound of Definition
1.12
. Choose some smooth bounded
\phi\geq 0
and smooth compactly supported
\psi\geq 0
with the property that
\big(N^{-1/2}+|x|\big)^{q(2-d)}\leq\phi(x)+\sum_{n=1}^{\lfloor\frac{1}{2}\log_{2}N\rfloor}2^{nq(d-2)}\psi(2^{n}x).
Using the second bound in the good sequence condition, one sees that
\sup_{b}\int_{I}2^{nd}\psi(2^{n}(a-b))\mathfrak{H}_{0}^{N}(\mathrm{d}a)\leq 2^{-2n(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}-1)}
for
n\leq\frac{1}{2}\log_{2}N
. The uniform and at-worst-exponential tail decay of
\mathfrak{H}_{0}^{N}
also implies that
(\phi,\mathfrak{H}_{0}^{N})\leq C
. Hence (
5.21
) is bounded above by
C\sum_{n\geq 0}2^{nq(d-2)-nd-2n(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}-1)}
, which converges for
q<1+\frac{2\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}}{d-2}
, with an upper bound independent of
N
, proving the uniform integrability in the case where
d\geq 3
.

d=2
case.
We proceed very similarly to the
d\geq 3
case above. By Proposition
5.7
and
|\varsigma_{N}|<\epsilon_{\mathrm{thr}}(\log N)^{-1/2p},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Q_{N}^{f}(t,\phi;a_{1},a_{2})]

\displaystyle\leq\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt}\mathbf{E}^{(\varsigma_{N},2)}_{(a_{1},a_{2})}[e^{\sum_{r=0}^{Nt}\boldsymbol{u}_{v}(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})}f(R^{1}_{s}-R^{2}_{s})]

\displaystyle\leq C\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}\cdot(1+|\log(Nt/(1+|a_{1}-a_{2}|^{2}))|).

Again it suffices to show that for some
q>1
one has that

\displaystyle\sup_{N}\int_{I^{2}}|\log(N/(1+|x-y|^{2}))|^{q}\nu_{N}^{\varsigma_{N}}(\mathrm{d}x)\nu_{N}^{\varsigma_{N}}(\mathrm{d}y)<\infty.

Since
d=2
one can actually show that this is true for all
q>1
. Write
|\log x|=\log_{+}x+\log_{-}x
. Dealing with
\log_{+}
is very easy thanks to the uniform-in-
N
exponential tail decay on
\mathfrak{H}_{0}^{N}
coming from the first bound in Definition
1.12
. The negative part
\log_{-}
is more subtle and requires the second bound of Definition
1.12
. Make change of variable
(x,y)\to N^{1/2}(x,y)
and we get that the negative part of the above integral is bounded by

\displaystyle\int_{I^{2}}\log_{-}\big(N^{-1/2}+|a-b|\big)^{q}\mathfrak{H}_{0}^{N}(\mathrm{d}a)\mathfrak{H}_{0}^{N}(\mathrm{d}b)

\displaystyle=\int_{I}\bigg[\int_{I}\log_{-}\big(N^{-1/2}+|a-b|\big)^{q}\mathfrak{H}_{0}^{N}(\mathrm{d}a)\bigg]\mathfrak{H}_{0}^{N}(\mathrm{d}b)

\displaystyle\leq C\sup_{b}\int_{I}\log_{-}\big(N^{-1/2}+|a-b|\big)^{q}\mathfrak{H}_{0}^{N}(\mathrm{d}a)

\displaystyle\leq C_{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}},q}\cdot\sup_{b}\int_{I}(N^{-1/2}+|a-b|\big)^{-\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}}\mathfrak{H}_{0}^{N}(\mathrm{d}a),

where we used the fact that
\mathfrak{H}_{0}^{N}
has uniformly bounded total mass and
|\log x|^{q}\leq C_{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}},q}x^{-\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}}
for
|x|<1
. Now this last quantity is finite thanks to the good sequence condition of Definition
1.12
, using a similar dyadic decomposition idea as in the
d\geq 3
case, thus proving the uniform integrability in the case where
d=2
.
∎

5.3.
Important moment estimates for the QVF

So far in this section, we proved
limit formulas
that will be crucial to identify limit points later. However, we still need to prove
upper bound estimates
that will be important to obtain the tightness (i.e., existence of limit points) in the first place.

Proposition 5.11

(Estimates for moments of the increments of QVF in all dimensions)
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
T>0
, and
F:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
decreasing and of exponential decay at infinity. Let
f(x):=F(|x|)
. If the sequence of initial data
\mathfrak{H}^{N}(0,\bullet)
is a good sequence in the sense of Definition
1.12
, then we have that there exists
C=C(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{i.c.}},k,d,f)
such that

(5.22)

\displaystyle\sup_{N\geq 1}\mathbb{E}\big[(Q_{N}^{f}(t,\phi)-Q_{N}^{f}(s,\phi))^{k}\big]\leq C\|\phi\|^{k}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\cdot(t-s)^{k\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}}.

where
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}
is as in Definition
1.12
.

Proof.

Using the definition of
Q_{N}
together with the trivial bound
|\phi(N^{-1/2}(R^{j}(Nt_{j})-d_{N}t_{j}))|\leq\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}},
we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\big(Q_{N}^{f}(t,\phi)-Q_{N}^{f}(s,\phi)\big)^{k}]\leq\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{k}\mathbb{E}[\big(Q_{N}^{f}(t,\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}})-Q_{N}^{f}(s,\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}})\big)^{k}]

\displaystyle=N^{\frac{k(d-2)}{2}}k!\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{k}\sum_{Ns\leq r_{1}\leq...\leq r_{k}\leq Nt}g_{\mathrm{count}}(\vec{r})\mathbf{E}_{\nu_{N}^{\otimes 2k}}^{(0,2k)}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{k}\bigg\{f(R^{2j-1}_{r_{j}}-R^{2j}_{r_{j}})\prod_{i=0,1}C_{N,N^{-1}r_{j},N^{-1/2}(R^{2j-i}_{{r_{j}}}-N^{-1}d_{N}r_{j}),\nu_{N}}\bigg\}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq N^{\frac{k(d-2)}{2}}k!\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{k}\sum_{Ns\leq r_{1}\leq...\leq r_{k}\leq Nt}\mathbf{E}_{\nu_{N}^{\otimes 2k}}^{(0,2k)}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{k}\bigg\{f(R^{2j-1}_{r_{j}}-R^{2j}_{r_{j}})\prod_{i=0,1}C_{N,N^{-1}r_{j},N^{-1/2}(R^{2j-i}_{{r_{j}}}-N^{-1}d_{N}r_{j}),\nu_{N}}\bigg\}\bigg].

Fix
d\geq 2
, then we claim that there exists some
K>d
such that uniformly over
N\geq 1
and
t>s\geq 0
and
\mathbf{a}\in I^{2k},
one has the bound

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{a}}^{(0,2k)}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{k}\bigg\{f(R^{2j-1}_{r_{j}}-R^{2j}_{r_{j}})\prod_{i=0,1}

\displaystyle C_{N,N^{-1}r_{j},N^{-1/2}(R^{2j-i}_{{r_{j}}}-a_{j}-N^{-1}d_{N}r_{j})}\bigg\}\bigg]

(5.23)

\displaystyle\leq C\prod_{j=1}^{k}r_{j}^{-d/2}\big(1+r_{j}^{-1/2}|a_{2j-1}-a_{2j}|\big)^{-K}.

Here
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},...,a_{2k})
can be taken as large as desired,
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}
is as in Definition
1.12
, and
C
may depend on
\varepsilon,k,d,f,K
but not on
s,t,\mathbf{a},N
.

For
d=1
, we claim that the bound still holds with time averaging, that is, with
\sum_{1\leq r_{1}\leq...\leq r_{k}\leq r}
on both sides and ignoring the factor
\big(1+r_{j}^{-1/2}|a_{2j-1}-a_{2j}|\big)^{-K}
on the right side, which is unnecessary.

The remainder of the proof will consist of two separate parts: establishing (
5.23
) and then using it to prove the claim. First, we assume it is true and use that to establish the claim.

Proof of (
5.22
) assuming (
5.23
).
This is where Definition
1.12
will be crucial. Recall that
\nu^{\varsigma_{N}}_{N}(\mathrm{d}x):=e^{\varsigma_{N}\bullet x-\log\int_{I}e^{\varsigma_{N}\bullet a}\nu_{N}(\mathrm{d}a)}\nu_{N}(\mathrm{d}x)
are the tilted initial states, and recall (from e.g. (
1.11
)) that
\mathfrak{H}_{0}^{N}(N^{-1/2}\mathrm{d}x)=\nu_{N}^{\varsigma_{N}}(\mathrm{d}x)
. Use the second bound in the definition of good sequence (Definition
1.12
), taking
t:=u/N
, and we obtain that

\displaystyle(N/u)^{d/2}

\displaystyle\int_{I^{2}}\big(1+u^{-1/2}|x_{1}-x_{2}|\big)^{-K}(\nu_{N}^{\varsigma_{N}})^{\otimes 2}(\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle=(N/u)^{d/2}\int_{I^{2}}\big(1+(u/N)^{-1/2}|x_{1}-x_{2}|\big)^{-K}(\mathfrak{H}_{0}^{N})^{\otimes 2}(\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle\leq C(u/N)^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}-1}.

Applying this iteratively and using (
5.1
), we have that

\displaystyle N^{\frac{k(d-2)}{2}}

\displaystyle\sum_{Ns\leq r_{1}\leq...\leq r_{k}\leq Nt}\mathbf{E}_{\nu_{N}^{\otimes 2k}}^{(0,2k)}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{k}\bigg\{f(R^{2j-1}_{r_{j}}-R^{2j}_{r_{j}})\prod_{i=0,1}C_{N,N^{-1}r_{j},N^{-1/2}(R^{2j-i}_{{r_{j}}}-N^{-1}d_{N}r_{j}),\nu_{N}}\bigg\}\bigg]

\displaystyle=N^{\frac{k(d-2)}{2}}\sum_{Ns\leq r_{1}\leq...\leq r_{k}\leq Nt}(r_{1}\cdots r_{k})^{-d/2}\int_{I^{2k}}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\big(1+r_{j}^{-1/2}|a_{2j-1}-a_{2j}|\big)^{-K}(\nu_{N}^{\varsigma_{N}})^{\otimes 2k}(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{a})

\displaystyle\leq N^{-k}\sum_{Ns\leq r_{1}\leq...\leq r_{k}\leq Nt}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\bigg(\frac{r_{j}}{N}\bigg)^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}-1}

\displaystyle\leq C_{k,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}}\cdot(t-s)^{k\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}}.

The last bound follows by e.g. interpreting it as a Riemann sum and iteratively integrating. This is the desired result.

Proof of (
5.23
).
Take
d\geq 2
. We prove this bound by induction on
k
. The claim is vacuously true when
k=0
, let us assume that the above bound has been proved up to
k-1
where
k\in\mathbb{N}.
By conditioning on time
r_{1}
, using the Markov property, and applying the inductive hypothesis for the conditioned expression, it suffices to show that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{a}}^{(0,2k)}\bigg[f(R^{1}_{r_{1}}-R^{2}_{r_{1}})\prod_{j=1}^{2k}C_{N,N^{-1}r_{1},N^{-1/2}(R^{j}_{r_{1}}-a_{j}-N^{-1}d_{N}r_{1})}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{j=2}^{k}(r_{j}-r_{1})^{-d/2}\big(1+(r_{j}-r_{1})^{-1/2}|R^{2j-1}_{r_{1}}-R^{2j}_{r_{1}}|\big)^{-K}\bigg]\leq C\prod_{j=1}^{k}r_{j}^{-d/2}\big(1+r_{j}^{-1/2}|a_{2j-1}-a_{2j}|\big)^{-K},

where in the left side everything beyond time
r_{1}
has already been reduced to time
r_{1}
using the inductive hypothesis. Cancelling out like terms which are deterministic, we just need to prove that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{a}}^{(0,2k)}

\displaystyle\bigg[f(R^{1}_{r_{1}}-R^{2}_{r_{1}})\prod_{j=1}^{2k}C_{N,N^{-1}r_{1},N^{-1/2}(R^{j}_{r_{1}}-a_{j}-N^{-1}d_{N}r_{1})}\prod_{j=2}^{k}\big(1+(r_{j}-r_{1})^{-1/2}|R^{2j-1}_{r_{1}}-R^{2j}_{r_{1}}|\big)^{-K}\bigg]

(5.24)

\displaystyle\leq C\prod_{j=1}^{k}(r_{j}-r_{1})^{d/2}r_{j}^{-d/2}\big(1+r_{j}^{-1/2}|a_{2j-1}-a_{2j}|\big)^{-K}.

Now we will use the constants to rewrite the left side in terms of the tilted measures
\mathbf{P}^{(\beta,k)}_{\mathbf{x}}
of Definition
2.7
. Define
\mathcal{H}_{N}:I^{2k}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
by

(5.25)

\mathcal{H}_{N}(\mathbf{R}_{s-1}):=\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{R}_{s-1},(\varsigma_{N},\ldots\varsigma_{N});2k)=\log\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(0,2k)}\big[e^{\varsigma_{N}\bullet\sum_{j=1}^{2k}(R^{j}_{s}-R^{j}_{s-1})}\big|\mathcal{F}_{s-1}\big]\;-2k\log M(\varsigma_{N})

where by the Markov property the equality is true for all
\mathbf{x}\in I^{2k}
. Here
\mathcal{K}
is as defined in Definition
2.4
. Then using (
2.5
), the previous bound (
5.24
) can be rewritten in terms of the tilted measures
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}
of Definition
2.7
as

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{a}}^{(\varsigma_{N},2k)}

\displaystyle\bigg[f(R^{1}_{r_{1}}-R^{2}_{r_{1}})e^{\sum_{s=0}^{r_{1}-1}\mathcal{H}_{N}(\mathbf{R}_{s})}\times\prod_{j=2}^{k}\big(1+(r_{j}-r_{1})^{-1/2}|R^{2j-1}_{r_{1}}-R^{2j}_{r_{1}}|\big)^{-K}\bigg]

(5.26)

\displaystyle\leq C\prod_{j=1}^{k}(r_{j}-r_{1})^{d/2}r_{j}^{-d/2}\big(1+r_{j}^{-1/2}|a_{2j-1}-a_{2j}|\big)^{-K}.

This is what we wish to prove. We begin by Taylor expanding (with respect to
\varsigma_{N}
) the expression inside of the exponential and using the result of Lemma
2.4
one will obtain for each
t,N>0
that

\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}}|\mathcal{H}_{N}(\mathbf{x})|\leq C|\varsigma_{N}|^{2p}\sum_{1\leq i\leq j\leq k}f_{0}(R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r})

deterministically for some function
f_{0}:I\to\mathbb{R}
decaying to 0 exponentially fast at infinity. Now use the very crude bound
\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq 2k}a_{ij}\leq\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 2k}a_{ij}^{k(2k+1)}
(valid if
a_{ij}\geq 0
), we obtain

\displaystyle e^{C\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 2k}\sum_{s=1}^{r}|\varsigma_{N}|^{2p}f_{0}(R^{i}_{s}-R^{j}_{s})}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 2k}e^{Ck(2k+1)\sum_{s=1}^{r}|\varsigma_{N}|^{2p}f_{0}(R^{i}_{s}-R^{j}_{s})}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 2k}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}C^{m}|\varsigma_{N}|^{2pm}\sum_{s_{1}\leq...\leq s_{m}\leq r}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}f_{0}(R^{i}_{s_{\ell}}-R^{j}_{s_{\ell}}).

We Taylor expanded the exponential into powers in the last line, and canceled the
m!
coming from the ordering of coordinates. Thus to prove (
5.26
), we are now reduced to proving the bound for each
a<b

\displaystyle\sum_{s_{1}\leq...\leq s_{m}\leq r_{1}}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{a}}^{(\varsigma_{N},2k)}

\displaystyle\bigg[f(R^{1}_{r_{1}}-R^{2}_{r_{1}})\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}f_{0}(R^{a}_{s_{\ell}}-R^{b}_{s_{\ell}})\times\prod_{j=2}^{k}\big(1+(r_{j}-r_{1})^{-1/2}|R^{2j-1}_{r_{1}}-R^{2j}_{r_{1}}|\big)^{-K}\bigg]

(5.27)

\displaystyle\leq C^{m}\prod_{j=1}^{k}(r_{j}-r_{1})^{d/2}r_{j}^{-d/2}\big(1+r_{j}^{-1/2}|a_{2j-1}-a_{2j}|\big)^{-K}.

This can be shown directly using the anticoncentration estimate in Theorem
4.7
combined with the Markov property of the measures
\mathbf{P}^{(\beta,k)}_{\mathbf{a}}
. The key here is to iteratively apply the elementary convolution bound

r^{-d/2}s^{-d/2}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\big(1+r^{-1/2}|x-y|\big)^{-K}\big(1+s^{-1/2}|x|\big)^{-K}\leq C(r+s)^{-d/2}\cdot\big(1+(r+s)^{-1/2}|y|\big)^{-K},

with
C
independent of
r,s,y
. To prove this bound, take e.g.
K=d+1
. The bound follows immediately from the fact that the radially symmetric Cauchy distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is stable, i.e.,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}a^{-d}(1+a^{-1}|x|^{2})^{-\frac{d+1}{2}}\cdot b^{-d}(1+b^{-1}|x-y|^{2})^{-\frac{d+1}{2}}\mathrm{d}x=\frac{\pi^{d/2}\Gamma(\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(1+\frac{d}{2})}(a+b)^{-d}(1+(a+b)^{-1}|y|^{2})^{-\frac{d+1}{2}}

for all
a,b>0
and
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

For the
d=1
case, one simply notes that at every step, the bounds are still true after time-averaging by the final bound of Theorem
4.7
. ∎

Proposition 5.12

(Controlling the error terms)
.

Let
\mathcal{E}^{j}_{N},\mathcal{V}^{j}_{N}

(1\leq j\leq 2)
be as in Proposition
3.6
. Let
q\geq 1
be an even integer, and let
T>0
. Then there exists
C>0
and a function
f:I\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
|f(x)|\leq F(|x|)
where
F:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is decreasing and of exponential decay at infinity, such that uniformly over all
N\geq 1
and
s,t\in[0,T]\cap(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})
we have the following:

(1)

Regime A:
In the case where
|\varsigma_{N}|=O(N^{-1/2})
we have the bound

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1,2}\mathbb{E}[|\mathcal{V}^{j}_{N}(t,\phi)-\mathcal{V}^{j}_{N}(s,\phi)|^{q}]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq CN^{-1/2}\|\phi\|_{C^{p+2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\bigg(\mathbb{E}[|Q^{f}_{N}(t,\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}})-Q^{f}_{N}(s,\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}})|^{q}]^{1/q}+|t-s|\bigg).

The integer
p
appearing on the right side is the one from Assumption
1.2
.

(2)

Regime B:
In the case where
N^{-1/2}\ll|\varsigma_{N}|\ll\psi_{N}(p,d)N^{-1}
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1,2}\mathbb{E}[|\mathcal{E}^{j}_{N}(t,\phi)-\mathcal{E}^{j}_{N}(s,\phi)|^{q}]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq C|\varsigma_{N}|^{1/2}\|\phi\|_{C^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\bigg(\mathbb{E}[|Q^{f}_{N}(t,\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}})-Q^{f}_{N}(s,\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}})|^{q}]^{1/q}+|t-s|\bigg).

(3)

Regime C:
In the case where
d=2
and
(\log N)^{1/2p}\varsigma_{N}\to{\boldsymbol{v}}\neq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1,2}\mathbb{E}[|\mathcal{E}^{j}_{N}(t,\phi)-\mathcal{E}^{j}_{N}(s,\phi)|^{q}]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq C|\varsigma_{N}|^{1/2}\|\phi\|_{C^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\bigg(\mathbb{E}[|Q^{f}_{N}(t,\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}})-Q^{f}_{N}(s,\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}})|^{q}]^{1/q}+|t-s|\bigg).

(4)

Regime D:
In the case where
d\geq 3
and
\varsigma_{N}\to{\boldsymbol{v}}\neq 0
we have that

\mathbb{E}[|\mathcal{E}^{1}_{N}(t,\phi)-\mathcal{E}^{1}_{N}(s,\phi)|^{q}]^{1/q}\leq C|\varsigma_{N}-{\boldsymbol{v}}|\|\phi\|_{C^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\bigg(\mathbb{E}[|Q^{f}_{N}(t,\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}})-Q^{f}_{N}(s,\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}})|^{q}]^{1/q}+|t-s|\bigg).

Note that the second and third cases have the same error bounds; however, we distinguish these two cases because they will need to be analyzed separately later on.

We remark that these bounds show that the “error terms”
\mathcal{E}^{1},\mathcal{E}^{2},\mathcal{V}^{1},\mathcal{V}^{2}
in Lemma
3.6
vanish relative to
Q_{N}(t,\phi^{2})
, which will be a crucial observation in showing tightness and identifying limit points in Section
6
just below.

Proof.

Proof of (2) and (3).
Let us first deal with the
\mathcal{E}^{j}_{N}
(the second bullet point) which corresponds to the extremal regime but below the critical scale.
First consider
\mathcal{E}_{N}^{2}
. In the expression for
\mathcal{E}^{2}_{N}
given by (
3.12
), use Lemma
3.5
to obtain immediately that that

|\mathcal{E}^{2}_{N}(t,\phi)-\mathcal{E}^{2}_{N}(s,\phi)|\leq C|\varsigma_{N}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\phi\|_{C^{p+2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}|Q_{N}^{f}(t,\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}})-Q_{N}^{f}(s,\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}})|,

where
f(x):=\mathrm{F_{dec}}(x)^{1/(2p+2)}
which decays exponentially fast at infinity by Assumption
1.2
.

Finally let us bound
\mathcal{E}^{1}_{N}
. To do this we will need to work with
\mathcal{A}^{1}_{N}(\phi,y_{1},y_{2})
as defined just before (
3.11
). Let us perform a second-order Taylor expansion of
\phi
centered at
N^{-1/2}y_{1}
. Then the expression for
\mathcal{A}^{1}_{N}(\phi,y_{1},y_{2})
can be written as a sum
\mathcal{A}^{1,a}_{N}(\phi,y_{1},y_{2})+\mathcal{A}^{1,b}_{N}(\phi,y_{1},y_{2})
where

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{1,a}_{N}(\phi,y_{1},y_{2})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\#k_{1},\#k_{2}\leq 2\\
\#k_{1}+\#k_{2}>0\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{k_{1}!k_{2}!}N^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})/2}(\partial^{k_{1}}\phi)(N^{-1/2}y_{1})(\partial^{k_{2}}\phi)(N^{-1/2}y_{1})

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\cdot\int_{I^{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}e^{\varsigma_{N}\bullet(x_{j}-y_{j})-\log M(\varsigma_{N})}\cdot(x_{1}-y_{1})^{k_{1}}(x_{2}-y_{1})^{k_{2}}\rho\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{\mathrm{d}}x_{1},\mathrm{\mathrm{d}}x_{2})\big)

where the sum is over multi-indices
k_{1},k_{2}
, where
\#j=j_{1}+...+j_{d}
, and where
x^{j}=x_{1}^{j_{1}}\cdots x_{d}^{j_{d}}
and
j!=j_{1}!\cdots j_{d}!
for vectors
x=(x_{1},...,x_{d})
and multi-indices
j=(j_{1},...,j_{d})
. By Taylor’s theorem the other term
\mathcal{A}^{1,b}_{N}
is a “remainder” satisfying

|\mathcal{A}^{1,b}_{N}(\phi,y_{1},y_{2})|\leq N^{-3/2}\|\phi\|_{C^{3}}^{2}\int_{I^{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}e^{|\varsigma_{N}||x_{j}-y_{j}|-\log M(\varsigma_{N})}\cdot|x_{1}-y_{1}|^{3}|x_{2}-y_{2}|^{3}\big|\rho\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{\mathrm{d}}x_{1},\mathrm{\mathrm{d}}x_{2})\big)\big|.

As in (
3.11
) we can write
\mathcal{E}^{1}_{N}=\mathcal{E}^{1,a}_{N}+\mathcal{E}^{1,b}_{N}
corresponding respectively to the contributions of
\mathcal{A}^{1,a}_{N}
and
\mathcal{A}^{1,b}_{N}
respectively.

The bound for
\mathcal{E}^{1,b}_{N}
is straightforward: since the power of
N
is
-3/2
, and since the number of summands in the expression for
\mathcal{E}^{1,b}_{N}
is
N(t-s)
, we can use brutal absolute value bounds to obtain the desired estimate using arguments very similar to the bound for
\mathcal{E}_{N}^{2}
above. By Lemma
2.9
, the integral is bounded independently of
y_{1},y_{2},N
.

Let us discuss the bound for
\mathcal{E}^{1,a}_{N}
. In the expression for
\mathcal{A}^{1,a}_{N}
, each of the terms
(x_{2}-y_{1})^{k_{2}}
can be further expanded out into a sum of terms of the form
(x_{2}-y_{2})^{i}(y_{1}-y_{2})^{k_{2}-i}
where
0\leq\#i\leq\#k_{2}.
Ultimately
\mathcal{A}^{1,a}_{N}
will be written as a sum of terms of the form

\displaystyle(y_{1}-y_{2})^{k_{2}-i}N^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})/2}

\displaystyle(\partial^{k_{1}}\phi)(N^{-1/2}y_{1})\cdot(\partial^{k_{2}}\phi)(N^{-1/2}y_{1})

\displaystyle\cdot\int_{I^{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}e^{\varsigma_{N}\bullet(x_{j}-y_{j})-\log M(\varsigma_{N})}\cdot(x_{1}-y_{1})^{k_{1}}(x_{2}-y_{2})^{i}\rho\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{\mathrm{d}}x_{1},\mathrm{\mathrm{d}}x_{2})\big)

where
0\leq k_{1}\leq 2
and
0\leq i\leq k_{2}\leq 2
.
Assuming that
\phi
is supported on
[-J,J]
we can then take the absolute value to bound the last expression by

\displaystyle|y_{1}-y_{2}|^{k_{2}-i}N^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})/2}

\displaystyle\|\phi\|_{C^{2}}^{2}\mathbf{1}_{[-J,J]}(N^{-1/2}y_{1})

\displaystyle\cdot\bigg|\int_{I^{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}e^{\varsigma_{N}\bullet(x_{j}-y_{j})-\log M(\varsigma_{N})}\cdot(x_{1}-y_{1})^{k_{1}}(x_{2}-y_{2})^{i}\rho\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{\mathrm{d}}x_{1},\mathrm{\mathrm{d}}x_{2})\big)\bigg|

At this point, apply a Taylor expansion of the quantity
\varsigma_{N}\mapsto\prod_{j=1}^{2}e^{\varsigma_{N}\bullet(x_{j}-y_{j})-\log M(\varsigma_{N})}
, then use Lemma
2.4
and the decay assumptions on
\mathrm{F_{dec}}
, and (using e.g. Lemma
A.2
and the uniform moment bounds of Lemma
2.9
) we will see that the absolute value of the integral is bounded above by
\mathrm{F_{dec}}(|y_{1}-y_{2}|)^{1/2}\cdot|\varsigma_{N}|^{2p-\#k_{2}-\#i}.
By considering all of the different cases of
(k_{1},k_{2},i)
, one verifies that the decay conditions on the function
\mathrm{F_{dec}}
in Item (
5
) of Assumption (
1.2
) are strong enough so that one may find
f
as in the proposition statement to bound
\mathcal{E}^{1,a}_{N}
.

Proof of (1).
The bounds for
\mathcal{V}^{1},\mathcal{V}^{2}
in Regime A are done in an extremely similar fashion, using the Taylor expansions of
\phi
in the
y_{i}
-variable as well as the Taylor expansions of the exponential in the
\varsigma_{N}
variable to bound things correctly. One instead uses Lemma
3.4
rather than
3.5
, and one replaces
\phi
by
\partial^{k}\phi
with
\#k=p
where appropriate, but the remaining details are extremely similar.

Proof of (4).
The final bullet point is also very similar to the proof of the second bullet point, noting here that
v\mapsto\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{v}
is a smooth function, more precisely since
\varsigma_{N}\to{\boldsymbol{v}}
we have
|\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{\varsigma_{N}}-\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{v}|\leq|\varsigma_{N}-v|e^{C\sum_{j=1,2}|y_{j}-x_{j}|}
where
C
is independent of
N
. Then an application of Lemma
2.9
will allow to absorb the integral over
I^{2}
into a constant and yield the desired bound as above while dealing with.
∎

Corollary 5.13

(Predictable quadratic variation bound)
.

Let
q
be a positive even integer, and let
T>0
. Let
M^{N}(\phi)
be the martingale defined in (
3.4
), and let
\langle M^{N}(\phi)\rangle
denote its predictable quadratic variation as in (
3.5
). Then there exists
C>0
and exists
C>0
and a function
f:I\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
|f(x)|\leq F(|x|)
where
F:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is decreasing and of exponential decay at infinity, such that we have the bound uniformly over all
N\geq 1
, all
s,t\in[0,T]\cap(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}),
and all
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
supported on
[-J,J]

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\big|\langle M^{N}(\phi)\rangle_{t}-\langle M^{N}(\phi)\rangle_{s}\big|^{q}\big]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\phi\|_{C^{p+2}}^{2}|\varsigma_{N}-v|^{1/2}\bigg(\mathbb{E}[|Q^{f}_{N}(t,\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}})-Q^{f}_{N}(s,\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}})|^{q}]^{1/q}+|t-s|\bigg)

In particular, if the sequence of initial data
\mathfrak{H}^{N}(0,\bullet)
is a good sequence in the sense of Definition
1.12
, then we have that the above quantity is bounded above by
C\|\phi\|_{C^{p+2}}^{2}|t-s|^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}},
with
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}
as in Theorem
1.12
.

Proof.

This is immediate from Propositions
3.6
and
5.12
. The last sentence then follows from (
5.22
). ∎

Proposition 5.14

(Martingale tightness bound)
.

Fix
q\geq 1
and

T>0
. For all
s,t\in(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\cap[0,T]
and all
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
we have that

\mathbb{E}\big[\big|M_{t}^{N}(\phi)-M_{s}^{N}(\phi)\big|^{q}\big]^{1/q}\leq C\|\phi\|_{C^{p+2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}|t-s|^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}}.

Here
C
is independent of
N,\phi,s,t
. Moreover
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}
is as in Definition
1.12
.

Proof.

By
[
HH14
, Theorem 2.11]
, we have the Burkholder-type bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\big|M_{t}^{N}(\phi)-M_{s}^{N}(\phi)\big|^{q}\big]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\mathbb{E}\big[\big(\langle M^{N}(\phi)\rangle_{t}-\langle M^{N}(\phi)\rangle_{s}\big)^{q/2}]^{1/q}+\mathbb{E}\big[\sup_{u\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}\cap[s,t]}|M_{u+N^{-1}}(\phi)-M_{u}(\phi)|^{q}\big]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\phi\|_{C^{p+2}}\cdot|t-s|^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}}+\mathbb{E}\big[\sup_{u\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}\cap[s,t]}|M_{u+N^{-1}}(\phi)-M_{u}(\phi)|^{q}\big]^{1/q}.

Recall from (
3.2
) that

\displaystyle(M^{N}_{t+N^{-1}}(\phi)-M^{N}_{t}(\phi))^{2}=\mathscr{B}_{N}^{2}\bigg(\int_{I}\int_{I}\phi(N^{-1/2}x)e^{\varsigma_{N}\bullet(x-y)-\log M(\varsigma_{N})}\big[K_{r+1}(y,\mathrm{\mathrm{d}}x)-\mu(\mathrm{\mathrm{d}}x-y)\big]Z_{N}(r,\mathrm{d}y)\bigg)^{2}

It suffices to prove the claim when
q=2k
for some positive integer
k
. We have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\sup_{u\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}\cap[s,t]}|M_{u+N^{-1}}(\phi)-M_{u}(\phi)|^{2k}\big]^{1/{2k}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum_{r=Ns}^{Nt}|M_{N^{-1}(r+1)}(\phi)-M_{N^{-1}r}(\phi)|^{2k}\bigg]^{1/{2k}}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\sum_{r=Ns}^{Nt}\mathbb{E}\big[|M_{N^{-1}(r+1)}(\phi)-M_{N^{-1}r}(\phi)|^{2k}\big]\bigg)^{1/{2k}}

For
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
let us now define the signed (random) measure
H_{r+1}(y,\mathrm{d}x):=K_{r+1}(y,\mathrm{\mathrm{d}}x)-\mu(\mathrm{\mathrm{d}}x-y).
Note by Assumption
1.2
Item (
3
) that
\int_{I}(y-x)^{j}H_{r+1}(y,\mathrm{d}x)=0
a.s. for all multi-indices
j
with
0\leq\#j\leq p-1
.

Recall from (
3.2
) that

\displaystyle(M^{N}_{t+N^{-1}}(\phi)-M^{N}_{t}(\phi))^{2k}=\mathscr{B}_{N}^{2k}\bigg(\int_{I}\int_{I}\phi(N^{-1/2}x)e^{\varsigma_{N}\bullet(x-y)-\log M(\varsigma_{N})}H_{r+1}(y,\mathrm{d}x)Z_{N}(r,\mathrm{d}y)\bigg)^{2k}

Then define the signed (random) measure on
I^{2}

\Phi_{r+1}\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{d}x_{1},\mathrm{d}x_{2})\big):=H_{r+1}(y_{1},\mathrm{d}x_{1})H_{r+1}(y_{2},\mathrm{d}x_{2}).

Let us therefore define a shorthand notation for the inner integral:

(5.28)

\mathpzc A_{N}(r,\phi,y_{1},y_{2}):=\int_{I^{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}\phi(N^{-1/2}x_{j})e^{\varsigma_{N}\bullet(x_{j}-y_{j})-\log M(\varsigma_{N})}\Phi_{r+1}\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{d}x_{1},\mathrm{d}x_{2})\big).

To prove the desired bound, we will proceed similarly to previous propositions by first Taylor expanding
\prod_{j=1}^{2}\phi(N^{-1/2}x_{j})
around the point
(y_{1},y_{2})
so we get that
\mathpzc A_{N}(r,\phi,y_{1},y_{2})
can be written as a sum
\mathpzc A^{a}_{N}(r,\phi,y_{1},y_{2})+\mathpzc A^{b}_{N}(r,\phi,y_{1},y_{2})
where

\displaystyle\mathpzc A^{a}_{N}(r,\phi,y_{1},y_{2})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\#k_{1},\#k_{2}\leq kd\\
\#k_{1}+\#k_{2}>0\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{k_{1}!k_{2}!}N^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})/2}(\partial^{k_{1}}\phi)(N^{-1/2}y_{1})(\partial^{k_{2}}\phi)(N^{-1/2}y_{1})

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\cdot\int_{I^{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}e^{\varsigma_{N}\bullet(x_{j}-y_{j})-\log M(\varsigma_{N})}\cdot(x_{1}-y_{1})^{k_{1}}(x_{2}-y_{1})^{k_{2}}\Phi_{r+1}\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{\mathrm{d}}x_{1},\mathrm{\mathrm{d}}x_{2})\big)

where the sum is over multi-indices
k_{1},k_{2}
, where
\#j=j_{1}+...+j_{d}
, and where
x^{j}=x_{1}^{j_{1}}\cdots x_{d}^{j_{d}}
and
j!=j_{1}!\cdots j_{d}!
for vectors
x=(x_{1},...,x_{d})
and multi-indices
j=(j_{1},...,j_{d})
. By Taylor’s theorem the other term
\mathpzc A^{b}_{N}
is a “remainder” satisfying

|\mathpzc A^{b}_{N}(r,\phi,y_{1},y_{2})|\leq N^{-\frac{kd+1}{2}}\|\phi\|_{C^{3}}^{2}\int_{I^{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}e^{|\varsigma_{N}||x_{j}-y_{j}|-\log M(\varsigma_{N})}\cdot|x_{1}-y_{1}|^{kd+1}|x_{2}-y_{2}|^{kd+1}\big|\Phi_{r+1}\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{\mathrm{d}}x_{1},\mathrm{\mathrm{d}}x_{2})\big)\big|.

Thus we obtain that for
r:=Nt
one has

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(M^{N}_{t+N^{-1}}(\phi)-M_{t}^{N}(\phi))^{2k}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(\mathscr{B}_{N}^{2}\int_{I^{2}}\mathpzc A_{N}(r,\phi,y_{1},y_{2})\prod_{j=1,2}Z_{N}(r,\mathrm{d}y_{j})\bigg)^{k}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathscr{B}_{N}^{2k}\int_{I^{2k}}\mathbb{E}[\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}\mathpzc A_{N}(r,\phi,y_{2j+1},y_{2j+2})]\;\mathbb{E}[\prod_{j=1}^{2k}Z_{N}(r,\mathrm{d}y_{j})]

where in the last line we used the independence of
\mathpzc A_{N}(r,\bullet)
and
Z_{N}(r,\bullet)
to split the expectations.

Now we have explicitly that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\prod_{j=1}^{2k}Z_{N}(r,\mathrm{d}y_{j})]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}^{(\varsigma_{N},2k)}_{(\nu_{N}^{\varsigma_{N}})^{\otimes 4k}}[e^{\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathcal{H}_{N}(\mathbf{R}_{s})}\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{R}_{r}\in\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}\}}],

where
\mathcal{H}_{N}
is exactly as in (
5.25
). Meanwhile we have a bound coming from Assumption
1.2
which says that :

\bigg\|\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}\Phi_{r+1}(y_{2j-1},y_{2j},\bullet)\bigg]\bigg\|_{TV}\leq C\mathrm{F_{dec}}(\max_{i<j}|y_{i}-y_{j}|).

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle[\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}\mathpzc A_{N}(r,\phi,y_{2j+1},y_{2j+2})|\leq C|\varsigma_{N}|^{2kp}\mathrm{F_{dec}}(\max_{i<j}|y_{i}-y_{j}|)^{1/2}.

Note here the max as opposed to the min which usually appears, which appears by considering all possible partitions in Assumption
1.2
Item (
5
) and taking the mimimum. Noting that
\mathscr{B}_{N}^{2k}\cdot|\sigma_{N}|^{2kp}\leq CN^{\frac{1}{2}k(d-2)},
we thus see that

\displaystyle\mathscr{B}_{N}^{2k}

\displaystyle\int_{I^{2k}}\mathbb{E}[\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}\mathpzc A_{N}^{a}(r,\phi,y_{2j+1},y_{2j+2})]\;\mathbb{E}[\prod_{j=1}^{2k}Z_{N}(r,\mathrm{d}y_{j})]

\displaystyle\leq CN^{\frac{1}{2}k(d-2)}\mathbf{E}^{(\varsigma_{N},2k)}_{(\nu_{N}^{\varsigma_{N}})^{\otimes 2k}}[e^{\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathcal{H}_{N}(\mathbf{R}_{s})}\mathrm{F_{dec}}(\max_{i<j}|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}|)^{1/2}].

These are the worst terms, as products with “
b
” indices will be similar but have extra factors of
N^{-\frac{kd+1}{2}}
which may be bounded brutally, enough to counteract the growth of
\mathscr{B}_{N}^{2k}
. As
\mathrm{F_{dec}}
has exponential decay at infinity, one has the bound which can be derived from Theorem
4.7
:

\mathbf{E}^{(\varsigma_{N},2k)}_{\mathbf{a}}[e^{\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathcal{H}_{N}(\mathbf{R}_{s})}\mathrm{F_{dec}}(\max_{i<j}|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}|)^{1/2}]\leq Cr^{-(2k-1)d/2}\big(1+r^{-1/2}\max_{i<j}|a_{i}-a_{j}|))^{-K}.

Integrating that over the initial conditions, fibering
I^{2k}
over
I^{2k-1}
by the subspace
\{(a,...,a):a\in I\}
, and using the second bound in Definition
1.12
we find that

(r/N)^{-(2k-1)d/2}\int_{I^{2k}}\big(1+r^{-1/2}\max_{i<j}|a_{i}-a_{j}|))^{-K}(\nu_{N}^{\varsigma_{N}})^{\otimes 2k}(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{a})\leq C(r/N)^{(2k-1)(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}-1)}.

Summarizing, we find that

\displaystyle\sum_{r=Ns}^{Nt-1}\mathbb{E}[(M^{N}_{t+N^{-1}}(\phi)-M_{t}^{N}(\phi))^{2k}]

\displaystyle\leq N^{\frac{1}{2}k(d-2)-(2k-1)(\frac{d}{2}-1+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}})}\sum_{r=Ns}^{Nt}r^{(2k-1)(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}-1)}

Note that for
k=1
, this bound does recover the one for the predictable variation from earlier. Without loss of generality,
k
is so large that
(2k-1)(1-\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}})>1.
Thus the whole sum is bounded above by
N^{(k-1)(1-\frac{d}{2}-2\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}})-\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}}
which is upper bounded by
|t-s|^{(k-1)(\frac{d}{2}+2\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}-1)}
as long as
t\neq s
(since
|t-s|\geq N^{-1})
. Taking the
2k
th root gives an upper bound of
|t-s|^{(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2k})(\frac{d}{2}+2\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}-1)},
which is enough (recalling that
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}=\frac{1}{2}
in
d=1
). ∎

6.
Tightness and identification of limit points

In this section we will finally prove our main results (Theorems
1.13
,
1.15
,
1.18
,
1.20
, and
1.21
) using the estimates of the previous section. The main idea is as follows. We want to study the field
\mathfrak{F}_{N}
appearing in the main results. In Section
4
, we derived a relation which approximately says “
(\partial_{t}-\frac{1}{2}\Delta)\mathfrak{F}_{N}(t,x)=M^{N}(t,x)
” for some martingale field
M^{N}.
In Section
5
we proved very strong bounds and limit formulas for the quadratic variations
Q_{N}
of the martingales
M^{N}
. Those estimates will be used in this section to show that as
N\to\infty
, this random process
Q_{N}
converges to a deterministic limit. Thus as
N\to\infty
, by the Dubins-Schwarz Theorem, the
M^{N}
must converge to some Gaussian limit with explicit covariance since their quadratic variation is deterministic. Since
\mathfrak{F}_{N}
and
M^{N}
are related by a linear map, this will imply that
\mathfrak{F}_{N}
is also converging to a Gaussian limit, and the covariance operator will be clear since the limit of the quadratic variations
Q_{N}
in every regime is explicit from Section
5
. This method for studying the density profile in RWRE models was introduced by us in our previous works
[
DDP24a
,
DDP24b
,
Par24
]
and was also used recently by
[
KLM25
]
to study the bulk regime of a Kraichnan-type model (Example
1.6
). An important goal of this paper is to illustrate that the same approach works in every regime up to the critical scale.

6.1.
Weighted Hölder spaces and Schauder estimates

We now introduce various natural topologies for our field
\mathfrak{F}_{N}
and its limit points. We then discuss how the heat flow affects these topologies and record Kolmogorov-type lemmas that will be key in showing tightness under these topologies. We begin by recalling many familiar and useful spaces of continuous and differentiable functions that have natural metric structures.

For
d\geq 1
, we denote by
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the space of all compactly supported smooth functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For a smooth function on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we define its
C^{r}
norm as

\displaystyle\|f\|_{C^{r}}:=\sum_{\#k\leq r}\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|D^{k}f(\mathbf{x})|

where the sum is over all multi-indices
k=(k_{1},...,k_{d})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{d}
with
\#k=\sum_{i=1}^{d}k_{i}\leq r
and
D^{{k}}:=\partial_{x_{1}}^{k_{1}}\cdots\partial_{x_{d}}^{k_{d}}
denotes the mixed partial derivative.

We now recall the definition of weighted Hölder spaces from
[
HL15
, Definitions 2.2 and 2.3]
. For the remainder of this paper, we shall work with
elliptic and parabolic
weighted Hölder spaces with polynomial weight function

\displaystyle w(x):=(1+x^{2})^{\tau}

for some fixed
\tau>1
. We introduce these weights because weighted spaces will be more convenient to obtain tightness estimates. We expect that it is possible to remove the weights throughout this section, but this would require more precise moment estimates than the ones we derived in previous sections, which take into account spatial decay of the fields.

Definition 6.1

(Elliptic Hölder spaces)
.

For
\alpha\in(0,1)
we define the space
C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to be the completion of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
with respect to the norm given by

\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|f(x)|}{w(x)}+\sup_{|x-y|\leq 1}\frac{|f(x)-f(y)|}{w(x)|x-y|^{\alpha}}.

For
\alpha<0
we let
r=-\lfloor\alpha\rfloor
and we define
C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to be the completion of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
with respect to the norm

\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\sup_{\lambda\in(0,1]}\sup_{\phi\in B_{r}}\frac{(f,S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}}{w(x)\lambda^{\alpha}}

where the scaling operators
S^{\lambda}_{x}
are defined by

(6.1)

\displaystyle S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi(y)=\lambda^{-d}\phi(\lambda^{-1}(x-y)),

and where
B_{r}
is the set of all smooth functions of
C^{r}
norm less than 1 with support contained in the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

One may verify that these spaces embed continuously into
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Definition 6.2

(Function spaces)
.

Let
C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be as in Definition
6.1
. We define
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
to be the space of continuous maps
H:[0,T]\to C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),
equipped with the norm

\|g\|_{C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}:=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|g(t)\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Definition 6.3
.

Here and henceforth we will define
\Psi_{[a,b]}:=[a,b]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\Psi_{T}:=\Psi_{[0,T]}.

Definition 6.4

(Parabolic Hölder spaces)
.

We define
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Psi_{T})
to be the set of functions on
\Psi_{T}
that are restrictions to
\Psi_{T}
of some function in
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})
, in particular we do not impose that elements of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Psi_{T})
vanish at the boundaries of
\Psi_{T}.

For
\alpha\in(0,1)
we define the space
C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})
to be the completion of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Psi_{T})
with respect to the norm

\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})}:=\sup_{(t,x)\in\Psi_{T}}\frac{|f(t,x)|}{w(x)}+\sup_{|s-t|^{1/2}+|x-y|\leq 1}\frac{|f(t,x)-f(s,y)|}{w(x)(|t-s|^{1/2}+|x-y|)^{\alpha}}.

For
\alpha<0
we let
r=-\lfloor\alpha\rfloor
and we define
C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})
to be the completion of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Psi_{T})
with respect to the norm

\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})}:=\sup_{(t,x)\in\Psi_{T}}\sup_{\lambda\in(0,1]}\sup_{\varphi\in B_{r}}\frac{(f,S^{\lambda}_{(t,x)}\varphi)_{L^{2}(\Psi_{T})}}{w(x)\lambda^{\alpha}}

where the scaling operators are defined by

(6.2)

\displaystyle S^{\lambda}_{(t,x)}\varphi(s,y)=\lambda^{-d-2}\varphi(\lambda^{-2}(t-s),\lambda^{-1}(x-y)),

and where
B_{r}
is the set of all smooth functions of
C^{r}
norm less than 1 with support contained in the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
.

So far we have used
\phi,\psi
for test functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. To make the distinction clear between test functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
, we shall use variant Greek letters such as
\varphi,\vartheta,\varrho
for test functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
. In many instances below, we will explicitly write
(f,\varphi)_{\mathrm{para}}
or
(g,\phi)_{\mathrm{ell}}
when we want to be clear about the space in which we are applying the
L^{2}
-pairing (parabolic vs. elliptic).

An important property of both the parabolic and elliptic spaces is that one has a continuous embedding
C^{\alpha,\tau}\hookrightarrow C^{\beta,\tau}
whenever
\beta<\alpha.
In fact this embedding is compact, though we will not use this. We also have the following embedding of function spaces inside parabolic spaces.

Lemma 6.5
.

For
\alpha<0,\tau>0
one has a continuous embedding
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\hookrightarrow C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})
given by identifying
v=(v(t))_{t\in[0,T]}
with the tempered space-time distribution given by

(v,\varphi)_{\mathrm{para}}=\int_{0}^{T}(v(t),\varphi(t,\cdot))_{\mathrm{ell}}dt.

The proof is straightforward from the definitions and is omitted.

Remark 6.6

(Derivatives of distributions)
.

Let
\alpha<0
. By definition, any element
f\in C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha,\tau}(\Psi_{T})
admits an
L^{2}
-pairing with any smooth function
\varphi:\Psi_{T}\to\mathbb{R}
of rapid decay (i.e.,
\varphi
is a Schwarz function restricted to
\Psi_{T}
). We will write this pairing as
(f,\varphi)_{\Psi_{T}}.
Consequently there is a natural embedding
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha,\tau}(\Psi_{T})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})
which is defined by formally setting
f
to be zero outside of
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
. More rigorously, this means that the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})
-pairing of
f
with any
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})
is defined to be equal to
(f,\varphi|_{\Psi_{T}})_{\Psi_{T}}
.

The image of this embedding consists of some specific collection of tempered distributions that are necessarily supported on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}.
Thus we can sensibly define
\partial_{t}f
and
\partial_{x_{j}}f
as elements of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})
whenever
f\in C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha,\tau}(\Psi_{T})
, by the formulas

(\partial_{t}f,\varphi)_{\Psi_{T}}:=-(f,\partial_{t}\varphi)_{\Psi_{T}},\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;(\partial_{x_{j}}f,\varphi)_{\Psi_{T}}:=-(f,\partial_{x_{j}}\varphi)_{\Psi_{T}}

for all smooth
\varphi:\Psi_{T}\to\mathbb{R}
of rapid decay. This convention on derivatives will be useful for certain computations later. From the definitions, when
\alpha<0
one can check that for such
f
one necessarily has
\partial_{t}f\in C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-2,\tau}(\Psi_{T})
and
\partial_{x}f\in C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-1,\tau}(\Psi_{T}).

The latter statement fails for
\alpha>0
. Indeed by our convention of derivatives,
\partial_{t}f
may no longer be a smooth function (or even a function) even if
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Psi_{T})
. This is because such an
f
gets extended to all of
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
by setting it to be zero outside
\Psi_{T}
. In particular, if
f
does not vanish on the boundary of
\Psi_{T}
, then it may become a discontinuous function under our convention of extension to
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
. Due to these discontinuities, the distributional derivative
\partial_{t}f
can be a tempered distribution with singular parts along the boundaries. In our later computations, we never take derivatives of functions in
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha,\tau}(\Psi_{T})
with
\alpha>0
. Sometimes we will write
\partial_{s}
to mean the same thing as
\partial_{t}
.

We end this subsection by recording a Kolmogorov-type lemma for the three spaces introduced at the beginning of this subsection. It will be crucial in proving tightness in those respective spaces.

Lemma 6.7

(Kolmogorov continuity lemma for distributions)
.

Let
(t,\phi)\mapsto V(t,\phi)
be a map from
[0,T]\times\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
into
L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
which is linear and continuous in
\phi
. Fix a non-negative integer
r
. Assume there exists some
\kappa>0,q>d/\kappa
and
\alpha<-r
and
C=C(\kappa,\alpha,q,{T})>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}[|V(t,S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi)|^{q}]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq C\lambda^{\alpha},

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}[|V(t,S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi)-V(s,S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi)|^{q}]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq C\lambda^{\alpha-\kappa}|t-s|^{\kappa},

uniformly over all smooth functions
\phi
on
\mathbb{R}
supported on the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}
with
\|\phi\|_{C^{r}}\leq 1
, and uniformly over
\lambda\in(0,1]
and
0\leq s,t\leq T
. Then for any
\tau>1
and any
\beta<\alpha-\kappa
there exists a random variable
\big(\mathscr{V}(t)\big)_{t\in[0,T]}
taking values in
C([0,T],C^{\beta,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
such that
(\mathscr{V}(t),\phi)=V(t,\phi)
almost surely for all
\phi
and
t
. Furthermore, one has that

\mathbf{E}[\|\mathscr{V}\|^{q}_{C([0,T],C^{\beta,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}]\leq C^{\prime},

where
C^{\prime}
depends on the choice of
\alpha,\beta,q,\kappa,
and the constant
C
appearing in the moment bound above but not on
V,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}
.

A proof may be adapted from the proof of Lemma 9 in Section 5 of
[
MW17
]
.

6.2.
Tightness

Throughout this section, we are going to fix a terminal time
T>0
. Let
\mu
and
z_{0}
be as in Assumption
1.2
Item (
3
). Recall that we denote

\mu^{\varsigma}(\mathrm{d}x):=e^{\varsigma x-\log M(\varsigma)}\mu(\mathrm{d}x),

where
\mu
as always is the annealed one-step measure of Assumption
1.2
. We claim that for any
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
and
|\varsigma|<z_{0},
the spatial convolution
g:=f*\mu^{\varsigma}
given by

(6.3)

(g,\phi):=(f,\phi*\mu^{\varsigma}),\;\;\;\;\;\;\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

is well-defined as another element of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. To prove this, we must show that
\phi\mapsto\phi*\mu^{\varsigma}
is a well-defined and continuous map from
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
into itself. By taking the Fourier transform (which is a linear isomorphism on
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
), it suffices to show that the multiplication operator
T_{\varsigma}\psi(\xi)=\widehat{\mu^{\varsigma}}(\xi)\psi(\xi)
is a continuous linear operator from
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. But this is clear from the fact that the Fourier transform
\widehat{\mu^{\varsigma}}
(i.e., the “characteristic function” of the measure
\mu^{\varsigma}
) is smooth with all derivatives bounded, since the measure
\mu^{\varsigma}
has exponentially decaying tails.

Likewise if
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})
and one would like to define a tempered distribution
g\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that formally one has
g(t,x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(t,x-y)\mu^{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}y),
the procedure is completely analogous, defining it via the Fourier transforms
\widehat{g}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}):=\widehat{\mu^{\lambda}}(\xi_{2})\widehat{f}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}).

Definition 6.8
.

Define

\Psi_{N,T}:=\{(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}:(Nt,N^{1/2}x)\in\mathbb{Z}\times I\}.

Also recall the discrete lattice

\Lambda_{N}:=\{(t,x)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}:x+tN^{-1}d_{N}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\times I\}

that was used in Section
3
.

Definition 6.9
.

For
(s,y)\in\mathbb{Z}\times I
define
p_{N}(s,dy)
to be the transition density at time
s
and position
y
of a random walker on
\Lambda_{N}
with increment distribution
\mu^{\varsigma_{N}}.
Define the (macroscopic) linear operators
D_{N},L_{N},K_{N}
on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})

by

\displaystyle D_{N}f(t,x)

\displaystyle=N\big[f(t+N^{-1},x)-f(t,x)\big],

\displaystyle L_{N}f(t,x)

\displaystyle=N\bigg[f(t+N^{-1},x-N^{-1}d_{N})-\int_{I}f(t,x-N^{-1/2}y)\mu^{\varsigma_{N}}(\mathrm{d}y)\bigg],

\displaystyle K_{N}f(t,x)

\displaystyle=N^{-1}\sum_{s\in[0,T]\cap(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})}\int_{I}f(t-s,x-N^{-1/2}y)p_{N}(Ns,\mathrm{d}y).

These equalities should be understood by integration against smooth functions
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})
, and the convolutions need to be understood using the explanation after (
6.3
).

L_{N}
is a diffusively rescaled version of the microscopic heat operator
\mathcal{L}_{N}
from Section
3
, but which acts on tempered distributions rather than measures on
\mathbb{Z}\times I
. Indeed if
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})
then a second-order Taylor expansion shows that
L_{N}\varphi
converges in
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})
as
N\to\infty
to
(\partial_{t}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2})f
, i.e.,
L_{N}
approaches the continuum heat operator. Then
K_{N}
is the inverse operator to
L_{N}
, in the following sense.

Lemma 6.10
.

L_{N}K_{N}f=K_{N}L_{N}f=f
whenever
f
is a tempered distribution supported on
[a,b]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
b-a<T+1
.

Proof.

Note that for each
N
the operators
K_{N},L_{N}
are continuous on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})
, as may be verified using the Fourier transform as explained after (
6.3
). Therefore it suffices to prove the claim for all smooth functions
f
that have compact support contained in
(a,b)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, since these are dense in the subset of distributions supported on
[a,b]\times\mathbb{R}
, with respect to the topology of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})
. The smooth analogue is true by direct calculations, since
p_{N}(s,\mathrm{d}y)
is the kernel for the inverse operator to the discrete heat operator
\mathcal{L}_{N}
introduced in Section
3
.
∎

Recall the fields
M^{N}_{t}(\phi)
and
Q^{f}_{N}(t,\phi)
as defined in (
3.4
) and (
3.13
) respectively. We can now view these as random elements of
C([0,T+1],\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}))
by defining these fields by linear interpolation for
t\notin N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

For any
T
, note that
C([0,T],\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
embeds naturally into the linear subspace of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})
consisting of distributions supported on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, thus we can make sense of
D_{N}f,L_{N}f,K_{N}f
for all
f\in C([0,T],\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),
and these will be elements of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})
in general.

Definition 6.11
.

We will say that two tempered distributions
f,g\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})
agree on
[0,T]
if there exists
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}>0
such that
(f,\varphi)=(g,\varphi)
for all
\varphi
supported on
[-\varepsilon,T+\varepsilon]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Definition 6.12
.

Sample the environment
\omega
and then define a collection of coefficients
a_{N}(s,\mathrm{d}y)
as
a_{N}(0,\mathrm{d}y)=\nu_{N}
and for
s\geq 1
,

a_{N}(s,A)=\int_{I}p_{N}(s,A-y)\;\;(\nu_{N}^{\varsigma_{N}})(\mathrm{d}x_{0}),

where
\nu_{N}^{\varsigma_{N}}
is the
\varsigma_{N}
-tilted initial state
(\nu_{N}^{\varsigma_{N}})(\mathrm{d}x):=e^{\varsigma_{N}\bullet x-\log\int_{I}e^{\varsigma_{N}\bullet a}\nu_{N}(\mathrm{d}a)}\nu_{N}(\mathrm{d}x)
and
p_{N}
is the same random walk kernel from Definition
6.9
. Then define the distribution
\mathfrak{G}^{N}\in C([0,T+1],\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
for
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
by

\mathfrak{G}^{N}(t,\phi):=\int_{I}\phi(N^{-1/2}y)a_{N}(Nt,\mathrm{d}y)=\mathbb{E}[\mathfrak{H}^{N}(t,\phi)],

and linearly interpolated for
t\notin N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

Lemma 6.13

(Discrete Duhamel formula)
.

Let
M^{N}
be as in (
3.4
). Furthermore, let
\mathfrak{H}^{N}
be as defined in (
1.11
), and define

\mathfrak{F}^{N}:=\mathscr{B}_{N}\cdot(\mathfrak{H}^{N}-\mathfrak{G}^{N}).

Restrict
\mathfrak{F}^{N}
to
[0,T]
thus viewed as an element of
C([0,T],\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. Then
L_{N}\mathfrak{F}^{N}
agrees with
D_{N}M^{N}
on
[0,T]
. Consequently
\mathfrak{F}^{N}
agrees with
K_{N}D_{N}M^{N}
on
[0,T]
.

Proof.

Firstly, notice that
L_{N}\mathfrak{G}^{N}(t,\cdot)=0
for
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

Let
V^{N}:=D_{N}M^{N}
. Then it is clear from (
3.4
) that

V^{N}(t,\phi)=N\big[M^{N}_{t+N^{-1}}(\phi)-M^{N}_{t}(\phi)\big]=N\mathscr{B}_{N}\int_{I}\phi(N^{-1/2}x)(\mathcal{L}_{N}Z_{N})(Nt,\mathrm{d}x),

for all
0\leq t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Now, with
Z_{N}
defined in (
3.1
), for
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{N}
we have

\mathfrak{H}^{N}(t,\phi)=\int_{I}\phi(N^{-1/2}x)Z_{N}(Nt,\mathrm{d}x).

With
\mathcal{L}_{N}
as defined in Section
3
, we than apply the convolution defining
L_{N}
to both sides and it is then clear that for
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
the expression for
L_{N}\mathfrak{H}^{N}(t,\cdot)
can be written as

\displaystyle N\mathscr{B}_{N}\int_{I}\phi(N^{-1/2}x)(\mathcal{L}_{N}Z_{N})(Nt,\mathrm{d}x).

which is the same as the expression for
V^{N}(t,\phi).

Now we need to show that equality holds even if
t\notin N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Since linear operators respect linear interpolation, and since all of the fields
\mathfrak{H}^{N},V_{N},M^{N}
are defined via linear interpolation, this is actually immediate.

Finally, if we view the restriction of
V^{N}
to
[0,T]
as an element of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})
supported on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
, then we can apply
K_{N}
to both sides and we obtain that
(\mathfrak{H}^{N}-\mathfrak{G}^{N})(t,\cdot)=K_{N}V^{N}(t,\cdot)=K_{N}D_{N}M^{N}(t,\cdot)
for all
t\in[0,T]
by Lemma
6.10
.
∎

6.3.
Identification of the correct noise coefficients for each regime

In this section, we specialize the function
f
appearing in the quadratic variation field (
3.13
) to the specific correct choices of functions
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\alpha,\beta}
,
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
,
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{v}
from Definitions
2.11
,
2.13
, and
2.15
respectively.

Proposition 6.14

(Tightness of all relevant processes)
.

The following are true.

(1)

The fields
M^{N}
from (
3.4
) may be realized as an element of
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
for any
\alpha<-5
and
\tau>1
. Moreover, they are tight with respect to that topology.

(2)

The fields
\mathfrak{F}_{N}:=\mathscr{B}_{N}\cdot(\mathfrak{H}^{N}-\mathbb{E}[\mathfrak{H}^{N}])
from (
1.11
) may be realized as an element of as an element of
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
for any
\alpha<-d-p-2
and
\tau>1
. Moreover, they are tight with respect to that topology.

(3)

For any
f:I\to\mathbb{R}
of exponential decay, the fields
Q_{N}^{f}
from Definition
3.13
may be realized as an element of
C([0,T],C^{\gamma,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
for any
\gamma<-1
and
\tau>1
. Moreover, they are tight with respect to that topology.

(4)

Let
\alpha,\gamma<0
be as in the previous items. Let
(M^{\infty},Q^{f,\infty},H^{\infty})
be a joint limit point of
(M^{N},\widehat{Q}_{N}^{f},\mathfrak{H}^{N})
in the space
C\big([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times C^{\gamma,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big),
where
f
could be any specific function on
I
. For all
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, the process
(M_{t}^{\infty}(\phi))_{t\in[0,T]}
is a continuous martingale with respect to the canonical filtration on that space, and moreover its quadratic variation is given by

(6.4)

\langle M^{\infty}(\phi)\rangle_{t}=\begin{cases}\sum_{\#(k_{1})=p,\#(k_{2})=p}\frac{1}{k_{1}!k_{2}!}Q_{t}^{\boldsymbol{\eta}_{k_{1},k_{2}},\infty}(\partial^{k_{1}}\phi\cdot\partial^{k_{2}}\phi),&N^{1/2}\varsigma_{N}\to 0\\
Q_{t}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{{\boldsymbol{v}}},\infty}(\phi^{2}),&N^{-1}\psi_{N}(p,d)\gg|\varsigma_{N}|\gg N^{-1/2},\frac{\varsigma_{N}}{|\varsigma_{N}|}\to{\boldsymbol{v}}\\
Q_{t}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{{\boldsymbol{v}}/|{\boldsymbol{v}}|},\infty}(\phi^{2}),&d=2\text{ and }(\log N)^{1/2p}\varsigma_{N}\to{\boldsymbol{v}}\neq 0\\
|{\boldsymbol{v}}|^{-2p}Q_{t}^{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{{\boldsymbol{v}}},\infty}(\phi^{2}),&d\geq 3\text{ and }\varsigma_{N}\to{\boldsymbol{v}}\neq 0.\end{cases}

Here
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\alpha,\beta}
,
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
,
\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{v}
are the specific functions from Definitions
2.11
,
2.13
, and
2.15
respectively. Furthermore, the same joint limit holds if
f
is replaced by
f_{N}
in the prelimit, where
\sup_{N}f_{N}
is dominated by some function of exponential decay and
f_{N}\to f
uniformly.

Proof.

Take any
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
with
\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq 1.
Recall
S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}
from (
6.2
). Using the first bound in Proposition
5.11
, we have for fixed
f:I\to\mathbb{R}
of rapid decay that

\mathbb{E}[(Q_{N}^{f}(t,S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi)-Q_{N}^{f}(s,S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi))^{k}]\leq C|t-s|^{k/2}\lambda^{-k},

uniformly over
x\in\mathbb{R},\lambda\in(0,1],0\leq s,t\leq T
with
s,t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Since
Q_{N}(0,\phi)=0
by definition, the assumptions of Lemma
6.7
are therefore satisfied for any
\kappa\leq 1/4
, any
p>1/\kappa
, and any
\alpha\leq-1
, and we conclude the desired tightness for
\widehat{Q}_{N}=Q_{N}
. This proves Item
(3)
.

Now we address the tightness of the
M^{N}
. Let
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}
be as in Definition
1.12
. Using Proposition
5.14
, we have that

(6.5)

\mathbb{E}[(M^{N}_{t}(\phi)-M^{N}_{s}(\phi))^{q}]^{1/q}\leq C(t-s)^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}}\|\phi\|_{C^{p+2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

where
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
C=C(k,q)>0
is free of
\phi,s,t,N.

This gives

\mathbb{E}\big[\big(M^{N}_{t}(S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi)-M^{N}_{s}(S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi)\big)^{2/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}}\big]^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}/2}\leq C(t-s)^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ic}}}\lambda^{-d-p-2}

uniformly over
x\in\mathbb{R},\lambda\in(0,1],0\leq s,t\leq T
, and
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\|\phi\|_{C^{p+2}}\leq 1
with support contained in the unit interval. Moreover
M^{N}(0,\phi)=0
by definition, therefore the assumptions of Lemma
6.7
are satisfied with
\kappa=\epsilon
, any
q>1/\kappa
, and any
\alpha\leq-d-p-2
. Hence, we conclude the desired tightness for
M^{N}
. This proves Item
(1)
.

Now tightness for the fields
\mathfrak{H}^{N}-\mathfrak{G}^{N}
is immediate from Lemma
6.15
, since we know from Lemma
6.13
that
\mathfrak{H}^{N}-\mathfrak{G}^{N}=K_{N}D_{N}M^{N}
where we view
K_{N}D_{N}
as a bounded operator from
C([0,T+1],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\to C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.

We next show that the limit point
M^{\infty}(\phi)
is a martingale indexed by
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Since
M^{N}_{0}(\phi)=0
, from (
6.5
), we see that
\sup_{N}\mathbb{E}[M^{N}_{t}(\phi)^{2k}]<\infty.
Thus
M^{\infty}(\phi)
is a martingale since martingality is preserved by limit points under the uniform integrability assumption. Continuity is guaranteed by the definition of the spaces in which we proved tightness. In the prelimit, we know
M_{t}^{N}(\phi)^{2}
minus the objects in Proposition
3.6
are martingales. By the latter proposition and the tightness estimates (
5.22
) of
Q_{N}^{f}
, it follows that the error terms
V^{j}_{N}(t,\phi)
and
\mathcal{E}_{N}^{j}(t,\phi)
vanish in probability (in the topology of
C[0,T]
for each
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
), so we conclude (again by uniform
L^{p}
boundedness guaranteed by Proposition
5.11
) that the process given in the proposition statement is a martingale. This verifies (
6.4
) completing the proof of Item
(4)
.
∎

6.4.
Technical lemmas on the operators

Lemma 6.15
.

Fix
\alpha<0,\tau>0
. Let
K_{N},D_{N}
be the operators from Definition
6.9
. Recall the function spaces from Definition
6.2
.Then we have the operator norm bound

\sup_{N\geq 1}\|K_{N}D_{N}\|_{C([0,T+1],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\to C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}<\infty.

Proof.

The full proof can be found in
[
Par24
, Lemma 5.18]
. Note that that proof is for
d=1
but readily extends to all dimensions.
∎

Lemma 6.16
