(Controlling the difference between the discrete and continuum time-derivatives)
.

Fix
\alpha<0,\tau>1
. The derivative operator
\partial_{s}:C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})\to C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-2,\tau}(\Psi_{T})
which was defined in Remark
6.6
, is a bounded linear map. Furthermore, let
D_{N}
be as in Definition
6.9
. Then we have the operator norm bounds

\displaystyle\sup_{N\geq 1}\|D_{N}\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})\to C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-2,\tau}(\Psi_{T})}

\displaystyle<\infty.

\displaystyle\|D_{N}-\partial_{s}\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})\to C^{\alpha-4,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}N^{-1}.

Proof.

The proof is fairly immediate from the definitions, see
[
DDP24a
, Lemma 6.21]
for the complete argument. ∎

Lemma 6.17

(Controlling the difference between the discrete and continuum heat operators)
.

Fix
\alpha<0,\tau>1
. Let
K_{N}
be as in Definition
6.9
, and let
K:C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})\to C^{\alpha+2,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})
by

(6.6)

\displaystyle Kf(t,x):=\int_{\Psi_{T}}G_{t-s}(x-y)f(s,y)dsdy,

where
G_{t}
as usual is the deterministic keat kernel on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then we have the operator norm bound

\|K_{N}-K\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})\to C^{\alpha-1,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})}\leq CN^{-1/4}.

Here,
C
is independent of
N
.

The above bound is crude; we do not claim optimality of the Hölder exponents here.

Proof.

The fact that
K
as defined by (
6.6
) actually defines a bounded linear map
C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})\to C^{\alpha+2,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})
is the so-called Schauder estimate, see e.g.
[
HL15
, Corollary 1.2]
.
For the operator norm bound, see
[
DDP24b
, Lemma 6.20]
, and replace
\mathbb{R}
by
\mathbb{R}^{d}
throughout. When applying the KMT coupling there, replace the nearest neighbor random walk by that of the increment distribution
\mu
instead, and the remaining details go through verbatim.
∎

Corollary 6.18
.

Let
\alpha<0
. With
K
as in (
6.6
) and
\partial_{s}
as in Remark
6.6
, we have that
K\partial_{s}:C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))\to C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})).
Furthermore,
\|K_{N}D_{N}-K\partial_{s}\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})\to C^{\alpha-5,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})}\to 0.

This is immediate from the result of Lemma
6.15
, and the previous two corollaries.

6.5.
Uniqueness of the martingale problem and proof of main results

Proposition 6.19
.

In any dimension, one has uniqueness of the martingale problem for the SPDEs appearing in Theorems
1.13

-

1.20
. More precisely, we have the following result.

Fix
\alpha<0
,
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
,
p\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},
and coefficients
\gamma_{k_{1},k_{2}}\in\mathbb{R}
. Consider any
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
-valued random process
u(t,\bullet)
adapted to the canonical filtration, with
u(0,\bullet)\equiv 0
. If the processes

\displaystyle M_{t}(\phi)

\displaystyle:=u(t,\phi)-u(0,G_{t}*\phi)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}u(s,\Delta\phi)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle G_{t}(\phi)

\displaystyle:=M_{t}(\phi)^{2}-\sum_{\#(k_{1})=p,\#(k_{2})=p}\gamma_{k_{1},k_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\partial^{k_{1}}\phi(a)\partial^{k_{2}}\phi(a)\cdot(\mathfrak{H}_{0}*G_{t})(a)^{2}\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}s

are both martingales for every
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
u(t,\bullet)
must have the same law as the solution of the additive-noise SPDE given by

(6.7)

\partial_{t}\mathcal{U}(t,x)=\frac{1}{2}\Delta\mathcal{U}(t,x)+\eta(t,x),

where
\eta
is the Gaussian noise on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
that has covariance structure given by

\mathbb{E}[(\eta(t,\bullet),\phi)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}]=\sum_{\#(k_{1})=p,\#(k_{2})=p}\gamma_{k_{1},k_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\partial^{k_{1}}\phi(a)\partial^{k_{2}}\phi(a)\cdot(\mathfrak{H}_{0}*G_{t})(a)^{2}\mathrm{d}a,\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;t>0,\;\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

In particular, if
p=0
, the result says the following. Consider any
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
-valued random process
u(t,\bullet)
adapted to the canonical filtration, with
u(0,\bullet)\equiv 0
. If the processes

\displaystyle M_{t}(\phi)

\displaystyle:=u(t,\phi)-u(0,G_{t}*\phi)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}u(s,\Delta\phi)ds

\displaystyle G_{t}(\phi)

\displaystyle:=M_{t}(\phi)^{2}-\gamma^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\big(G(s,\bullet)^{2},\phi^{2}\big)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mathrm{d}s

are both martingales for every
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
u(t,\bullet)
must have the same law as the solution of the additive-noise SPDE

(6.8)

\partial_{t}\mathcal{U}(t,x)=\frac{1}{2}\Delta\mathcal{U}(t,x)+\gamma\cdot(\mathfrak{H}_{0}*G_{t})(x)\;\xi(t,x),

where
\mathcal{U}(t,0)=0
and
\xi
is a standard space-time white noise.

Thus, for
p=0
the result is relevant to the extremal SPDE appearing in Theorems
1.13

-

1.18
, whereas for
p\geq 1
it is more relevant to the bulk SPDE.

Proof.

This is quite standard from the theory of martingale problems (see
[
SV97
]
), so we only sketch the proof here. It is easily shown using the Dubins-Schwarz time change theorem that any continuous martingale with a
deterministic
quadratic variation process is a Gaussian process. Using the Cramer-Wold device and linearity of
\phi\mapsto M_{t}(\phi)
, it follows that the entire family of random variables
(M_{t}(\phi))_{\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),t\geq 0}
is a jointly Gaussian process in the variables
t,\phi.
Thus, using Lemma
6.7
and the Gaussianity of the marginals of
M
, the law of
M
can be realized as a Gaussian measure on the Banach space
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
. From here, notice that in the notation of Lemmas
6.15
and
6.16
and
6.17
, the field
u
is just
K\partial_{s}
applied to
M
, where
K\partial_{s}:C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))\to C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
is a
bounded linear map
(see Corollary
6.18
). In particular, the law of
u
can also be realized as a Gaussian field on the same Banach space. Now one simply matches the covariances of
u
to the correct Gaussian field, but this is immediate from the given expressions for the quadratic variation.∎

Now we are in a position to prove the main theorems of the paper.

Proof of Theorems
1.13
,
1.15
,
1.18
, and
1.20
.

There are four different cases depending on the choice of location strength
\varsigma_{N}
. Recall that we referred to these as
Regime A
,
Regime B
,
Regime C
, and
Regime D
in the introduction. We also recall the constant
c_{1}:=1,c_{2}=2\pi,
and
c_{d}:=d(2-d)/\Gamma(1+\frac{d}{2})
. To prove the theorems, note from Proposition
6.19
that we just need to show that the limiting quadratic variation field
Q^{f,\infty}
constructed in Proposition
6.14
is actually deterministic with the correct quadratic variation in each case.

Regime A.
N^{1/2}\varsigma_{N}\to\boldsymbol{\varsigma}
.
As explained in the proof of
3.6
it suffices to consider only the case
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}=0.
As guaranteed by Proposition
6.14
, let
(M^{\infty},Q^{\infty},H^{\infty})
be a joint limit point of
(M^{N},\widehat{Q}_{N}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}},\mathfrak{H}^{N})
in the space
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times C^{\gamma,\tau}(\mathbb{R})\times C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),
where
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
is the specific function from Definition
2.13
. On one hand, Proposition
6.14
guarantees that the process
\langle M^{\infty}(\phi)\rangle_{t}=\sum_{\#(k_{1})=p,\#(k_{2})=p}\frac{1}{k_{1}!k_{2}!}Q_{t}^{\eta_{k_{1},k_{2}},\infty}(\partial^{k_{1}}\phi\cdot\partial^{k_{2}}\phi)
. On the other hand, we have by Lemmas
6.15
and
6.16
and
6.17
, and the relation of Lemma
6.13
that
H^{\infty}=K\partial_{s}M^{\infty}.
Furthermore by Proposition
5.10
and the uniform integrability guaranteed by Proposition
5.11
, we also have that the process

Q^{f,\infty}_{t}(\phi)-\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(f)\cdot c_{d}\cdot\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big((G_{s}*\mathfrak{H}_{0})(a)\big)^{2}\phi(a)\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}s

is a martingale starting at 0, by using the Markov property and directly computing the conditional expectation. On the other hand, as long as
\phi\geq 0
, one verifies that it is a difference of two increasing processes, thus of finite variation. We conclude that the above process is in fact 0.
This identifies
H^{\infty}
as an element of
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
with the property that
M^{\infty}_{t}(\phi)=(H^{\infty}_{t},\phi)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}(H^{\infty}_{s},\Delta\phi)ds
is a martingale with the deterministic quadratic variation given by
\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}A_{p}[\phi,\phi](a)(\mathfrak{H}_{0}*G_{s})(a)\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}a
. By the uniqueness of the martingale problem from
6.19
, this uniquely identifies
H^{\infty}
in law as the solution of the SPDE written in the first bullet points of Theorems
1.13
,
1.15
, and
1.18
.

Regime B.
\psi_{N}(p,d)\gg|\varsigma_{N}|\gg N^{-1/2}
and
\varsigma_{N}/|\varsigma_{N}|\to{\boldsymbol{v}}
.
As guaranteed by Proposition
6.14
, let
(M^{\infty},Q^{\infty},H^{\infty})
be a joint limit point of
(M^{N},\widehat{Q}_{N}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}},\mathfrak{H}^{N})
in the space
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times C^{\gamma,\tau}(\mathbb{R})\times C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),
where
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
is the specific function from Definition
2.13
. On one hand, Proposition
6.14
guarantees that
\langle M^{\infty}(\phi)\rangle_{t}=Q^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{{\boldsymbol{v}}},\infty}(\phi^{2}).
On the other hand, we have by Lemmas
6.15
and
6.16
and
6.17
, and the relation of Lemma
6.13
that
H^{\infty}=K\partial_{s}M^{\infty}.
Furthermore by Proposition
5.10
and the uniform integrability guaranteed by Proposition
5.11
, we also have that the process

Q^{f,\infty}_{t}(\phi)-\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(f)\cdot c_{d}\cdot\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big((G_{s}*\mathfrak{H}_{0})(a)\big)^{2}\phi(a)\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}s

is a martingale starting at 0, by using the Markov property and directly computing the conditional expectation. On the other hand, as long as
\phi\geq 0
, one verifies that it is a difference of two increasing processes, thus of finite variation. We conclude that the above process is in fact 0.This identifies
H^{\infty}
as an element of
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
with the property that
M^{\infty}_{t}(\phi)=(H^{\infty}_{t},\phi)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}(H^{\infty}_{s},\Delta\phi)ds
is a martingale with the correct quadratic variation. By the uniqueness of the martingale problem from
6.19
, this uniquely identifies
H^{\infty}
in law as the solution of the SPDE written in the second bullet points of Theorems
1.13
,
1.15
, and
1.18
.

Regime C.
d=2
and
(\log N)^{1/2p}\varsigma_{N}\to{\boldsymbol{v}}
.
As guaranteed by Proposition
6.14
, let
(M^{\infty},Q^{\infty},H^{\infty})
be a joint limit point of
(M^{N},\widehat{Q}_{N}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}},\mathfrak{H}^{N})
in the space
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times C^{\gamma,\tau}(\mathbb{R})\times C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),
where
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
is the specific function from Definition
2.13
. On one hand, Proposition
6.14
guarantees that
\langle M^{\infty}(\phi)\rangle_{t}=Q_{t}^{\infty}(\phi^{2}).
On the other hand, we have by Lemmas
6.15
and
6.16
and
6.17
, and the relation of Lemma
6.13
that
H^{\infty}=K\partial_{s}M^{\infty}.
Furthermore by Proposition
5.10
and the uniform integrability guaranteed by Proposition
5.11
, we also have that the process

Q^{f,\infty}_{t}(\phi)-\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(f)\frac{2}{2-\gamma_{\mathrm{ext}}({\boldsymbol{v}})}\cdot 2\pi\cdot\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big((G_{s}*\mathfrak{H}_{0})(a)\big)^{2}\phi(a)\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}s

is a martingale starting at 0, by using the Markov property and directly computing the conditional expectation. On the other hand, as long as
\phi\geq 0
, one verifies that it is a difference of two increasing processes, thus of finite variation. We conclude that the above process is in fact 0. This identifies
H^{\infty}
as an element of
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
with the property that
M^{\infty}_{t}(\phi)=(H^{\infty}_{t},\phi)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}(H^{\infty}_{s},\Delta\phi)ds
is a martingale with the correct quadratic variation. By the uniqueness of the martingale problem from
6.19
, this uniquely identifies
H^{\infty}
in law as the solution of the SPDE written in the third bullet point of Theorems
1.15
.

Regime D.
d\geq 3
and
\varsigma_{N}\to v.
As guaranteed by Proposition
6.14
, let
(M^{\infty},Q^{\infty},H^{\infty})
be a joint limit point of
(M^{N},\widehat{Q}_{N}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}},\mathfrak{H}^{N})
in the space
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times C^{\gamma,\tau}(\mathbb{R})\times C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),
where
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
is the specific function from Definition
2.13
. On one hand, Proposition
6.14
guarantees that
\langle M^{\infty}(\phi)\rangle_{t}=Q_{t}^{\infty}(\phi^{2}).
On the other hand, we have by Lemmas
6.15
and
6.16
and
6.17
, and the relation of Lemma
6.13
that
H^{\infty}=K\partial_{s}M^{\infty}.
Furthermore by Proposition
5.10
and the uniform integrability guaranteed by Proposition
5.11
, we also have that the process

Q^{f,\infty}_{t}(\phi)-\Theta_{\mathrm{eff}}^{2}(f;{\boldsymbol{v}})\cdot c_{d}\cdot\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big((G_{s}^{({\boldsymbol{v}})}*\mathfrak{H}_{0})(a)\big)^{2}\phi(a)\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}s

is a martingale starting at 0, by using the Markov property and directly computing the conditional expectation. On the other hand, as long as
\phi\geq 0
, one verifies that it is a difference of two increasing processes, thus of finite variation. We conclude that the above process is in fact 0. This identifies
H^{\infty}
as an element of
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
with the property that
M^{\infty}_{t}(\phi)=(H^{\infty}_{t},\phi)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}(H^{\infty}_{s},\Delta_{v}\phi)ds
is a martingale with the correct quadratic variation (here
\Delta_{v}=\mathrm{div}(H_{v}\nabla))
which is self-adjoint). By the uniqueness of the martingale problem from
6.19
, this uniquely identifies
H^{\infty}
in law as the solution of the SPDE written in the third bullet point of Theorems
1.18
with the coefficient given by that of Theorem
1.20
.
∎

Finally, we prove Theorem
1.21
.

Proof of Theorem
1.21
.

The proof is extremely similar to before: the main idea is to study the
cross variations
of the martingales
M_{t}^{N}(\phi)
for different initial data.

Thus we let
\nu^{j}_{N}

(1\leq j\leq n)
be probability measures on
I
with the property that the associated sequences of rescaled initial data
\mathfrak{H}^{N}_{j}(0,\phi)
as derived from
\nu^{j}_{N}
via (
1.11
) are good sequences in the sense of Definition
1.12
, and furthermore converge weakly as
N\to\infty
to some measures
\mathfrak{H}_{j}.
Then we define for
1\leq j\leq n
the martingales
M^{j,N}_{t}(\phi)
to be the martingales from (
3.4
), but with the property that the initial condition is replaced by
\nu_{N}^{j}
.

For
i\neq j
, one needs to compute
\langle M^{i,N}(\phi),M^{j,N}(\phi)\rangle
. Redoing the calculation of Proposition
3.6
for this case, one has that

\langle M^{i,\infty}(\phi),M^{j,\infty}(\phi)\rangle_{t}=Q^{\bullet}_{N}(t,\phi;\nu^{i}_{N}\otimes\nu^{j}_{N})+\sum_{1,2}\mathcal{E}^{a}+\mathcal{V}^{a}

where
Q_{N}
is exactly as in (
3.13
), where the “
\bullet
” depends on the regime in exactly the same way as in Proposition
3.6
, and the error terms are defined likewise. Note the only difference here compared to earlier is that the initial condition for the random walkers is
\nu_{N}^{i}\otimes\nu_{N}^{j}
rather than
\nu_{N}^{\otimes 2}
as before.

Going through exactly the same calculation as in Theorem
5.10
but replacing
\nu_{N}^{\otimes 2}
by
\nu_{N}^{i}\otimes\nu_{N}^{j}
throughout that proof, one has the following limits:

•

If
d=1
and
N^{\frac{1}{4p}}\varsigma_{N}\to 0
then

\lim_{N\to\infty}\;\mathbb{E}\big[Q_{N}^{f}(t,\phi;\nu^{i}_{N}\otimes\nu^{j}_{N})\big]=\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(f)\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(G_{t}*\mathfrak{H}_{i})(a)(G_{t}*\mathfrak{H}_{j})(a)\phi(a)\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}s.

•

If
d=2
and
(\log N)^{1/2p}\varsigma_{N}\to{\boldsymbol{v}}
then we have

\lim_{N\to\infty}\;\mathbb{E}\big[Q_{N}^{f}(t,\phi;\nu^{i}_{N}\otimes\nu^{j}_{N})\big]=\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(f)\frac{2}{2-\gamma_{\mathrm{ext}}({\boldsymbol{v}})}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(G_{t}*\mathfrak{H}_{i})(a)(G_{t}*\mathfrak{H}_{j})(a)\phi(a)\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}s.

•

If
d\geq 3
and if
\varsigma_{N}\to{\boldsymbol{v}}
then

\lim_{N\to\infty}\;\mathbb{E}\big[Q_{N}^{f}(t,\phi;\nu^{i}_{N}\otimes\nu^{j}_{N})\big]=\Theta_{\mathrm{eff}}^{2}(f;{\boldsymbol{v}})\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(G_{t}^{(\boldsymbol{v})}*\mathfrak{H}_{i})(a)(G_{t}^{(\boldsymbol{v})}*\mathfrak{H}_{j})(a)\phi(a)\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}s,

where
\Theta_{\mathrm{eff}}^{2}(f;{\boldsymbol{v}})
is as in Proposition
5.5
.

We allow
\boldsymbol{v}
to be 0 above, so that this covers all regimes. Now going through the proof of the uniqueness in (
6.19
), one finds that for any joint limit point as guaranteed by Proposition
6.14
, this then leads to the correct expressions to match the quadratic variations of the flow of solutions for the SPDEs written in the main theorems.
∎

Appendix A
Proof of Theorem
4.6
: Invariance principle for SRI chains

These appendices are devoted to the proofs of the three major theorems in Section
4
. Most of the proofs will involve fairly standard techniques involving well-known bounds on martingales and additive functionals of time-homogeneous Markov chains. A few ideas from the theory of Fourier transforms will also be used.

We start with the proof of Theorem
4.6
. We first need three lemmas and another definition.

Lemma A.1
.

Consider a martingale
M
defined on any filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},P),
and suppose that the increments of
M
satisfy a uniform
q^{th}
moment bound under the probability measure
P
:

\mathcal{S}_{q}(M;P):=\sup_{n\geq 1}E[|X_{n+1}-X_{n}|^{q}]<\infty,

where
q\geq 2
. Then
E[|M_{r}-M_{s}|^{q}]\leq C_{q}(r-s)^{\frac{q}{2}}\mathcal{S}_{q}(M;P),
for some absolute constant
C_{q}
that is uniform over all martingales
M
and probability spaces
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},P).

Proof.

We may apply Burkholder-Davis-Gundy, then Jensen, then the definition of
\mathcal{S}_{q}(M;P)
in that order to obtain uniformly over
r\geq s\geq 0

\displaystyle E[|M_{r}-M_{s}|^{q}]

\displaystyle\leq C_{q}E\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{n=s}^{r-1}(M_{n+1}-M_{n})^{2}\bigg)^{q/2}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq C_{q}E\bigg[(r-s)^{\frac{q}{2}-1}\sum_{n=s}^{r-1}|M_{n+1}-M_{n}|^{q}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq C_{q}(r-s)^{\frac{q}{2}}\mathcal{S}_{q}(M;P).

Since the constant
C_{q}
in Burkholder-Davis-Gundy is known to be universal, the claim follows.
∎

Lemma A.2
.

Let
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}
be two probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, and let
g:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
G:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}g(x)(\nu_{1}(\mathrm{d}x)+\nu_{2}(\mathrm{d}x))<\infty
. Then for any measurable
f:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
f(x)^{2}\leq g(x)
we have

\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}f(x)\big((\nu_{1}(\mathrm{d}x)-\nu_{2}(\mathrm{d}x)\big)\bigg|\leq 2G^{1/2}\|\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}\|_{TV}^{1/2}

Typically we will apply this lemma when
g(x)=|x|^{q}
for some
q>1
, or where
g(x)=e^{\sigma|x|}
for some
\sigma>0.

Proof.

Let
\rho:=\|\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}\|_{TV}.
Using the coupling definition of total variation distance, one may construct a probability measure
\omega
on
\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
with the property that
\omega(\{(x,y):x\neq y\})\leq 2\rho.
Then

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}f(x)\big((\nu_{1}(\mathrm{d}x)-\nu_{2}(\mathrm{d}x)\big)\bigg|

\displaystyle=\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}}(f(x)-f(y))\omega(\mathrm{d}x,\mathrm{d}y)\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\{(x,y):x\neq y\}}\big(|f(x)|+|f(y)|\big)\omega(\mathrm{d}x,\mathrm{d}y)

\displaystyle\leq\omega(\{(x,y):x\neq y\})^{1/2}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}}\big(2f(x)^{2}+2f(y)^{2}\big)\omega(\mathrm{d}x,\mathrm{d}y)\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq(2\rho)^{1/2}(2G)^{1/2}.

In the second line, we used triangle inequality and the fact that
f(x)-f(y)=0
on the set
\{(x,y):x=y\}
. We used Cauchy-Schwartz in the third line, together with the fact that
(|a|+|b|)^{2}\leq 2a^{2}+2b^{2}
.
∎

Definition A.3
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
1\leq i\neq j\leq k
. Consider a decreasing function
F:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
of exponential decay at infinity. We define the functional
V^{ij}(F;\bullet):(I^{k})^{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}\to I^{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
by

(A.1)

\displaystyle V^{ij}(F;r):=\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}F(|R^{i}_{s}-R^{j}_{s}|).

We remark that the processes
V^{ij}(F;\bullet)
are predictable with respect to the canonical filtration on
(I^{k})^{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
.

These processes
V^{ij}
will play a crucial role going forward in this appendix.

Lemma A.4
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
q\geq 2
,
M>0
, a decreasing function
F:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
of exponential decay at infinity, and
1\leq i,j\leq k
. Let
u_{\mathbf{x}}^{ij}(\mathbf{y}):=|y_{i}-y_{j}-(x_{i}-x_{j})|
, for
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in I^{k}
. Here
|u|
as usual denotes the Euclidean norm of
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let
\boldsymbol{b}_{0}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(q,M,F)
be centered. Then there exists
C>0
such that uniformly over all
\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(q,M,F)
and
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in I^{k}
one has

-C\cdot d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})^{1/4}F\big(\min_{i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}}|y_{i^{\prime}}-y_{j^{\prime}}|\big)^{1/4}\leq(P^{\boldsymbol{b}}-\mathrm{Id})u_{\mathbf{x}}^{ij}(\mathbf{y})\leq C,

where
P^{\boldsymbol{b}}
is the Markov operator associated to
P_{\boldsymbol{b}}
.

Proof.

For the upper bound, we have that

\displaystyle(P^{\boldsymbol{b}}-\mathrm{Id})u_{\mathbf{x}}^{ij}(\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\int_{I^{k}}\bigg(\big|a_{i}-a_{j}-(x_{i}-x_{j})\big|-\big|y_{i}-y_{j}-(x_{i}-x_{j})\big|\bigg)\boldsymbol{b}(\mathbf{y},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{a})

\displaystyle\leq\int_{I^{k}}|a_{i}-a_{j}-(y_{i}-y_{j})|\boldsymbol{b}(\mathbf{y},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{a}).

From here, the uniform bound follows from
(q,M)
being fixed.

For the lower bound, use Jensen’s inequality to say

\displaystyle P^{\boldsymbol{b}}u_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\int_{I^{k}}|a_{i}-a_{j}-(x_{i}-x_{j})|\boldsymbol{b}(\mathbf{y},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{a})

\displaystyle\geq\bigg|\int_{I^{k}}(a_{i}-a_{j})\boldsymbol{b}(\mathbf{y},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{a})-(x_{i}-x_{j})\bigg|

\displaystyle\geq|y_{i}-y_{j}-(x_{i}-x_{j})|-\bigg|y_{i}-y_{j}-\int_{I^{k}}(a_{i}-a_{j})\boldsymbol{b}(\mathbf{y},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{a})\bigg|

\displaystyle=u_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{y})-\bigg|y_{i}-y_{j}-\int_{I^{k}}(a_{i}-a_{j})\boldsymbol{b}(\mathbf{y},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{a})\bigg|.

Thus we just need to show that

(A.2)

\bigg|y_{i}-y_{j}-\int_{I^{k}}(a_{i}-a_{j})\boldsymbol{b}(\mathbf{y},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{a})\bigg|\leq C\cdot d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})^{1/4}F\big(\min_{i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}}|y_{i^{\prime}}-y_{j^{\prime}}|\big)^{1/4}.

In fact, we will actually show the stronger claim that

\bigg|y_{i}-y_{j}-\int_{I^{k}}(a_{i}-a_{j})\boldsymbol{b}(\mathbf{y},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{a})\bigg|\leq C\min\big\{d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})^{1/2},F\big(\min_{i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}}|y_{i^{\prime}}-y_{j^{\prime}}|\big)^{1/2}\big\},

from which (
A.2
) will follow immediately using
\min\{a,b\}\leq a^{1/2}b^{1/2}.

For the upper bound of
d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})^{1/2}
, first note by the centered assumption that
y_{i}-y_{j}=\int_{I^{k}}(a_{i}-a_{j})\boldsymbol{b}_{0}(\mathbf{y},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{a})
, then apply Lemma
A.2
. For the upper bound of
F\big(\min_{i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}}|y_{i^{\prime}}-y_{j^{\prime}}|\big)^{1/2}
, let
\nu_{\boldsymbol{b}}
denote the base measure of
\boldsymbol{b}
. Note that
\nu_{\boldsymbol{b}}^{\otimes k}
is a permutation-invariant measure, thus
\int_{I^{k}}(a_{i}-a_{j})\nu_{\boldsymbol{b}}^{\otimes k}(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{a})=0
. Then apply Lemma
A.2
.
∎

The above bound will be quite useful in showing tightness of all of the relevant processes in the topology of
C[0,T]
. The following estimate is one of the key results of this appendix, and an important step towards proving both Theorem
4.6
and Theorem
4.7
.

Theorem A.5
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
q\geq 2
and a decreasing function
F:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
such that
\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\ell^{d-1}F(\ell)^{1/4}<\infty
. Let
\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(q,M,F)
be centered and
\delta
-repulsive.

Also fix some decreasing function
H:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
such that
\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\ell^{d-1}H(\ell)<\infty
. There exists some
C=C(k,F,H,q)>0
and
\epsilon=\epsilon(k,F,H,q)>0
such that uniformly over all
\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(q,M,F)
with
d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})<\epsilon
, and all integers
r\geq r^{\prime}\geq 0
and
1\leq i,j\leq k
we have the bounds

(A.3)

\displaystyle\;\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}}\;\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\big[|(R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r})-(R^{i}_{r^{\prime}}-R^{j}_{r^{\prime}})|^{q}\big]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq C(r-r^{\prime})^{1/2}.

(A.4)

\displaystyle\;\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}}\;\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\big[\big|V^{ij}(H;r)-V^{ij}(H;r^{\prime})\big|^{q}\big]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq C(r-r^{\prime})^{1/2}.

Proof.

By the Markov property and the fact that there is a supremum over
\mathbf{x}
, it suffices to prove the claim when
r^{\prime}=0
, so that
R^{i}_{r^{\prime}}-R^{j}_{r^{\prime}}=x_{i}-x_{j}
where
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},...,x_{k})
in coordinates. We can also assume without any loss of generality that
H\geq F^{1/4}
because otherwise one may replace
G
with
\tilde{H}:=\max\{H,F^{1/4}\}
, note that
\tilde{H}
is still decreasing and summable, then prove the claim for
\tilde{G}
, and note that
V^{ij}(\tilde{H};r)-V^{ij}(\tilde{H};r^{\prime})\geq V^{ij}(H;r)-V^{ij}(H;r^{\prime})
pathwise to immediately obtain the claim for the original function
H
.

We now break the proof into five steps.

Step 1.
In this step, we show that there exists
\epsilon_{0}=\epsilon_{0}(q,M,\delta)>0
, so that
\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(q,M,F)
and
d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})<\epsilon_{0}
together imply that
\boldsymbol{b}
is
(\delta/2)
-repulsive. To prove this, notice by Lemma
A.2
that

\bigg|\int_{I^{k}}|y_{i}-y_{j}-(x_{i}-x_{j})|\big(\boldsymbol{b}-\boldsymbol{b}_{0})(\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})\bigg|\leq C\cdot d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})^{1/2},

for some
C=C(q,M)>0.
Thus taking
\epsilon_{0}:=\delta^{2}/(4C)
gives the claim.

Step 2.
Let
\epsilon_{0}
be as in Step 1. In this step, we show that the collection of Markov kernels
\mathscr{M}:=\{\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(q,M,F):d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})<\epsilon_{0}\}
satisfies a certain coercivity condition on test functions given by absolute values of differences of coordinates, specifically (
A.5
) below. This will be extremely useful later. For
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in I^{k}
, let

u_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{y}):=|y_{i}-y_{j}-(x_{i}-x_{j})|\;\;\;\;\text{and}\;\;\;\;v_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}:=P^{\boldsymbol{b}}u_{\mathbf{x}}-u_{\mathbf{x}},

so by Lemma
A.4

we have that
v_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}(\mathbf{y})\geq-C\cdot d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})^{1/4}\cdot F\big(\min_{i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}}|y_{i^{\prime}}-y_{j^{\prime}}|\big)^{1/4}
.

We now claim a stronger bound: specifically, we claim that there exists
\delta^{\prime}>0
so that for all
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in I^{k}
and
\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathscr{M}
one has that

(A.5)

v_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}(\mathbf{y})>\delta^{\prime}\mathbf{1}_{B_{\delta^{\prime}}(0)}\big(y_{i}-y_{j}-(x_{i}-x_{j})\big)-C\cdot d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})^{1/4}\cdot F\big(\min_{i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}}|y_{i^{\prime}}-y_{j^{\prime}}|\big)^{1/4}.

To prove this, note that

\displaystyle|P^{\boldsymbol{b}}u_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{y})-P^{\boldsymbol{b}}u_{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{y})|

\displaystyle=\bigg|\int_{I^{k}}|a_{i}-a_{j}-(x_{i}-x_{j})|\boldsymbol{b}(\mathbf{y},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{a})-\int_{I^{k}}|a_{i}-a_{j}-(y_{i}-y_{j})|\boldsymbol{b}(\mathbf{y},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{a})\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{I^{k}}\big||a_{i}-a_{j}-(x_{i}-x_{j})|-|a_{i}-a_{j}-(y_{i}-y_{j})|\big|\boldsymbol{b}(\mathbf{y},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{a})

\displaystyle\leq\int_{I^{k}}|x_{i}-x_{j}-(y_{i}-y_{j})|\boldsymbol{b}(\mathbf{y},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{a})=|x_{i}-x_{j}-(y_{i}-y_{j})|.

Recall from Step 1 that
\inf_{\beta\in B_{z_{0}/(2k)}(0)}\inf_{\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}}P^{\boldsymbol{b}}u_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{x})\geq\delta/2
. Thus for all
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in I^{k}
we have
P^{\boldsymbol{b}}u_{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{y})\geq\delta/2
, and from the above bound it then follows that
P^{\boldsymbol{b}}u_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{y})>\delta/3
if
|x_{i}-x_{j}-(y_{i}-y_{j})|<\delta/6.
Thus we see that
v_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}(\mathbf{y})=P^{\boldsymbol{b}}u_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{y})-|x_{i}-x_{j}-(y_{i}-y_{j})|>\delta/6
if
|x_{i}-x_{j}-(y_{i}-y_{j})|<\delta/6
, thus proving the claim (
A.5
) with
\delta^{\prime}:=\delta/6.

Step 3.
In this step, we are going to find a useful family of martingales and derive a discrete “Tanaka’s formula,” see (
A.7
) below. That formula will be instrumental in the later analysis. We will also derive bounds related to these martingales, specifically (
A.6
), (
A.8
), and (
A.9
) below.

If
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let
x^{(i)}
denote the element of
\mathbb{R}^{kd}
with
x_{i}
in the
i^{th}
coordinate and
0
in all other coordinates. Equation (
A.5
) easily implies that for all
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},...,y_{k})\in I^{k}
one has

\displaystyle H(|y_{i}-y_{j}|)

\displaystyle\leq(\delta^{\prime})^{-1}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\delta^{\prime}\mathbf{1}_{B_{\delta^{\prime}}(0)}\big(y_{i}-y_{j}-\delta^{\prime}m\big)\cdot G(\delta^{\prime}|m|)

(A.6)

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathscr{M}}(\delta^{\prime})^{-1}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}G(\delta^{\prime}|m|)\big[v^{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\delta^{\prime}m^{(i)}}(\mathbf{y})+C\cdot d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})^{1/4}F\big(\min_{i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}}|y_{i^{\prime}}-y_{j^{\prime}}|\big)^{1/4}\big].

For every
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a}\in I^{k}
, note that
u_{\mathbf{x}}(R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r})-\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}v_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}(R^{i}_{s}-R^{j}_{s})
is a
\mathbf{P}^{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\mathbf{a}}
-martingale for every
\mathbf{a}
, just by definition of the Markov operator (these are the Dynkin martingales). Defining
f_{H},g_{\boldsymbol{b},H}:I^{k}\to\mathbb{R}
by

f_{H}:=(\delta^{\prime})^{-1}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}H(\delta^{\prime}|m|)u_{\delta m^{(i)}},\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;g_{\boldsymbol{b},H}:=(\delta^{\prime})^{-1}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}G(\delta^{\prime}|m|)v^{\beta}_{\delta m^{(i)}},

we see that the process

(A.7)

\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{b},H}(r):=-(f_{G}(R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r})-f_{H}(\mathbf{x}))+\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}g_{\boldsymbol{b},H}(R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r})

is a
\mathbf{P}^{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\mathbf{a}}
-martingale (for all
\mathbf{y}\in I^{k})
with
\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{b},H}(0)=0
.
Note that
f
is globally Lipschitz, in fact

(A.8)

\displaystyle|f_{F}(\mathbf{x})-f_{F}(\mathbf{y})|

\displaystyle\leq\bigg((\delta^{\prime})^{-1}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}H(\delta^{\prime}|m|)\bigg)|x_{i}-x_{j}-(y_{i}-y_{j})|\leq C|x_{i}-x_{j}-(y_{i}-y_{j})|,

where we absorbed all constants in the last bound. Note that the sum is convergent precisely because of the assumption that
\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\ell^{d-1}H(\ell)<\infty
. We also claim that
g_{\boldsymbol{b},G}
is a globally bounded function. Indeed, Lemma
A.4
gives that
\Gamma:=\sup_{\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathscr{M}}\sup_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in I^{k}}v^{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{y})<\infty
, thus we see that

(A.9)

\sup_{\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathscr{M}}\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}}|g_{\boldsymbol{b},H}(\mathbf{x})|\leq\bigg((\delta^{\prime})^{-1}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}H(\delta^{\prime}|m|)\bigg)\cdot\Gamma<\infty.

From (
A.7
), (
A.8
), and (
A.9
) one sees that the increments of the martingales
\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{b},H}
have
q^{th}
moments bounded
uniformly over
\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathscr{M}
and all times
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, thus by Lemma
A.1
one obtains that

(A.10)

\sup_{\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathscr{M}}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[|\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{b},H}(r)|^{q}]^{1/q}\leq r^{1/2},

where
C
does not depend on
q
.

Step 4.
In this step, we will show an
a priori
bound that the left side of (
A.4
) can actually be bounded above by some multiple of the left side of (
A.3
) plus
C(r-r^{\prime})^{1/2},
which will effectively reduce the problem to showing the first bound. This bound is (
A.11
) below.

In (
A.6
), note by the decreasing property that
F\big(\min_{i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}}|x_{i^{\prime}}-x_{j^{\prime}}|\big)^{1/4}\leq\sum_{i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}}F(|x_{i^{\prime}}-x_{j^{\prime}}|)^{1/4}
. Furthermore, recall from the beginning of the proof our assumption (without loss of generality) that
H\geq F^{1/4}
, thus
F\big(|x_{i^{\prime}}-x_{j^{\prime}}|)^{1/4}\leq H(|x_{i^{\prime}}-x_{j^{\prime}}|)
. Thus from (
A.6
) we have the preliminary bound

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[V^{ij}(H;r)^{q}]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}g_{\boldsymbol{b},H}(R^{i}_{s}-R^{j}_{s})\bigg)^{q}\bigg]^{1/q}+C\cdot d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})^{1/4}\sum_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}[V^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}(H;r)^{q}]^{1/q}.

Using (
A.7
), (
A.8
), (
A.9
), and (
A.10
), we furthermore have

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}g_{\boldsymbol{b},H}(R^{i}_{s}-R^{j}_{s})\bigg)^{q}\bigg]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[|\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{b},G}(r)|^{q}]^{1/q}+\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[|f_{F}(R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r})-f_{F}(\mathbf{x})|^{q}]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq Cr^{1/2}+C\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-(x_{i}-x_{j})|^{q}]^{1/q}.

Combining the last two expressions and summing over all indices
(i,j)
with
1\leq i<j\leq k
, then moving all instances of
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[V^{ij}(G;r)^{q}]^{1/q}
to the left side of the inequality, yields

\displaystyle\big(1-C\cdot d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})^{1/4}\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}k(k-1)\big)

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[V^{ij}(H;r)^{q}]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq Cr^{1/2}+C\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-(x_{i}-x_{j})|^{q}]^{1/q}.

Now choose
\epsilon<\epsilon_{0}
small enough so that
C\cdot\epsilon^{1/4}\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}k(k-1)\leq\frac{1}{2}.
Then after absorbing all constants into some larger one, the last expression yields for
d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})<\epsilon
that

(A.11)

\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}[V^{ij}(H;r)^{q}]^{1/q}\leq Cr^{1/2}+C\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}[|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-(x_{i}-x_{j})|^{q}]^{1/q}.

This bound will be a key step in proving the theorem shortly.

Step 5.
In this step, we will obtain a bound that is in a sense converse to (
A.11
). Recall from (
A.2
) that

(A.12)

\bigg|y_{i}-y_{j}-\int_{I^{k}}(a_{i}-a_{j})\boldsymbol{b}(\mathbf{y},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{a})\bigg|\leq C\cdot d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})^{1/4}F\big(\min_{i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}}|y_{i^{\prime}}-y_{j^{\prime}}|\big)^{1/4}.

uniformly over
\beta\in B_{z_{0}/(2k)}(0)
and
\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}.

Let
\pi_{i}:I^{k}\to I
be the coordinate function
\mathbf{x}\mapsto x_{i}
, and define the process

M^{i,j,\boldsymbol{b}}_{r}:=R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-(x_{i}-x_{j})-\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\big((P^{\boldsymbol{b}}-\mathrm{Id})(\pi_{i}-\pi_{j})\big)(\mathbf{R}_{s}).

This is a
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}
-martingale for all
\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}
, which should
not
be confused with the Tanaka martingales appearing in (
A.7
), as they are unrelated. Now we can use Lemma
A.1
, because (
A.12
) and the condition that
\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(q,M,F)
show that the increments of this martingale have uniformly bounded
q^{th}
moments. Thus we see that
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}[|M^{i,j,\boldsymbol{b}}_{r}|^{q}]^{1/q}\leq Cr^{1/2},
where
C
does not depend on
\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(q,M,F)
and integers
r\geq 0
. Thus we find that

(A.13)

\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-(x_{i}-x_{j})|^{q}]^{1/q}\leq Cr^{1/2}+\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\big((P^{\boldsymbol{b}}-\mathrm{Id})(\pi_{i}-\pi_{j})\big)(\mathbf{R}_{s})\bigg)^{q}\bigg]^{1/q}.

Equation (
A.12
) precisely says
|(P^{\boldsymbol{b}}-\mathrm{Id})(\pi_{i}-\pi_{j})\big)(\mathbf{R}_{s})|\leq C\cdot d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})^{1/4}F\big(\min_{i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}}|y_{i^{\prime}}-y_{j^{\prime}}|\big)^{1/4},
which can be further bounded above by
C\cdot d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})^{1/4}\sum_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}F\big(|R^{i^{\prime}}_{s}-R^{j^{\prime}}_{s}|\big)^{1/4}.
Using the definition (
A.1
) of the processes
V^{ij}
, this precisely means that

\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\big|\big(P^{\boldsymbol{b}}-\mathrm{Id})(\pi_{i}-\pi_{j})\big)(\mathbf{R}_{s})\big|\leq Cd_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})^{1/4}\sum_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}V^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}(F^{1/4};r).

Plugging that bound into (
A.13
), and recalling
H\geq F^{1/4}
, we obtain

(A.14)

\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-(x_{i}-x_{j})|^{q}]^{1/q}\leq Cr^{1/2}+C\cdot d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})^{1/4}\sum_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[V^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}(H,r)^{q}]^{1/q}.

uniformly over
\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathscr{M}
and
\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}.

Step 6.
In this step, we will establish the theorem. Apply (
A.14
) and then (
A.11
) in that order, and one finds that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-(x_{i}-x_{j})|^{q}]^{1/q}\leq Cr^{1/2}+C\cdot d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})^{1/4}\sum_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[|R^{i^{\prime}}_{r}-R^{j^{\prime}}_{r}-(x_{i}-x_{j})|^{q}]^{1/q}.

Now sum the left side over all indices
(i,j)
with
1\leq i<j\leq k
, and move all instances of
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-(x_{i}-x_{j})|^{q}]^{1/q}
to the left side of the expression, and we obtain

\big(1-\tfrac{1}{2}k(k-1)\cdot C\cdot d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})^{1/4}\big)\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-(x_{i}-x_{j})|^{q}]^{1/q}\leq Cr^{1/2}.

Now make
\epsilon
smaller (if needed) so that
C\cdot\epsilon^{1/4}\cdot\frac{1}{2}k(k-1)\leq 1/2
. Then, for
d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})\leq\epsilon
, one obtains from the previous expression (after absorbing constants on the right side into some larger constant) that

\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-(x_{i}-x_{j})|^{q}]^{1/q}\leq Cr^{1/2}.

This proves (
A.3
), and then (
A.4
) follows immediately from (
A.3
) and (
A.11
).
∎

Now we are in a position to prove Theorem
4.6
.

Proof of Theorem
4.6
.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that the covariance matrix of the base measure
\nu_{0}
of
\boldsymbol{b}_{0}
is equal to the
d\times d
identity matrix. If not, one can always replace
I
by some linear transform of that lattice. We break the proof into several steps.

Step 1.
In this step, we find a useful family of martingales associated to each of the coordinates of the process. Define the
j^{th}
coordinate function
\pi_{j}:I^{k}\to I
by
(x_{1},...,x_{k})\mapsto x_{j}.
Notice that for each
1\leq j\leq k
that the process

M^{j,\boldsymbol{b}}_{r}:=R^{j}_{r}-\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}(P^{\boldsymbol{b}}-\mathrm{Id})\pi_{j}(\mathbf{R}_{s})

is a
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}
-martingale for all
\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}
and
\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(q,M,F)
. Explicitly we have that

(A.15)

(P^{\boldsymbol{b}}-\mathrm{Id})\pi_{j}(\mathbf{x})=\int_{I^{k}}(y_{j}-x_{j})\boldsymbol{b}(\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}).

Step 2.
Fix
j\in\{1,...,k\}
, and let
R^{j}
as usual denote the
j^{th}
coordinate of the canonical process
\mathbf{R}
on
(I^{k})^{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
. Recall the quantity
\mathcal{S}_{q}(M,P)
from Lemma
A.1
. In this step we will show that for each
q\geq 1
,

(A.16)

\sup_{\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(q,M,F)}\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}}\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M}^{\boldsymbol{b}};\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}})<\infty.

First note that

(A.17)

\sup_{\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(q,M,F)}\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}}|(P_{\boldsymbol{b}}-\mathrm{Id})\pi_{j}(\mathbf{x})|\leq\sup_{\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(q,M,F)}\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}}\int_{I^{k}}|y_{j}-x_{j}|\boldsymbol{b}(\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})<\infty.

Then note by the Markov property that

(A.18)

\sup_{r\geq 1}\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}}\sup_{\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(q,M,F)}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[|R^{j}_{r}-R^{j}_{r-1}|^{q}]\leq\sup_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in I^{k}}\sup_{\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(q,M,F)}\int_{I^{k}}|y_{j}-x_{j}|^{q}\boldsymbol{b}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})<\infty.

Combining (
A.17
) and (
A.18
), we immediately obtain (
A.16
).

Step 3.
In this step we study the processes given by

\mathcal{B}_{N}(t):=N^{-1/2}\mathbf{s}_{N}t-N^{-1/2}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt-1}(P^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}-\mathrm{Id})\pi_{j}(\mathbf{R}_{r}).

In particular we will show that if
q\geq 1
then
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[|\mathcal{B}_{N}(t)-\mathcal{B}_{N}(s)|^{q}]^{1/q}\leq Cd_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b}_{N},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})^{1/4}|t-s|^{1/2},
so that
\mathcal{B}_{N}
converges to the zero process in the topology of
C[0,T]
. To prove this, let
s(\nu):=\int_{I}u\nu(\mathrm{d}u)
so that
\mathbf{s}_{N}=(s(\nu_{N}),...,s(\nu_{N})).
First notice that by an extremely similar argument as that used in proving (
A.2
), one has

(A.19)

\bigg|s(\nu_{\boldsymbol{b}})-\int_{I^{k}}(a_{i}-y_{i})\boldsymbol{b}(\mathbf{y},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{a})\bigg|\leq C\cdot d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})^{1/4}F\big(\min_{i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}}|y_{i^{\prime}}-y_{j^{\prime}}|\big)^{1/4}.

uniformly over
\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(q,M,F)
and
\mathbf{y}\in I^{k}
. Here
\nu_{\boldsymbol{b}}
denotes the base measure of
\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(q,M,F)
. Thus for
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we have

\displaystyle|\mathcal{B}_{N}(t+N^{-1})-\mathcal{B}_{N}(t)|

\displaystyle=N^{-1/2}|s(\nu_{N})-\int_{I^{k}}(y_{j}-R^{j}_{r})\boldsymbol{b}(\mathbf{R}_{r},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})|

\displaystyle\leq N^{-1/2}d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b}_{N},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})^{1/4}F\big(\min_{i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}}|R_{r}^{i^{\prime}}-R_{r}^{j^{\prime}}|\big)^{1/4}

where
r:=Nt
. Thus using the bound
F(\min_{\ell}u_{\ell})^{1/4}\leq\sum_{\ell}F(u_{\ell})^{1/4}
we find that

\displaystyle|\mathcal{B}_{N}(t)-\mathcal{B}_{N}(s)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{u\in[s,t]\cap N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}|\mathcal{B}_{N}(u+N^{-1})-\mathcal{B}_{N}(u)|

\displaystyle\leq d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b}_{N},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})^{1/4}\sum_{r\in[Ns,Nt]\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}\sum_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}F(|R^{i^{\prime}}_{r}-R^{j^{\prime}}_{r}|)^{1/4}

Thus taking expectation and applying (
A.4
) we find that

(A.20)

\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[|\mathcal{B}_{N}(t)-\mathcal{B}_{N}(s)|^{q}]^{1/q}\leq Cd_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b}_{N},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})^{1/4}|t-s|^{1/2}.

Step 4.
Combining the results of (
A.16
) and (
A.20
) and Lemma
A.1
, we immediately obtain tightness of the rescaled process
\mathbf{X}_{N}
in the space
C[0,T]
. It remains to identify the limit point as Brownian motion. In this step, we will begin by showing that any limit point
\mathbf{U}=(U^{1},...,U^{k})
is necessarily a martingale in the joint filtration of all coordinates, and moreover each coordinate
U^{j}
is
individually
distributed as a standard Brownian motion started from
x_{j}
where
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},...,x_{k})
is as in the theorem statement.

Write
\mathbf{X}_{N}=(X_{N}^{1},...,X_{N}^{j})
in coordinates. By (
A.20
), the difference
X_{N}^{j}(t)-N^{-1/2}M^{j,\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}_{Nt}
converges in probability to zero in the topology of
C[0,T]
, thus it suffices to show that if
\mathbf{U}
is any limit point of the processes
(N^{-1/2}M^{j,\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}_{Nt})_{1\leq j\leq k,t\geq 0}
then
\mathbf{U}
is a martingale and each coordinate is individually a Brownian motion. The martingality of the limit point is clear, since martingality is preserved by limit points as long as one has uniform integrability as guaranteed by the
L^{q}
bounds (
A.16
).

Now we will show that each coordinate of the limit point
\mathbf{U}
is a standard Brownian motion. To do this, we need to study the quadratic variations of the martingales
(N^{-1/2}M^{j,\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}_{Nt})_{t\geq 0}
. Fix some vector
\mathbf{a}:=(a_{1},...,a_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{kd}
and define the martingale

Z^{\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}_{r}:=\sum_{j=1}^{k}a_{k}\bullet M^{j,\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}_{r},

where
\bullet
is the dot product in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Note that
(N^{-1/2}Z^{\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}_{Nt})_{t\geq 0}
is a tight family of processes in
C[0,T]
simply by Lemma
A.1
and (
A.17
).
Note that the process

Y^{\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{b}}_{r}:=(Z^{\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{b}}_{r})^{2}-\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[(Z^{\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{b}}_{s+1}-Z^{\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{b}}_{s})^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]

is also a
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}
-martingale. We claim that
(N^{-1}Y^{\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}_{Nt})_{t\geq 0}
is a tight family of processes in
C[0,T]
as
N\to\infty
. Indeed, one easily verifies that
\big(N^{-1}(M^{j,\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}_{Nt})^{2}\big)_{t\geq 0}
is tight and satisfies the same
L^{q}
estimates as the processes
\big(N^{-1/2}M^{j,\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}_{Nt}\big)_{t\geq 0}
on any compact time interval
t\in[0,T]
, simply because it is the square of a process satisfying such bounds as shown in Steps 2 and 3. On the other hand, by Minkowski’s inequality, we also have that

\displaystyle\bigg\|N^{-1}\sum_{r=Ns}^{Nt}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[(M^{j,\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}_{r+1}-M^{j,\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}_{r})^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]\bigg\|_{L^{q}(\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)})}

\displaystyle\leq N^{-1}\sum_{r=Ns}^{Nt}\big\|M^{j,\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}_{r+1}-M^{j,\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}_{r}\big\|_{L^{2q}(\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}})}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\sup_{N\geq 1}\mathcal{S}_{2q}(M^{j,\boldsymbol{b}_{N}};\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}})\bigg)|t-s|,

where the supremum is finite by (
A.16
). Summarizing these bounds, we have that for some
q>2
we have
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\big[\big(N^{-1}|Y^{\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}_{Nt}-Y^{\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}_{Ns}|\big)^{q}\big]^{1/q}\leq C|t-s|^{1/2}
for all
s,t
in a compact interval, thus completing the proof that
(N^{-1}Y^{\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}_{Nt})_{t\geq 0}
is a tight family of processes in
C[0,T]
as
N\to\infty
.

We will now study the joint limit as
N\to\infty
under the measures
\mathbf{P}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}
of the pair of processes
(N^{-1/2}Z^{\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}_{Nt},N^{-1}Y^{\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}_{Nt})_{t\geq 0}.
Consider any joint limit point
(U,V).
Then the pair are martingales in their joint filtration, since martingality is preserved by limit points as long as one has the
L^{q}
bounds as shown above. Let us study how
U
and
V
must be related. From (
A.15
) and (
A.19
), it is immediate that

(A.21)

\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\big[\big((P^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}-\mathrm{Id})\pi_{j}(\mathbf{R}_{r})-s(\nu_{N})\big)^{2}\big]\leq Cd_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b}_{0},\boldsymbol{b}_{N})^{1/4},

which implies from the definition of the martingales
M^{j,\boldsymbol{b}}
that

(A.22)

\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[N^{-1}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\bigg|\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[(Z_{r+1}^{\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}-Z_{r}^{\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{b}_{N}})^{2}-\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{k}a_{j}\bullet(R^{j}_{r+1}-R^{j}_{r}-s(\nu_{N}))\bigg)^{2}\bigg|\mathcal{F}_{r}\bigg]\bigg|\bigg]\leq Cd_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b}_{0},\boldsymbol{b}_{N})^{1/4}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle N\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}0.

To prove this, one expands out

\displaystyle(Z_{r+1}^{\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}

\displaystyle-Z_{r}^{\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{b}_{N}})^{2}=\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{k}a_{j}\bullet(R^{j}_{r+1}-R^{j}_{r}-s(\nu_{N}))\bigg)^{2}

\displaystyle+2\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{k}a_{j}\bullet(R^{j}_{r+1}-R^{j}_{r}-s(\nu_{N}))\bigg)\big((P^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}-\mathrm{Id})\pi_{j}(\mathbf{R}_{r})-s(\nu_{N})\big)+\big((P^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}-\mathrm{Id})\pi_{j}(\mathbf{R}_{r})-s(\nu_{N})\big)^{2},

then one uses (
A.21
). With (
A.22
) established, set
\mathrm{var}^{\mathbf{a}}_{N}:=\int_{I^{k}}\big(\sum_{j=1}^{k}a_{j}\bullet(u_{j}-s(\nu_{N}))\big)^{2}\nu_{N}^{\otimes k}(\mathrm{d}u).
Then using similar arguments to (
A.19
) we also have that

\displaystyle\bigg|\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}

\displaystyle\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{k}a_{j}\bullet(R^{j}_{r+1}-R^{j}_{r}-s(\nu_{N}))\bigg)^{2}\bigg|\mathcal{F}_{r}\bigg]\;\;-\;\;\mathrm{var}^{\mathbf{a}}_{N}\bigg|

\displaystyle=\bigg|\int_{I^{k}}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{k}a_{j}\bullet(y_{j}-R^{j}_{r}-s(\nu_{N}))\bigg)^{2}\boldsymbol{b}_{N}(\mathbf{R}_{r},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})\;\;-\;\;\mathrm{var}^{\mathbf{a}}_{N}\bigg|\leq C\cdot d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b}_{N},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})^{1/4}F\big(\min_{i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}}|R_{r}^{i^{\prime}}-R_{r}^{j^{\prime}}|\big)^{1/4}.

This implies that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[N^{-1}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\bigg|\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}

\displaystyle\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{k}a_{j}\bullet(R^{j}_{r+1}-R^{j}_{r}-s(\nu_{N}))\bigg)^{2}\bigg|\mathcal{F}_{r}\bigg]\;\;-\;\;\mathrm{var}^{\mathbf{a}}_{N}\bigg|\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b}_{N},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})^{1/4}.\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[N^{-1}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\sum_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}F\big(|R^{i^{\prime}}_{r}-R^{j^{\prime}}_{r}|\big)^{1/4}\bigg]

(A.23)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle N\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}0.

where we applied (
A.4
) and
d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{N})\to 0
in the last line. Combining (
A.22
) and (
A.23
), and recalling that the covariance matrix of the base measure
\nu
of
\boldsymbol{b}_{0}
is
\mathrm{Id}_{d\times d}
, we have that
\mathrm{var}^{\mathbf{a}}_{N}\to|\mathbf{a}|^{2}
as
N\to\infty
we find that

(A.24)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[\bigg||\mathbf{a}|^{2}t\;\;\;-\;\;\;N^{-1}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\big[(Z_{r+1}^{\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}-Z_{r}^{\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{b}_{N}})^{2}\big|\mathcal{F}_{r}\big]\bigg|\bigg]=0.

Using (
A.24
), one may immediately conclude that the joint limit point
(U,V)
of the pair of processes
(N^{-1/2}Z^{\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}_{Nt},N^{-1}Y^{\mathbf{a},\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}_{Nt})_{t\geq 0}
must satisfy
V_{t}=U_{t}^{2}-|\mathbf{a}|^{2}t
. Since both are continuous martingales as explained above, and since this holds true for all choices of
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{kd}
, it must be true that
(U^{1},...,U^{k})
is a standard Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}^{kd}
.
∎

Appendix B
Proof of Theorem
4.7
: Heat kernel bounds for SRI chains

Lemma B.1
.

Let
m\geq 1
and
z_{0}>0
and
K>0
. Let
\mathscr{A}_{\gamma}(m)
denote the set of all probability measures
\rho
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with mean zero, with covariance matrix
\mathrm{Id}_{d\times d}
, and satisfying
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{3}\rho(\mathrm{d}x)<\gamma
. There exists a constant
C=C(d,z_{0},K)>0
such that for all
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
one has

\sup_{\rho\in\mathscr{A}_{\gamma}(d)}\nu^{*r}(\{x:|x-y|\leq 1\})\leq Cr^{-d/2}\big(1+r^{-1/2}|y-x|\big)^{-K}.

Here
\rho^{*r}
denotes the
r
-fold convolution of
\rho
with itself.

Proof.

We will effectively follow the proof of “Esseen’s concentration inequality,” which is actually an anti-concentration inequality. Choose
\gamma=\gamma(z_{0},M)>0
(small enough) so that
|\widehat{\rho}(\xi)|\leq e^{-\frac{1}{4}|\xi|^{2}}
for
|\xi|<\gamma.
This is permissible since the covariance matrix of
\rho
is
\mathrm{Id}_{d\times d}
. Next, choose a smooth even function
H
supported on
[-\gamma,\gamma]
, and let
U
be the Fourier transform of
H*H
. By Plancherel and then a change of variable, we have that

\int_{I}U(x-y)\rho^{*r}(\mathrm{d}x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\xi\bullet y}H(\xi)^{2}\widehat{\rho}(\xi)^{r}\mathrm{d}\xi=r^{-d/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\xi\bullet y/\sqrt{r}}|H(\xi/\sqrt{r})|^{2}\widehat{\rho}(\xi/\sqrt{r})^{r}\mathrm{d}\xi.

We need to show that the integral is bounded above by
C\big(1+r^{-1/2}|y|\big)^{-K}
for some large enough constant
C=C(M,z_{0},K).
To do this, we will actually show that the integral is bounded above by
C\min\{1,r^{K/2}|y|^{-K}\},
which is an equivalent bound. The upper bound of 1 is clear since
H
being supported on
[-\gamma,\gamma]
implies
|H(\xi/\sqrt{r})|^{2}|\widehat{\rho}(\xi/\sqrt{r})|^{r}\leq Ce^{-\frac{1}{4}|\xi|^{2}}.
For the upper bound of
r^{K/2}|y|^{-K}
, integrate by parts exactly
K
times to yield that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\xi\bullet y/\sqrt{r}}|H(\xi/\sqrt{r})|^{2}\widehat{\rho}(\xi/\sqrt{r})^{r}\mathrm{d}\xi=\bigg(-\frac{i\sqrt{r}}{|y|}\bigg)^{K}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\xi\bullet y/\sqrt{r}}\partial_{\xi}^{K}\bigg(|H(\xi/\sqrt{r})|^{2}\widehat{\rho}(\xi/\sqrt{r})^{r}\bigg)\mathrm{d}\xi.

Now we will take absolute values on both sides. If we can prove that
\big|\partial_{\xi}^{K}\big(|H(\xi/\sqrt{r})|^{2}\widehat{\rho}(\xi/\sqrt{r})^{r}\big)\big|\leq C(1+|\xi|^{m})e^{-\frac{1}{8}|\xi|^{2}},
for some constant
C=C(K,z_{0},M)>0
, then this would complete the proof of the claim. Since
H
is smooth and compactly supported, it is clear that the first
K
derivatives of the function
\xi\mapsto|H(\xi/\sqrt{r})|^{2}
are bounded independently of
\xi
and
r
, thus by the product rule for derivatives, we just need to obtain a sufficiently strong bound on
\big|\partial_{\xi}^{K}\big(\widehat{\rho}(\xi/\sqrt{r})^{r}\big)\big|
, that is independent of
r\geq 0
and
|\xi|<\gamma\sqrt{r}
.

We will now show that
\big|\partial_{\xi}^{K}\big(\widehat{\rho}(\xi/\sqrt{r})^{r}\big)\big|\leq C(1+|\xi|^{K})e^{-\frac{1}{8}|\xi|^{2}}
for
|\xi|\leq\gamma
, and then we will be finished. When
K=0
, we already know this to be true by construction. Abbreviate
\phi:=\widehat{\rho}
, and
\phi^{(\ell)}:=\partial_{\xi}^{\ell}\phi
. Since
\rho
is assumed to have moments of all orders, we have
\max_{1\leq\ell\leq m}\|\phi^{(\ell)}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C.
Thus at each instance of differentiation, notice that the only seemingly problematic terms that could arise are derivatives of terms of the form
\phi(\xi/\sqrt{r})^{r-\ell}
with
\ell<K
. The derivative of such a term is
(r-\ell)\phi(\xi/\sqrt{r})^{r-\ell-1}\phi^{\prime}(\xi/\sqrt{r})\cdot r^{-1/2}.
Since the measure
\rho
has mean zero, one has that
|\phi^{\prime}(\xi)|\leq C|\xi|
for
|\xi|<\gamma
. We thus obtain that
(r-\ell)\phi(\xi/\sqrt{r})^{r-\ell-1}\phi^{\prime}(\xi/\sqrt{r})\cdot r^{-1/2}\leq C|\xi|\phi(\xi/\sqrt{r})^{r-\ell-1}\leq C|\xi|e^{-\frac{r-\ell-1}{r}\cdot\frac{1}{4}|\xi|^{2}}
for
|\xi|<\gamma\sqrt{r}
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that
r\geq 2K
since controlling some finite set of
r
-values is vacuous (just making
C
larger if necessary), which means
\frac{r-\ell-1}{r}\geq\frac{1}{2}
for
\ell<K,
so that
e^{-\frac{r-\ell-1}{r}\cdot\frac{1}{4}|\xi|^{2}}\leq e^{-\frac{1}{8}|\xi|^{2}}.
∎

Lemma B.2
.

Consider any adapted sequence of random variables
(X_{n})_{n\geq 0}
defined on any filtered probability space
(\Omega,(\mathcal{F}_{r})_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}},P),
with
M_{0}=0
. Assume that we have
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda_{0}}(M;P):=\sup_{n\geq 1}\|E[e^{4\lambda_{0}|X_{n+1}-X_{n}|}|\mathcal{F}_{n}]\big\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},P)}<\infty,
for some
\lambda_{0}>0
. Then for
|\lambda|<\lambda_{0}
we have that

(B.1)

E[e^{\lambda X_{r}}]\leq E\bigg[\prod_{s=0}^{r-1}E[e^{2\lambda(X_{s+1}-X_{s})}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]\bigg]^{1/2}.

If in particular
X
is a martingale, then for
|\lambda|<\lambda_{0}
we have that

\displaystyle E[e^{\lambda X_{r}}]

\displaystyle\leq E\bigg[\prod_{s=0}^{r-1}\big(1+4\lambda^{2}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda_{0}}(M,P)^{1/2}E[|X_{s+1}-X_{s}|^{4}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]^{1/2}\big)\bigg]^{1/2}

(B.2)

\displaystyle\leq E\bigg[e^{4\lambda^{2}\mathcal{E}_{\lambda_{0}}(M,P)^{1/2}\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}E\big[|X_{s+1}-X_{s}|^{4}\big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\big]^{1/2}}\bigg]^{1/2}

Proof.

Fix
\lambda>0
, and let
W_{r}:=\sum_{n=0}^{r-1}\log E[e^{2\lambda(X_{n+1}-X_{n})}|\mathcal{F}_{n}],
and notice that
e^{2\lambda X_{r}-W_{r}}
is a
P
-martingale. Therefore by Cauchy-Schwartz we have that

E[e^{\lambda X_{r}}]=E[e^{\lambda X_{r}-\frac{1}{2}W_{r}}e^{\frac{1}{2}W_{r}}]\leq E[e^{2\lambda X_{r}-W_{r}}]^{1/2}E[e^{W_{r}}]^{1/2}=1\cdot E[e^{W_{r}}]^{1/2},

which already proves (
B.1
). To prove the first bound in (
B.2
), we just need to show that for
|\lambda|<\lambda_{0}
one has

E[e^{2\lambda(X_{s+1}-X_{s})}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]\leq 1+4\lambda^{2}E[e^{4|\lambda||X_{s+1}-X_{s}|}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]^{1/2}\cdot E[|X_{s+1}-X_{s}|^{4}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]^{1/2}.

By Cauchy-Schwartz it suffices to show the weaker bound

E[e^{2\lambda(X_{s+1}-X_{s})}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]\leq 1+4\lambda^{2}E[(X_{s+1}-X_{s})^{2}e^{2|\lambda(X_{s+1}-X_{s})|}|\mathcal{F}_{s}].

But this is immediate from the elementary inequality
|e^{u}-1-u|\leq u^{2}e^{|u|},
and the assumption that
E[X_{s+1}-X_{s}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]=0.

The second bound of (
B.2
) then just follows from the trivial bound
1+a\leq e^{a},
applied to each term in the product.
∎

Lemma B.3
.

Consider decreasing functions
F,H:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
of exponential decay at infinity. Let
q\geq 2,M>0
and let
\boldsymbol{b}_{0}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(q,M,F)
be centered and
\delta
-repulsive for some
\delta>0
. Then there exists
\epsilon>0
such that for all
\lambda>0
one has that

(B.3)

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(q,M,F)\\
d(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})<\epsilon\end{subarray}}\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}}\sup_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[e^{\lambda r^{-1/2}\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\sum_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}H(|R^{i^{\prime}}_{s}-R^{j^{\prime}}_{s}|)}]<\infty.

We remark that this will only serve as an initial bound, not the optimal one by any means (as is indicated by the fact that the bound does not depend on
d
in any way). More precisely, it is only optimal in
d=1
, or more generally when the covariance matrix of
\nu
has rank 1, i.e.,
\nu
is supported on a dimension-one subgroup of
I
.

Proof.

One first Taylor expands the exponential as an infinite series. It then suffices to show that there exists
C>0
such that uniformly over all
d(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})<\epsilon
, all
m\in\mathbb{N}
, and all integers
r\geq 0
one has

(B.4)

\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\sum_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}H(|R^{i^{\prime}}_{s}-R^{j^{\prime}}_{s}|)\bigg)^{m}\bigg]\leq C^{m}\sqrt{m!}\cdot r^{m/2},

with the understanding that
s_{0}=0
. Multiplying both sides by
r^{-m/2}\gamma^{2m}/m!
and summing over
m
will then imply the claim.

To prove (
B.4
), use induction on
m
. For the
m=1
case, the bound in (
B.4
) is immediate from (
A.4
) with
q=1
.

For the inductive step, let us define
\varrho_{m}
to be the optimal constant so that the left side of (
B.4
) is bounded above by
\varrho_{m}r^{m/2}
uniformly over all
r\geq 0
. We have

\displaystyle h_{m}(r;\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{s_{1},s_{2},...,s_{m}=0}^{r-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{m}H(|R^{i^{\prime}}_{s_{j}}-R^{j^{\prime}}_{s_{j}}|)\bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq s_{1},s_{2},...,s_{m}<r\\
\mathrm{no\;repeated\;index}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{m}H(|R^{i^{\prime}}_{s_{j}}-R^{j^{\prime}}_{s_{j}}|)\bigg]+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq s_{1},s_{2},...,s_{m}<r\\
\mathrm{repeated\;index}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{m}H(|R^{i^{\prime}}_{s_{j}}-R^{j^{\prime}}_{s_{j}}|)\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq m!\cdot\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\bigg[\sum_{0\leq s_{1}<...<s_{m}<r}\prod_{j=1}^{m}H(|R^{i^{\prime}}_{s_{j}}-R^{j^{\prime}}_{s_{j}}|)\bigg]+\|H\|_{\infty}h_{m-1}(r;\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=m!\cdot\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\bigg[\sum_{s_{1}=0}^{r-1}H(|R^{i^{\prime}}_{s_{1}}-R^{j^{\prime}}_{s_{1}}|)\cdot\frac{1}{(m-1)!}h_{m-1}(r-s_{1};\mathbf{R}_{s_{1}})]\bigg]+\|H\|_{\infty}h_{m-1}(r;\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\leq m\varrho_{m-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\bigg[\sum_{s_{1}=0}^{r-1}H(|R^{i^{\prime}}_{s_{1}}-R^{j^{\prime}}_{s_{1}}|)\cdot(r-s_{1})^{(m-1)/2}\bigg]+\|H\|_{\infty}h_{m-1}(r;\mathbf{x})

We used the Markov property in the fourth line. Now for the first term on the right side, use summation by parts and we obtain that

\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}H(|R^{i^{\prime}}_{s}-R^{j^{\prime}}_{s}|)\cdot(r-s)^{(m-1)/2}=\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\big[(r-s)^{(m-1)/2}-(r-1-s)^{(m-1)/2}\big]\cdot\bigg(\sum_{t=0}^{s-1}H(|R^{i^{\prime}}_{t}-R^{j^{\prime}}_{t}|)\bigg).

Now note that
(r-s)^{(m-1)/2}-(r-1-s)^{(m-1)/2}
can be bounded above by
C(\frac{m-1}{2})(r-s)^{(m-3)/2}
, then apply the expectation
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}
over the last expression, then use the
m=1
case once again, and we see that the expectation is bounded above by
C(\frac{m-1}{2})\cdot\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}(r-s)^{(m-3)/2}s^{1/2}
which is equal to
r^{m/2}
multiplied by a Riemann sum approximation for
\int_{0}^{1}(1-u)^{(m-3)/2}u^{1/2}du
. Notice that the latter is
O(m^{-3/2})
as
m\to\infty.
This whole discussion yields

\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\bigg[\sum_{s_{1}=0}^{r-1}H(|R^{i^{\prime}}_{s_{1}}-R^{j^{\prime}}_{s_{1}}|)\cdot(r-s_{1})^{(m-1)/2}\bigg]\leq Cm^{-1/2}r^{m/2}

for some absolute constant
C
not depending on
m,r
.

As for the second term
\|H\|_{\infty}h_{m-1}(r;\mathbf{x})
, we can absorb
\|H\|_{\infty}
into some absolute constant
C
since
F
is just a fixed function. By the inductive hypothesis,
Ch_{m-1}(r;\mathbf{x})
may then be bounded above by
C\varrho_{m-1}r^{(m-1)/2}\leq C\varrho_{m-1}r^{m/2}.

The entire above discussion yields the relation
\varrho_{m}\leq Cm^{1/2}\varrho_{m-1}
for some absolute constant
C
independent of
m\in\mathbb{N},
which easily implies (
B.4
). ∎

Definition B.4
.

Let
k,d\in\mathbb{N}
. Suppose that
\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(\Psi_{\sigma},M,F)
, and define

(B.5)

\mathrm{Dr}_{\boldsymbol{b}}(\mathbf{x}):=\bigg|\int_{I^{k}}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x})\boldsymbol{b}(\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})-\mathbf{s}(\nu_{\boldsymbol{b}})\bigg|.

Note that this is 0 for any centered chain
\boldsymbol{b}
. Here
\mathbf{s}(\nu_{\boldsymbol{b}})=\int_{I^{k}}\mathbf{u}\nu_{\boldsymbol{b}}(\mathrm{d}u)
is the deterministic drift vector as used in Theorem
4.6
.

Typically, the underlying chain will be clear, and we will just write
\mathrm{Dr}_{\boldsymbol{b}}(\mathbf{x})=\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{x})
.

The following proposition will be an intermediate step towards the proof of Theorem
4.7
.

Proposition B.5
.

Fix
K,\delta,\beta>0
, and let
\boldsymbol{b}_{0}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(\Psi_{\sigma},M,F)
be centered and
\delta
-repulsive. Then there is a
\epsilon=\epsilon(F,z_{0},M,\delta)>0
and
C=C(F,\sigma,\delta,K)>0
such that uniformly over
\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}
and
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{kd}
and integers
r\geq 0
one has the bound: We have

\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\bigg[\bigg(1+r^{-\beta}|\mathbf{R}_{r}-r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|\bigg)^{-K}e^{-r^{-\beta}\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{R}_{s})}\bigg]\leq C^{m}r^{-\frac{kd}{2}\big(1-2\beta\big)}\big(1+r^{-1/2}|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}|\big)^{-K}.

Note that if
\beta=0
, this would prove the first bound in Theorem
4.7
, but unfortunately, we cannot obtain this strong of a bound just yet. The point is that we can still get close, with
\beta
as close to 0 as we want.

Note that in
d=1
, we want
\beta>1/2
on the left-hand side to kill the sum in the exponential, which is of order
r^{1/2}
. However, such a
\beta
gives a bad bound on the RHS.

Proof.

Throughout the proof, we will assume as always that
q,M,F
as well as
\boldsymbol{b}_{0}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(\Psi_{\sigma},M,F)
are fixed, with
\boldsymbol{b}_{0}
centered and
\delta
-repulsive.

Step 1.
In this step, we construct a coupled Markov chain under which the first marginal is the SRI chain
\boldsymbol{b}
of interest, and the second marginal just a random walk with increment law
\nu_{\boldsymbol{b}}^{\otimes k}
, and the increments of both coordinates are equal with high probability away from the boundaries of the “
(k-1)d
-dimensional Weyl chamber.”

For now, fix some
\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(q,M,F)
. Using the total variation bound in tshe definition of SRI chain, consider any Markov kernel
\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{b}}((\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}),(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}_{2}))
on
I^{2k}
with the following properties:

•

The first marginal of
\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{b}}((\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}),\bullet)
is equal to
\boldsymbol{b}(\mathbf{x}_{1},\bullet)
.

•

The second marginal of
\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{b}}((\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}),\bullet)
is
\nu_{\boldsymbol{b}}^{\otimes k}(\mathbf{x}_{2}-\bullet).

•

We have that
\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{b}}((\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}),\{(\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{y}_{2}):\mathbf{y}_{2}-\mathbf{y}_{1}\neq\mathbf{x}_{2}-\mathbf{x}_{1}\})\leq 2F(\min_{i<j}|x_{1}^{i}-x_{1}^{j}|)
where
\mathbf{x}_{1}=(x^{1}_{1},x_{1}^{2},...,x_{1}^{k})
in coordinates.

Such a coupling is possible simply by the coupling definition of total variation distance and the conditions in the definition of SRI chains. Denote by
(\mathbf{R}_{r},\mathbf{S}_{r})
the canonical process on
(I^{2k})^{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}},
and let
\mathbf{E}^{\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{b}}}_{(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2})}
denote the canonical measure on that space, under which
(\mathbf{R}_{r},\mathbf{S}_{r})
is distributed as the Markov chain started from
(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2})
with transition kernels
\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{b}}((\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}),\bullet).
Under
\mathbf{E}^{\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{b}}}_{(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2})}
, notice that the marginal law of
\mathbf{R}_{r}
is simply that of the SRI chain associated to
\boldsymbol{b}
started from
\mathbf{x}_{1}
, and the marginal law of
\mathbf{S}_{r}
is that of a random walk on
I^{k}
with increment law
\nu_{\boldsymbol{b}}^{\otimes k}
.

Step 2.
Henceforth denote

\mathbf{X}_{r}:=\mathbf{R}_{r}-\mathbf{S}_{r}.

If
q\geq 1
and
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we may use Cauchy-Schwartz to say that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}^{\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{b}}}_{(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2})}\big[|\mathbf{X}_{s+1}-\mathbf{X}_{s}|^{q/2}\big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{E}^{\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{b}}}_{(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2})}\big[|\mathbf{X}_{s+1}-\mathbf{X}_{s}|^{q}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]^{1/2}\mathbf{P}^{\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{b}}}_{(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2})}\big(\mathbf{X}_{s+1}-\mathbf{X}_{s}\neq 0\big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq(Cq)^{q/2}\mathbf{P}^{\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{b}}}_{(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2})}\big(\mathbf{X}_{s+1}-\mathbf{X}_{s}\neq 0\big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq(Cq)^{q/2}F\big(\min_{i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}}|R^{i^{\prime}}_{s}-R^{j^{\prime}}_{s}|\big)^{1/2}

(B.6)

\displaystyle\leq(Cq)^{q/2}\sum_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}F\big(|R^{i^{\prime}}_{s}-R^{j^{\prime}}_{s}|\big)^{1/2}.

Here
C
is a large constant that independent of
q\geq 1
,
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(\Psi_{\sigma},M,F),
and
\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}\in I^{k}.
Note here that we are using the exponential tail bound inherent in Definition
4.5
to bound the
q^{th}
moment by
(Cq)^{q}
for some absolute constant
C.
In the last line, we are using that
F
is decreasing.

Step 3.
In this step, we define some useful martingales and obtain some preliminary bounds associated to them. We can define the martingale
\mathbf{M}_{r}:=\mathbf{X}_{r}-\mathbf{D}_{r},
where

\mathbf{D}_{r}:=\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathbf{E}^{\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{b}}}_{(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2})}\big[\mathbf{X}_{s+1}-\mathbf{X}_{s}\big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\big].

Note the decomposition

(B.7)

\mathbf{R}_{r}=\mathbf{S}_{r}+\mathbf{M}_{r}+\mathbf{D}_{r}

which will be crucial to our analysis.

Since
\mathbf{M}
is a martingale, Lemma
B.2
(together with the exponential moment assumption inherent in Definition
4.5
) yields for
\lambda>0
that

(B.8)

\sup_{\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}\in I^{k}}\sup_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}\mathbf{E}^{\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{b}}}_{(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2})}[e^{\lambda r^{-1/4}|\mathbf{M}_{r}|}]\leq\sup_{\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}\in I^{k}}\sup_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}\mathbf{E}^{\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{b}}}_{(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2})}[e^{C\lambda^{2}r^{-1/2}\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}E[|\mathbf{M}_{s+1}-\mathbf{M}_{s}|^{4}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]}]<\infty,

where the finiteness of the last supremum follows from (
B.6
) (with
q=8
) and (
B.3
), and the trivial fact that by definition, one has
|\mathbf{M}_{s+1}-\mathbf{M}_{s}|^{4}\leq 2^{4}|\mathbf{X}_{s+1}-\mathbf{X}_{s}|^{4}+2^{4}\mathbf{E}^{\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{b}}}_{(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2})}[|\mathbf{X}_{s+1}-\mathbf{X}_{s}|^{4}|\mathcal{F}_{s}].

Step 4.
In this step, we obtain a preliminary anticoncentration estimate for
|\mathbf{R}_{r}|
under the measure
\mathbf{E}^{\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{b}}}_{(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2})}
. In the notation of Theorem
4.7
, notice that
|\mathbf{D}_{s+1}-\mathbf{D}_{s}|=\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{R}_{s}).
Using the results of the previous steps, we thus have uniformly over all
d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})<\epsilon
and
r\geq 0
and
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}\in I^{k}
one has

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}^{\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{b}}}_{(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2})}

\displaystyle\bigg[e^{-r^{-1/3}|\mathbf{R}_{r}-r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|-r^{-1/3}\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{R}_{s})}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{E}^{\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{b}}}_{(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2})}\bigg[e^{-r^{-1/3}\big(|\mathbf{R}_{r}-r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|+|\mathbf{D}_{r}|\big)}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{E}^{\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{b}}}_{(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2})}\bigg[e^{-r^{-1/3}\big(|\mathbf{S}_{r}-r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|-|\mathbf{M}_{r}|\big)}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}^{\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{b}}}_{(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2})}\bigg[e^{-r^{-1/3}\big(|\mathbf{S}_{r}-r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|-|\mathbf{M}_{r}|\big)}\mathbf{1}_{\{|\mathbf{M}_{r}|\leq r^{1/3}\}}\bigg]+\mathbf{E}^{\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{b}}}_{(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2})}\bigg[e^{-r^{-1/3}\big(|\mathbf{S}_{r}-r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|-|\mathbf{M}_{r}|\big)}\mathbf{1}_{\{|\mathbf{M}_{r}|>r^{1/3}\}}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq e\cdot\mathbf{E}^{\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{b}}}_{(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2})}\big[e^{-r^{-1/3}|\mathbf{S}_{r}-r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|}\big]+\mathbf{E}^{\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{b}}}_{(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2})}\big[e^{2r^{-1/3}|\mathbf{M}_{r}|}\big]^{1/2}\mathbf{P}^{\boldsymbol{v}_{\boldsymbol{b}}}_{(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2})}\big(|\mathbf{M}_{r}|>r^{1/3}\big)^{1/2}

(B.9)

\displaystyle\leq Cr^{-kd/6}\big(1+r^{-1/2}|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}|\big)^{-K}+Ce^{-r^{\frac{1}{12}}}.

Indeed the second line has already been explained. The fourth line follows from (
B.7
). The fifth line is just Cauchy-Schwartz. The final line follows from Lemma
B.1
and the result (
B.8
) of Step 3.

Now we are going to argue why the stretch-exponential term can be disregarded. Assume without loss of generality that
K>10kd
since the theorem for some
K
implies the theorem for any smaller
K
(and we are claiming that the bound in the theorem holds for all
K>0
). If
|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|<r^{K}
then it is clear that
e^{-r^{\frac{1}{12}}}\leq Cr^{-kd/6}r^{-(K-\frac{1}{2})}\leq C^{\prime}r^{-kd/6}(1+r^{-1/2}|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|)^{-K}
. On the other hand, if
|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|\geq r^{K}
then
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[e^{-r^{-1/3}|\mathbf{R}_{r}-r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|}]\leq e^{-r^{-1/3}|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[e^{+r^{-1/3}\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}|\mathbf{R}_{s+1}-\mathbf{R}_{s}-\mathbf{s}_{N}|}]\leq e^{-r^{-1/3}|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|+Cr^{2/3}},
where the expectation being bounded above by
e^{Cr^{2/3}}
can be deduced by iteratively applying the Markov property and the definition of what it means for
\boldsymbol{b}
to be an element of
\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(\Psi_{\sigma},M,F)
. Now
|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|>r^{K}
and
K>2
means that
Cr^{2/3}=Cr^{-4/3}\cdot r^{2}\leq Cr^{-4/3}\cdot|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|.
Thus the last expression can be bounded above by
e^{-r^{-1/3}|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|+Cr^{-4/3}|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|}\leq C^{\prime}e^{-\frac{1}{2}r^{-1/3}|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|}.
Again using
|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|>r^{K}
it is not hard to see that this can be bounded above by
Cr^{-kd/6}\big(1+r^{-1/2}|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|\big)^{-K},
for example since one has that
e^{-\frac{1}{2}r^{-1/3}|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|}\leq e^{-\frac{1}{2}r^{-1/3}|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|^{2/3}}
and then using the fact that the exponential decays faster than any polynomial.

The last two paragraphs combined with (
B.9
) yield the bound that

\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\bigg[e^{-r^{-1/3}|\mathbf{R}_{r}-r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|-r^{-1/3}\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{R}_{s})}\bigg]\leq Cr^{-kd/6}\big(1+r^{-1/2}|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}|\big)^{-K}.

Assuming without loss of generality that
K>kd
, we have that

(1+r^{-1/3}|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}|)^{-K}\leq C\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{kd}}e^{-r^{-1/3}|\mathbf{a}-\mathbf{x}|}(1+|\mathbf{a}-\mathbf{y}|)^{-K},

and for similar reasons we also have

\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{kd}}(1+|\mathbf{a}-\mathbf{y}|)^{-K}(1+r^{-1/2}|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{a}|)^{-K}\leq C(1+r^{-1/2}|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|)^{-K},

thus from the last expression we obtain that

(B.10)

\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\bigg[\bigg(1+r^{-1/3}|\mathbf{R}_{r}-r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|\bigg)^{-K}e^{-r^{-1/3}\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{R}_{s})}\bigg]\leq Cr^{-kd/6}\big(1+r^{-1/2}|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}|\big)^{-K}.

Step 5.
Note that (
B.10
) yields a bound that is
optimal
at scales of size
r^{1/3}
from the origin, which is strictly better than one can do just using the invariance principle of Theorem
4.6
which only yields good bounds at scales
r^{1/2}.
In particular since
1/3
is smaller than
1/2
, the bound (
B.10
) can be repeatedly
iterated
using the Markov property to yield bounds at better and better scales, approaching (but not quite reaching) scales of size 1 which is what we ultimately want.

To illustrate the iteration, note that
\frac{2}{9}=\frac{1}{3}\cdot\frac{2}{3}
, thus we can use the Markov property and apply (
B.10
) twice to see that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\bigg[\bigg(1+r^{-2/9}|\mathbf{R}_{r}-r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|\bigg)^{-K}e^{-r^{-2/9}\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{R}_{s})}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\bigg[e^{-r^{-2/9}\sum_{s=0}^{r-r^{2/3}-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{R}_{s})}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \cdot\mathbf{E}^{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\mathbf{R}_{r-r^{2/3}}}\bigg[e^{-r^{-2/9}\sum_{s=0}^{r^{2/3}-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\tilde{\mathbf{R}}_{s})}\bigg(1+r^{-2/9}|\tilde{\mathbf{R}}_{r^{2/3}}-r^{2/3}\mathbf{s}_{N}-((r-r^{2/3})\mathbf{s}_{N}+\mathbf{y})|\bigg)^{-K}\bigg]\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq C\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\bigg[e^{-r^{-1/3}\sum_{s=0}^{r-r^{2/3}-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{R}_{s})}\cdot(r^{2/3})^{-kd/6}\bigg(1+r^{-1/3}|\mathbf{R}_{r-r^{2/3}}-(r-r^{2/3})\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|\bigg)^{-K}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq C^{2}r^{-\frac{kd}{6}\big(1+\frac{2}{3}\big)}\big(1+r^{-1/2}|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}|\big)^{-K}.

Above
\tilde{\mathbf{R}}
is an independent copy of the Markov chain. This is now the optimal bound on scale
r^{2/9}
, which is better than the
r^{1/3}
from (
B.10
). Keep doing this iteration repeatedly, and it will yield that for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
one has

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\bigg[\bigg(

\displaystyle 1+r^{-\frac{1}{3}\big(\frac{2}{3}\big)^{m}}|\mathbf{R}_{r}-r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|\bigg)^{-K}e^{-r^{-\frac{1}{3}\big(\frac{2}{3}\big)^{m}}\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{R}_{s})}\bigg]\leq C^{m}r^{-\frac{kd}{6}\big(1+\frac{2}{3}+...+\big(\frac{2}{3}\big)^{m}\big)}\big(1+r^{-1/2}|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}|\big)^{-K}.

This implies the proposition for
\beta
of the form
\frac{1}{3}\big(\frac{2}{3}\big)^{m}
, with
m\in\mathbb{N}
, and the case of general
\beta
follows easily.
∎

Notice that we would really like to take
m=\infty
in the last bound, because
\frac{1}{6}\big(1+\frac{2}{3}+...+\big(\frac{2}{3}\big)^{m}+...\big)=\frac{1}{2}
which is the optimal bound as claimed in the theorem, however the problem is that the constant
C^{m}
blows up. In fact, one may convince themselves that only
m=\log\log r
iterations are needed, but even then
C^{m}
blows up like
(\log r)^{C^{\prime}}
which is bad despite being extremely close to optimal. Thus some new idea is needed, where we can actually truncate the above bound at some
fixed
value of
m
, then yield the optimal bound immediately afterwards. Using this idea, we now prove Theorem
4.7
.

Proof of Theorem
4.7
.

Step 1.
Fix arbitrary
\beta>0
. Combining the results of Lemma
B.1
and Proposition
B.5
yields the relation

(B.11)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[E[e^{-|\mathbf{R}_{r}+\mathbf{S}_{r^{2\beta}}-(r+r^{2\beta})\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|}]e^{-r^{-\beta}\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{R}_{s})}]\leq Cr^{-kd/2}(1+r^{-1/2}|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}|)^{-K},

where
\mathbf{S}
is a random walk with increment law
\nu^{\otimes k}
that is independent of
\mathbf{R}
, and
E
denotes an expectation over
\mathbf{S}
only (conditional on
\mathbf{R})
. In other words, appending a random walk of length
r^{2\beta}
to the
end
of the trajectory of
\mathbf{R}
yields a bound of the desired type. To prove this, condition on
\mathbf{R}
