and take the expectation just over
\mathbf{S}
first, then Lemma
B.1
shows that
E[e^{-|\mathbf{R}_{r}+\mathbf{S}_{r^{2\beta}}-(r+r^{2\beta})\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|}]\leq Cr^{-\beta kd}(1+r^{-\beta}|\mathbf{R}_{r}-r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|)^{-K}.
Then one applies Proposition
B.5
.

We claim that appending a random walk of length
r^{2\beta}
to the
beginning
of the trajectory of
\mathbf{R}
also yields a bound of the desired type, as long as the initial point
\mathbf{x}
is far from the boundary of the Weyl chamber. More precisely, if
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},...,x_{k})
and if
\min_{i<j}|x_{i}-x_{j}|>r^{2\beta}
we claim that

(B.12)

\displaystyle E[\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{S}_{r^{2\beta}}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[e^{-|\mathbf{R}_{r}-(r+r^{2\beta})\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|-r^{-\beta}\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{R}_{s})}]]\leq Cr^{-kd/2}(1+r^{-1/2}|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}|)^{-K}.

To prove this, define the function
h_{r}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}):=\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[e^{-|\mathbf{R}_{r}-r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|-r^{-\beta}\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{R}_{s})}],
so by Proposition
B.5
and the fact that
e^{-|\mathbf{R}_{r}-r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|}\leq e^{-r^{-\beta}|\mathbf{R}_{r}-r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|},
we get that
h_{r}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\leq Cr^{-\frac{kd}{2}(1-2\beta)}(1+r^{-1/2}|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|)^{-K},
and thus we can write the left side of (
B.12
) as

E[h_{r}(\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{S}_{r^{2\beta}},\mathbf{y}+r^{2\beta}\mathbf{s}_{N})]\leq Cr^{-\frac{kd}{2}(1-2\beta)}E[(1+r^{-1/2}|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}+\mathbf{S}_{r^{2\beta}}-r^{2\beta}\mathbf{s}_{N}|)^{-K}].

Since we can take
K
as large as we like, the result of Lemma
B.1
implies that the expectation on the right side is bounded above by
C(r^{2\beta})^{-kd/2}(1+r^{-1/2}|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|)^{-K},
which in turn implies (
B.12
) as desired.

Step 2.
Fix
\beta>0
. We now claim that if
\min_{i<j}|x_{i}-x_{j}|>r^{2\beta}
or
if
\min_{i<j}|y_{i}-y_{j}|>r^{2\beta}
then one necessarily has

\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[e^{-|\mathbf{R}_{r}-r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|-r^{-\beta}\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{R}_{s})}]\leq Cr^{-kd/2}(1+r^{-1/2}|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|)^{-K},

in other words the optimal bound does indeed hold as long as
at least one
of
\mathbf{x}
or
\mathbf{y}
are sufficiently far from the boundary of the Weyl chamber (where sufficiently far means of distance at least
r^{2\beta}
at time
r
).

First consider the case where
\min_{i<j}|x_{i}-x_{j}|>r^{2\beta}
. In this case, just using the total variation bound in the definition of SRI chain, one can use a coupling to replace the trajectory
\mathbf{R}|_{[0,r^{2\beta}]}
with its initial condition plus the trajectory of the random walk
\mathbf{S}|_{[0,r^{2\beta}]}
, and the probability of any disagreement can be bounded above by
Ce^{-cr^{\beta}}
. Just as we explained after (
B.9
), the stretch exponential terms can be disregarded as they decay sufficiently rapidly. Then (
B.12
) immediately gives the claim.

Now consider the case where
\min_{i<j}|y_{i}-y_{j}|>r^{2\beta}
. Break the expectation into two pieces according to
\{|\mathbf{R}_{r}-r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|\geq\frac{1}{2}r^{2\beta}\}
and
\{|\mathbf{R}_{r}-r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|<\frac{1}{2}r^{2\beta}\}
. The first event has probability bounded above by by
Ce^{-cr^{\beta}}
. Just as we explained after (
B.9
), the stretch exponential terms can be disregarded as they decay sufficiently rapidly. For the second term one can use a coupling to replace the trajectory
\mathbf{R}|_{[r-r^{2\beta},r]}
with its initial condition plus the trajectory of the random walk
\mathbf{S}|_{[r-r^{2\beta},r]}
, and the probability of any disagreement can be bounded above by
Ce^{-cr^{\beta}}
. Thus (
B.11
) immediately gives the claim.

Step 3.
Every point in
\mathbb{R}^{kd}
is within distance 1 of some point in the lattice
\Lambda:=(kd)^{-1/2}\mathbb{Z}^{kd}
. Thus apply a union bound, and then use the Markov property to write

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[e

{}^{-|\mathbf{R}_{2r}-2r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|-r^{-\beta}\sum_{s=0}^{2r-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{R}_{s})}]\leq\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\Lambda}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[\mathbf{1}_{\{|\mathbf{R}_{r}-r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{a}|\leq 1\}}e^{-|\mathbf{R}_{2r}-\mathbf{y}|-r^{-\beta}\sum_{s=0}^{2r-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{R}_{s})}]]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\Lambda}\bigg(\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\big[\mathbf{1}_{\{|\mathbf{R}_{r}-r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{a}|\leq 1\}}e^{-r^{-\beta}\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{R}_{s})}\mathbf{E}^{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\mathbf{R}_{s}}[e^{-|\tilde{\mathbf{R}}_{r}-2r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|-r^{-\beta}\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\tilde{\mathbf{R}}_{s})}]\big]\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\Lambda}\bigg(\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[\mathbf{1}_{\{|\mathbf{R}_{r}-r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{a}|\leq 1\}}e^{-r^{-\beta}\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{R}_{s})}]\cdot\sup_{\mathbf{u}:|\mathbf{u}-r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{a}|\leq 1}\mathbf{E}^{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\mathbf{u}}[e^{-|\tilde{\mathbf{R}}_{r}-2r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|-r^{-\beta}\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\tilde{\mathbf{R}}_{s})}]\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq e\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\Lambda}\bigg(\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[e^{-|\mathbf{R}_{r}-r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{a}|}e^{-r^{-\beta}\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{R}_{s})}]\cdot\sup_{\mathbf{u}:|\mathbf{u}-r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{a}|\leq 1}\mathbf{E}^{\boldsymbol{b}}_{\mathbf{u}}[e^{-|\tilde{\mathbf{R}}_{r}-2r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|-r^{-\beta}\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\tilde{\mathbf{R}}_{s})}]\bigg)

Writing
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},...,a_{k})
, split the sum according to
\min_{i<j}|a_{i}-a_{j}|>r^{2\beta}
or
\min_{i<j}|a_{i}-a_{j}|\leq r^{2\beta}
. Write the corresponding sums as
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
. Note that
\mathbf{s}_{N}
is a vector of the form
(s_{N},...,s_{N})
for some
s_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, thus using the result of Step 2, we have that

I_{1}\leq e\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\Lambda}\bigg(r^{-kd/2}(1+r^{-1/2}|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{a}|)^{-K}\bigg)\bigg(r^{-kd/2}(1+r^{-1/2}|\mathbf{a}-\mathbf{y}|)^{-K}\bigg)\leq Cr^{-kd/2}(1+r^{-1/2}|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|)^{-K},

which is the required bound (note here:
K>kd
without loss of generality). Now let us bound
I_{2}
which is where we will need to make an explicit (and very small) choice of
\beta>0
. We have by Proposition
B.5
that

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle\leq e\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\in\Lambda\\
\min_{i<j}|a_{i}-a_{j}|\leq r^{2\beta}\end{subarray}}\bigg(r^{-\frac{kd}{2}(1-2\beta)}(1+r^{-1/2}|\mathbf{a}-\mathbf{x}|)\bigg)\bigg(r^{-\frac{kd}{2}(1-2\beta)}(1+r^{-1/2}|\mathbf{a}-\mathbf{y}|)\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq Cr^{3\beta kd}r^{(k-1)d/2}r^{-kd}\big(1+r^{-1/2}|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|\big)^{-K}

\displaystyle=r^{3\beta kd-\frac{d}{2}}r^{-kd/2}\big(1+r^{-1/2}|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|\big)^{-K}.

Now take any
\beta<1/(6k)
to obtain the bound

(B.13)

\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[e^{-|\mathbf{R}_{2r}-2r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|-r^{-\beta}\sum_{s=0}^{2r-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{R}_{s})}]\leq Cr^{-kd/2}\big(1+r^{-1/2}|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|\big)^{-K},

where
C
may depend on
K,\beta
but nothing else.

Step 4.
In this step, we prove the theorem. For
d=1
note that (
B.13
) already implies the given bound in the second bullet point in Theorem
4.7
, since
\mathrm{Dr}
vanishes for a centered chain. Thus we focus on
d\geq 2
.

Henceforth fix
\beta
as appearing in (
B.13
). From (
B.13
) and the decay conditions on
\mathrm{Dr}
we have

\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\bigg[\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{R}_{r})e^{-r^{-\beta}\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{R}_{s})}\bigg]\leq Cr^{-d/2},

for
r\geq 1
. Thus if
0\leq s_{1}\leq...\leq s_{m}\leq r
then by iterating this bound and using the Markov property, we have that

(B.14)

\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{R}_{s_{j}})e^{-r^{-\beta}\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{R}_{s})}\bigg]\leq C^{m}\prod_{j=0}^{m-1}1\wedge(s_{j+1}-s_{j})^{-d/2}.

The remainder of the proof will be split into cases depending on the dimension.

If
d\geq 2
, then (
B.14
) implies that e.g.

\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\bigg[\bigg(r^{-\beta/2}\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{R}_{s})\bigg)^{m}e^{-r^{-\beta}\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{R}_{s})}\bigg]\leq m!(C^{\prime}r^{-\beta/2}\log r)^{m}.

Divide both sides by
m!
and sum over
m
, and we will obtain that

\sup_{r\geq 0}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\bigg[e^{(r^{-\beta/2}-r^{-\beta})\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{R}_{s})}\bigg]<\infty.

Note that
r^{-\beta/2}-r^{-\beta}>\frac{1}{2}r^{-\beta/2}
for sufficiently large
r\geq 0
, so by Markov’s inequality, the last expression implies that

\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\bigg(\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{R}_{s})>r^{\beta}\bigg)=\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}r^{-\frac{\beta}{2}}\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{R}_{s})>\frac{1}{2}r^{\beta/2}\bigg)\leq Ce^{-\frac{1}{2}r^{\beta/2}}.

Going back to (
B.13
), we find that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[e^{-|\mathbf{R}_{2r}-2r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[e^{-|\mathbf{R}_{2r}-2r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|}\mathbf{1}_{\{\sum_{s=0}^{2r-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{R}_{s})>r^{\beta}\}}]+\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[e^{-|\mathbf{R}_{2r}-2r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|}\mathbf{1}_{\{\sum_{s=0}^{2r-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{R}_{s})\leq r^{\beta}\}}]

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{-\frac{1}{2}r^{\beta/2}}+e\cdot\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\boldsymbol{b}}[e^{-|\mathbf{R}_{2r}-2r\mathbf{s}_{N}-\mathbf{y}|-r^{-\beta}\sum_{s=0}^{2r-1}\mathrm{Dr}(\mathbf{R}_{s})}]

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{-\frac{1}{2}r^{\beta/2}}+Cr^{-kd/2}\big(1+r^{-1/2}|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|\big)^{-K}.

Just as we explained after (
B.9
), the stretch exponential terms can be disregarded as they decay sufficiently rapidly, which gives the claim.
∎

Appendix C
Proof of Theorem
4.10
: Invariant measures for SRI chains

We will see that the existence and uniqueness proofs of
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
will be different in dimensions
d=1,2
versus
d\geq 3.
This is because recurrence essentially gives automatic existence and uniqueness, while in the case of a transient SRI chain it is somewhat trickier to show both, since transient chains need not have invariant measures. Again we emphasize that
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
will be an infinite measure, not a probability measure (it is not an invariant
distribution
).

Lemma C.1
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
4.10
, the limiting chain
\boldsymbol{b}
is
\delta
-repulsive for some
\delta>0,
and centered.

Proof.

The centeredness is clear. To show
\delta
-repulsivity, notice from the symmetry assumptions that we just need to show

\inf_{x\in I}\int_{I}|y-x|\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}y)>0.

By Lemma
A.2
, one has that
\big|\int_{I}|y-x|\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}y)-\int_{I}|u|\nu(\mathrm{d}u)\bigg|\leq 2F(|x|),
and since
\nu
has covariance matrix
\mathrm{Id}_{d\times d}
this rules out the possibility of some sequence
x_{n}\in I
with
|x_{n}|\to\infty
such that
\int_{I}|y-x_{n}|\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x_{n},\mathrm{d}y)\to 0.
Thus we just need to show that for each compact set
K\subset I
one has
\inf_{x\in K}\int_{I}|y-x|\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}y)>0
. By the regularity assumptions on
\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}
, the latter is actually a continuous function of
x
, thus it achieves its infimum on each compact set. Thus if the infimum was zero, then there would be an absorbing state, contradicting the topological irreducibility assumption on
\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}
in Assumption
1.2
(
6
).
∎

Proposition C.2

(Existence/Uniqueness/nontriviality for
d=1,2
—recurrent case)
.

Let
d\leq 2
. With
\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}
as in Theorem
4.10
, there is a unique nontrivial invariant measure
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
up to scalar multiple.

Proof.

Let
d\leq 2
. The proof will proceed by construction of Lyapunov functions to show recurrence. By the continuity of
x\mapsto\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\bullet)
in total variation norm, the Markov kernel
\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}
is
Strong Feller,
which means that the associated Markov operator
P_{\mathbf{dif}}
sends bounded measurable functions to bounded continuous functions. In particular
\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}
is a “
T
-chain” in the sense of
[
MT93
, Definition 6.0.0 (iii)]
.

Furthermore
\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}
is “open-set irreducible” in the sense of
[
MT93
, Section 6.1.2]
by the assumptions of Theorem
4.10
. In particular, by
[
MT93
, Proposition 6.1.5]
the chain is “
\psi
-irreducible” where the maximal irreduciblilty measure
\psi
has full support due to the open-set irreducibility and the Strong Feller property.

We will now show that for all
x\in I
, there exists a compact set
K\subset I
such that the Markov chain
(X_{r})_{r\geq 0}
visits
K
infinitely often almost surely when started from
x
. In other words, the Markov kernel
\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}
is “non-evanescent” in the sense of
[
MT93
, Section 9.2.1]
. Now by
[
MT93
, Theorem 9.2.2 (ii)]
any non-evanescent
\psi
-irreducible
T
-chain is automatically
Harris recurrent
.

By
[
MT93
, Theorem 17.3.2]
, Harris recurrent chains can be characterized as exactly those chains for which a unique invariant measure
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
exists uniquely (up to scalar multiple). By
[
MT93
, Theorem 10.4.9]
, the measure
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
is equivalent to the maximal irreducibility measure
\psi
, which has already been explained to have full support, in particular it is a nonzero measure.

The manner in which we will whow the non-evanescence of the chain
\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}
is by showing that there exists a Lyapunov drift function
V
satisfying the so-called
(V1)
drift condition, that is the function has compact sublevel sets and furthermore one has
P_{\mathbf{dif}}V(x)\leq V(x)
for all
x
outside some compact set. By
[
MT93
, Theorem 9.4.1]
, this would imply the non-evanescence.

Existence of a Lyapunov drift function in
d=1
:
We set
V(x):=\sqrt{|x|+1},
and we let
P_{\mathbf{dif}}
denote the Markov operator associated to the Markov kernel
\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}
. We will show that
P_{\mathbf{dif}}V(x)\leq V(x)
for all positive and sufficiently large
x
, and the proof for negative
x
would be completely symmetric.

For
x>2
and
a\in(x/2,3x/2)
we can write

\displaystyle V(a)-V(x)

\displaystyle=V^{\prime}(x)(a-x)+\frac{1}{2}V^{\prime\prime}(x)(a-x)^{2}+R(a,x)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}(x+1)^{-1/2}(a-x)-\frac{1}{4}(x+1)^{-3/2}(a-x)^{2}+R(a,x),

where by Taylor’s remainder theorem one has uniformly over all
x>2
and
a\in(x/2,3x/2)
the bound

|R(a,x)|\leq\frac{1}{6}|a-x|^{3}\sup_{b\in[x/2,3x/2]}|V^{\prime\prime\prime}(b)|\leq\frac{3}{48}(x/2)^{-5/2}|a-x|^{3}.

Now recall that
\int_{I}(a-x)\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)=0
for all
x\in I
, which is immediate from the definitions. Consequently we can disregard the first-order term when calculating
P_{\mathbf{dif}}V
and we find that

\displaystyle P_{\mathbf{dif}}V(x)-V(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{I}(V(a)-V(x))\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{1}{4}(x+1)^{-3/2}\int_{I}(a-x)^{2}\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)+\frac{3}{48}(x/2)^{-5/2}\int_{I}|a-x|^{3}\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)

(C.1)

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;+\int_{I}|V(a)-V(x)|\mathbf{1}_{\{|x-a|>x/2\}}\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a).

Let
\sigma
be as the definition of the class
\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(\Psi_{\sigma},M,F)
, and let us define constants

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathrm{exp}}:

\displaystyle=\sup_{y\in I}\int_{I}e^{\frac{\sigma}{4}|a-y|}\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}(y,\mathrm{d}a),

\displaystyle\omega_{2}:

\displaystyle=\sup_{y\in I}\int_{I}(a-y)^{2}\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}(y,\mathrm{d}a),

\displaystyle\omega_{3}:

\displaystyle=\sup_{y\in I}\int_{I}|a-y|^{3}\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}(y,\mathrm{d}a),

\displaystyle\delta_{2}:

\displaystyle=\inf_{y\in I}\int_{I}(a-y)^{2}\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}(y,\mathrm{d}a).

The first three constants are all finite by e.g. Lemma
2.9
, and the last constant is strictly positive by e.g. Lemma
C.1
. Notice that
V
is a globally Lipchitz function on
I
with Lipchitz constant 1/2, i.e.,
|V(x)-V(a)|\leq\frac{1}{2}|x-a|,
thus using Cauchy-Schwarz and Markov’s inequality we see that

\displaystyle\int_{I}|V(a)-V(x)|\mathbf{1}_{\{|x-a|>x/2\}}\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\int_{I}(V(a)-V(x))^{2}\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg(\int_{I}\mathbf{1}_{\{|x-a|>x/2\}}\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\frac{1}{4}\int_{I}(a-x)^{2}\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg(\frac{\int_{I}e^{\frac{\sigma}{4}|x-a|}\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)}{e^{\frac{\sigma}{4}(x/2)}}\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\omega_{2}^{1/2}\omega_{\mathrm{exp}}^{1/2}\cdot e^{-\frac{\sigma x}{16}}.

Plugging this bound back into (
C.1
), we find that for
x>2
one has

P_{\mathbf{dif}}V(x)-V(x)\leq-\frac{\delta_{2}}{4}(x+1)^{-3/2}+\frac{3\omega_{3}}{48}(x/2)^{-5/2}+\frac{1}{2}\omega_{2}^{1/2}\omega_{\mathrm{exp}}^{1/2}\cdot e^{-\frac{\sigma x}{16}}.

The right side is clearly negative for sufficiently large
x>2
, thus proving the claim.

Existence of a Lyapunov drift function in
d=2
:
We set
V(x):=\sqrt{\log(1+|x|^{2})}.
A direct calculation reveals that for
|x|
large one has
\Delta V(x)\leq-\frac{1}{2|x|^{2}\log^{3/2}|x|}
.

We will show that
P_{\mathbf{dif}}V(x)\leq V(x)
for all sufficiently large
|x|
. For
|x|>2
and
a
within a ball of radius
|x|/2
of
x
, we can write

\displaystyle V(a)-V(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1,2}V_{j}(x)(a_{j}-x_{j})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1,2}V_{ij}(x)(a_{i}-x_{i})(a_{j}-x_{j})+R(a,x),

where by Taylor’s remainder theorem one has uniformly over all
x>2
and
a\in(x/2,3x/2)
the bound

|R(a,x)|\leq\frac{1}{6}|a-x|^{3}\sup_{b:|b-x|<|x|/2}\sum_{i,j,k=1,2}|V_{ijk}(b)|\leq C|x|^{-3}|a-x|^{3},

where
C
is some large enough constant as one may verify by direct calculation. Now recall that
\int_{I}(a-x)\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)=0
for all
x\in I
, which is immediate from the definitions.

Consequently we can disregard the first-order term when calculating
\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}V
and we find that

\displaystyle P_{\mathbf{dif}}V(x)-V(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{I}(V(a)-V(x))\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1,2}V_{ij}(x)\int_{I}(a_{i}-x_{i})(a_{j}-x_{j})\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)+C|x|^{-3}\int_{I}|a-x|^{3}\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)

(C.2)

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;+\int_{I}|V(a)-V(x)|\mathbf{1}_{\{|x-a|>x/2\}}\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a).

We also define the measure
\nu_{\mathrm{dif}}
by
\int_{I}f(u)\nu_{\mathrm{dif}}(\mathrm{d}u):=\int_{I^{2}}f(x-y)\nu(\mathrm{d}x)\nu(\mathrm{d}y),
and we recall from the assumptions in Theorem
4.10
that the covariance matrix of
\nu_{\mathrm{dif}}
is
\mathrm{Id}_{d\times d},
thus
\int_{I}u_{i}u_{j}\nu_{\mathrm{dif}}(\mathrm{d}u)=\delta_{ij}
for
i,j=1,2
. Since
\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}
is an SRI we have by Lemma
A.2

\bigg|\int_{I}u_{i}u_{j}\nu_{\mathrm{dif}}(\mathrm{d}u)(\mathrm{d}u)-\int_{I}(a_{i}-x_{i})(a_{j}-x_{j})\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}(\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{a})\bigg|\leq CF(|x|)^{1/2}.

One furthermore directly verifies that
|V_{ij}(x)|\leq C|x|^{-2}
for some sufficiently large
C>0
. This means that

(C.3)

\bigg|\frac{1}{2}\Delta V(x)-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1,2}V_{ij}(x)\int_{I}(a_{i}-x_{i})(a_{j}-x_{j})\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)\bigg|\leq C|x|^{-2}F(|x|)^{1/2}\leq C|x|^{-3},

where we are using the fact that the given conditions on
F
clearly imply that
F(|x|)
decays faster than
|x|^{-1}
(in fact exponentially).

Furthermore, just like in the
d=1
case above, one easily shows that

\int_{I}|V(a)-V(x)|\mathbf{1}_{\{|x-a|>x/2\}}\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)\leq Ce^{-c|x|}

for some
C,c>0
. In summary, we have
P_{\mathbf{dif}}V(x)-V(x)\leq\frac{1}{2}\Delta V(x)+C|x|^{-3}.
As noted above, we have
\Delta V(x)\leq-\frac{1}{2|x|^{2}\log^{3/2}|x|}
for sufficiently large
x
, which proves the claim.
∎

Proposition C.3

(Existence/Uniqueness/nontriviality for
d\geq 3
—transient case)
.

Let
d\geq 3
. With
\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}
as in Theorem
4.10
, there is a unique nontrivial invariant measure
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
up to scalar multiple. The measure
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
may be a signed measure, but its positive and negative parts both have constant growth at infinity.

We can rule out the possibility of a signed measure using the results of Theorems
4.16
and
4.17
which are proved Appendix
D
, showing that
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
is actually a nonnegative measure, but for now this is the best we can say.

Proof.

In
d\geq 3
, one cannot rely on recurrence techinques to prove the existence of invariant measures. This is because the chain is simply
not
recurrent. However, there is an easy alternative in higher dimensions: an explicit formula for invariant measure.

Recall that
I
is a locally compact additive subgroup of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, thus
I
has a Haar measure which we write
\mathrm{d}x
. In fact one may write
I=\Sigma(\mathbb{Z}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-m})
for some
d\times d
matrix
\Sigma
and
m\in\{0,...,d\}
, and then the measure
\mathrm{d}x
can just be identified with the product of counting measure and Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{Z}^{m}
and
\mathbb{R}^{d-m}
respectively.

Let
P_{\mathbf{dif}}
be the Markov operator for the Markov kernel
\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}
, and let
P_{\mathrm{RW}}
be the Markov operator for the simple random walk with increment law
\nu_{\mathrm{dif}}
. Then we claim that for
f:I\to\mathbb{R}
of exponential decay, the invariant measure acts on
f
explicitly by

\int_{I}f\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}=\int_{I}\big(P_{\mathrm{RW}}-\mathrm{Id}\big)\bigg(\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}P_{\mathbf{dif}}^{m}f\bigg)(x)\mathrm{d}x.

We need to justify why the integral converges if
f
is of exponential decay. We have the bound

\displaystyle\bigg|\big(P_{\mathrm{RW}}-\mathrm{Id}\big)\bigg(\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}P_{\mathbf{dif}}^{m}f\bigg)(x)\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\bigg|\big(P_{\mathbf{dif}}-\mathrm{Id}\big)\bigg(\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}P_{\mathbf{dif}}^{m}f\bigg)(x)\bigg|+\bigg|\big(P_{\mathrm{RW}}-P_{\mathbf{dif}}\big)\bigg(\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}P_{\mathbf{dif}}^{m}f\bigg)(x)\bigg|

\displaystyle=|f(x)|+\bigg|\big(P_{\mathrm{RW}}-P_{\mathbf{dif}}\big)\bigg(\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}P_{\mathbf{dif}}^{m}f\bigg)(x)\bigg|

(C.4)

\displaystyle\leq|f(x)|+F(|x|)\cdot\bigg\|\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}P_{\mathbf{dif}}^{m}f\bigg\|_{L^{\infty}(I)},

where the last bound follows from the definition of the SRI chain having decay function
F
. The exponential decay condition on
F
imply that it is indeed integrable with respect to the Haar measure on
I
. The fact that the infinite series converges and yields a bounded function is clear from the anticoncentration estimate in Theorem
4.7
, since it implies that
\|P_{\mathbf{dif}}^{m}f\|\leq Cm^{-d/2}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(I)}
which is summable over
m\in\mathbb{N}
as long as
d\geq 3
. That theorem is applicable because the topological irreducibility plus regularity conditions on
\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}
imply that the chain
\boldsymbol{b}
on
I^{k}
is indeed centered and
\delta
-repulsive for some
\delta>0
, see Lemma
C.1
. This justifies finiteness of the integral defining
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
.

Then it is clear that

\int_{I}P_{\mathbf{dif}}f\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}=\int_{I}\big(P_{\mathrm{RW}}-\mathrm{Id}\big)\bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}P_{\mathbf{dif}}^{m}\bigg)f(x)\mathrm{d}x=\int_{I}f\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}-\int_{I}\big(P_{\mathrm{RW}}-\mathrm{Id}\big)f(x)\mathrm{d}x=\int_{I}f\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}},

because we know that
\int_{I}\big(P_{\mathrm{RW}}-\mathrm{Id}\big)f(x)\mathrm{d}x=0
since the Haar measure
\mathrm{d}x
is easily checked to be invariant for
P_{\mathrm{R}W}.
Thus we see that
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
is indeed an invariant measure for
\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}},
and (
C.4
) easily implies that its positive and negative parts have constant growth at infinity.

Now we just need to justify the uniqueness. Consider two different invariant measures
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
, each of which may be signed measures whose positive and negative parts have constant growth at infinity. Define measures
\lambda_{i}
for
i=1,2
by the formula

\int_{I}f\;\mathrm{d}\lambda_{i}=\int_{I}\big(P_{\mathbf{dif}}-\mathrm{Id}\big)\bigg(\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}P_{\mathrm{RW}}^{m}f\bigg)(x)\;\;\pi_{i}(\mathrm{d}x),\;\;\;\;i=1,2.

Since this is just an “inversion” of the above formula for
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
, one then easily shows that the measures
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
must both be invariant for the random walk on
I
with increment distribution
\nu_{\mathrm{dif}}
. Thus, we just need to show that the random walk on
I
with increment distribution
\nu_{\mathrm{dif}}
has a unique invariant measure (up to scalar multiple) given by the Haar measure on
I
, which is a simpler problem.

Let
\lambda
be an invariant (signed) measure for the random walk with increment law
\nu_{\mathrm{dif}}
, of constant growth at infinity. We need to show that
\lambda
is a multiple of Haar measure. As a first step, we claim that the random walk with increment law
\nu_{\mathrm{dif}}
is not supported on a proper closed subgroup of
I
. This is where we need to use the irreducibility assumptions on
\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}
.

We assumed that
\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}
is continuous in TV norm in the
x
variable, thus so is the underlying random walk under translations. This means that
\nu_{\mathrm{dif}}
must have a
L^{1}
density with respect to the Haar measure on
I
, so that it cannot be supported on a proper closed subgroup.

Assuming without loss of generality that
I=\mathbb{Z}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-m}
for some
m\in\{0,...,d\}
we can then speak of the Fourier transform
\phi_{\nu}
of the measure
\nu
which is a complex-valued function on
\mathbb{T}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-m}
. Since
\nu_{\mathrm{dif}}
is not supported on a proper subgroup of
I
, the same holds true for
\nu
, and thus
|\phi_{\nu}(\xi)|=1
implies that
\xi=(0,...,0).
Now if we write the relation for
\lambda
to be an invariant measure (for the random walk with increment law
\nu_{\mathrm{dif}}
) in Fourier space, it reads
(|\phi_{\nu}(\xi)|^{2}-1)\widehat{\lambda}(\xi)=0
for all
\xi
, where one needs to interpret
\widehat{\lambda}
as a tempered distribution on
\mathbb{T}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-m}
(this is permissible because the constant growth condition on
\lambda
implies that it does have a finite value when paired against smooth functions of rapid decay on
\mathbb{T}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-m}
). The relation
(|\phi_{\nu}(\xi)|^{2}-1)\widehat{\lambda}(\xi)=0
means that
\widehat{\lambda}
must be a tempered distribution that is supported at the origin of
\mathbb{T}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-m}
. Since
\lambda
is assumed to be a signed measure of constant growth at infinity, this means that
\widehat{\lambda}
can only be
c\delta_{0}
for some
c\in\mathbb{R}
, which means by inverting the Fourier transform that
\lambda
itself must be
c
times Haar measure. This proves the uniqueness.

Nontriviality of
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
just follows from the explicit formula above. Indeed, take any strictly positive function
h
of exponential decay at infinity, and note that if we set
f:=(P_{\mathbf{dif}}-\mathrm{Id})\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}P_{\mathrm{RW}}^{m}h,
then one has
\int_{I}f\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}=\int_{I}h(x)\mathrm{d}x>0,
so that
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
cannot be the zero measure.∎

Combining the results of Propositions
C.2
and
C.3
immediately proves Theorem
4.10
.

Appendix D
Proof of Theorems
4.16
-
4.17
: Expectation limits for SRI chains

Now we are in a position to complete the proof of Theorem
4.16
. The key ingredients to proving this theorem will be the invariance principle of Theorem
4.6
together with the anticoncentration result of Theorem
4.7
and the uniqueness of
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
(up to scalar multiple) guaranteed by Propositions
C.2
and
C.3
. We shall start with the case
\ell=1
, then proceed inductively.

Proof of Theorem
4.16
.

Existence and uniqueness are immediate from the previous two propositions. It remains to prove the limit theorem for the expectations. For
d=1,2
it also remains to prove constant growth at infinity of
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
, but note that this is immediate from the expectation limit theorem and from the bound in Theorem
4.7
. For
d\geq 3
we must also rule out the possibility that
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
is a signed measure, but again this can be easily ruled out by the expectation limit theorem.

Thus it only remains to prove the expectation limit theorem. We break the proof into five steps. As always let
P_{\mathbf{dif}}
be the Markov operator associated to
\boldsymbol{b}_{\mathbf{dif}}
.

Step 1.
We first reduce to the case where all
f_{N}
are equal to some fixed function
f
. This is fairly straightforward given Theorem
4.7
. Indeed since we have by assumption
|f(x)|\leq H(|x|)
and
H
is of exponential decay, we have
|f_{N}(x)-f(x)|\leq\min\{\|f_{N}-f\|_{\infty},2H(|x|)\}\leq\|f_{N}-f\|_{\infty}^{1/2}(2H(|x|))^{1/2}
, and it follows from the latter theorem that

N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[|(f_{N}-f)(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})|]\leq C\|f_{N}-f\|_{L^{\infty}(I)}^{1/2}\cdot\bigg(N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[H(|R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r}|)^{1/2}]\bigg)\to 0,

which completes this reduction.

Step 2.
Take any function
f:I\to\mathbb{R}
such that
|f(x)|\leq H(|x|)
for some decreasing
H:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
of exponential decay at infinity. In this step, we will show that

\sup_{N\geq 1}N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[f(R^{1}_{s}-R^{2}_{s})]\leq C(H),

where the constant
C(H)
may depend on
H
but not on
N
or on any
f
dominated by
x\mapsto H(|x|)
as above. By the assumptions of Theorem
4.16
, we have that
|x^{i}_{N}-x^{j}_{N}|\geq cN^{1/2}
for
N\geq N_{0}
, where
N_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
and
c>0
. Thus, taking
K:=d
in Theorem
4.7
, we have for
N\geq N_{0}
that

\displaystyle\int_{I}f\;\mathrm{d}\gamma_{N}

\displaystyle\leq N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{s=1}^{Nt-1}r^{-d/2}\big(1+N^{1/2}cr^{-1/2}\big)^{-d}

\displaystyle=N^{-1}\sum_{s=1}^{Nt-1}\bigg(\frac{N}{r}\bigg)^{d/2}\bigg(1+c\sqrt{\frac{N}{r}}\bigg)^{-d}.

The latter is a Riemann sum approximation for
\int_{0}^{t}u^{-d/2}(1+cu^{-1/2})^{-d}\mathrm{d}u,
which is finite since
(1+cu^{-1/2})^{-d}\leq c^{-d}u^{d/2}.
Consequently, the last sum remains bounded in
N
.

Step 3.
We now claim that in Theorem
4.16
, the quantity
\phi(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}r)\big)
can be replaced by
\phi(\frac{1}{2}N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r}+R^{2}_{r}-2s_{N}r)\big)
without changing the value of the limit. To prove this, notice that

|\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}r)\big)-\phi\big(\tfrac{1}{2}N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r}+R^{2}_{r}-2s_{N}r)\big)\big|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\|\phi\|_{C^{1}}N^{-1/2}|R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r}|.

Thus setting
\tilde{f}_{N}(x)=|x||f_{N}(x)|,
we find that

\displaystyle N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}

\displaystyle\bigg|\sum_{r=0}^{Nt}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[\phi(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r}-s_{N}r)\big)f_{N}(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})]-\sum_{r=0}^{Nt}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[\phi\big(\tfrac{1}{2}N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r}+R^{2}_{r}-2s_{N}r)\big)f_{N}(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})]\big)\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq N^{-1/2}\|\phi\|_{C^{1}}\cdot N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[\tilde{f}_{N}(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})]

\displaystyle\leq CN^{-1/2}\|\phi\|_{C^{1}},

where we use the result of Step 1 in the last step, noting that
|x|\sup_{N}|f_{N}(x)|
still decays exponentially. Thus the difference tends to zero, which means we now need to show that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}

\displaystyle[\phi\big(\tfrac{1}{2}N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{r}+R^{2}_{r}-2s_{N}r)\big)f_{N}(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})]

(D.1)

\displaystyle=\bigg(\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}G(2s,x_{i}-x_{j})G(2s,y)\phi(\tfrac{1}{2}(y+x_{i}+x_{j}))\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}s\bigg)\int_{I}f\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}},

where
R^{1}_{r}
appearing inside
\phi
in the original formulation has now been replaced by
\tfrac{1}{2}(R^{1}_{r}+R^{2}_{r}).

We will see shortly that the latter formulation has certain advantages.

Step 4.
The remainder of the proof will focus on proving (
D.1
). Henceforth abbreviate

X^{N}_{r}:=\tfrac{1}{2}(R^{1}_{r}+R^{2}_{r}-2N^{-1}s_{N}),\;\;\;\;\;\;\;Y_{r}:=R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r}.

Consider the sequence
\gamma_{N}
of measures on
I
defined by

N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[\phi\big(N^{-1/2}X^{N}_{s}\big)f(Y_{s})].

We will show that any subsequence
\gamma_{N_{k}}
of this sequence of measures has a further subsequence
\gamma_{N_{k_{j}}}
converging as
N\to\infty
to an invariant measure
\gamma
for the Markov kernel
\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}
. By convergence, we mean that for all continuous
f:I\to\mathbb{R}
such that
|f(x)|\leq H(|x|)
, we have
\int_{I}f\;\mathrm{d}\gamma_{N}\to\int_{I}f\;\mathrm{d}\gamma
along this subsequence
\gamma_{N_{k_{j}}}
. By the uniqueness of
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
proved in Propositions
C.2
and
C.3
, this uniquely identifies any subsequential limit of
\gamma_{N}
as a scalar multiple of
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
. Notice that the ultimate goal of the theorem can be formulated as establishing convergence of the
entire
sequence
\gamma_{N}
to
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
multiplied by the constant
\big(\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}G(s,x_{1}-x_{2})G(s,y)\phi(y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}s\big),
which will be accomplished in the next step by identifying the correct constant.

Thus consider a subsequence
\gamma_{N_{k}}
. There is indeed a further convergent subsequence
\gamma_{N_{k_{j}}}
. This is because we can apply the Banach-Alaoglu theorem to the sequence of measures
\chi_{N}:=H(|x|)\gamma_{N}(\mathrm{d}x)
. By the result of Step 2, we have that

\displaystyle\sup_{N\geq 1}\|\chi_{N}\|_{TV}

\displaystyle=\sup_{N\geq 1}N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[H(|R^{1}_{s}-R^{2}_{s}|)]<\infty

so that
\|\gamma\|_{TV}<\infty.

Let us call this subsequential limit
\gamma
. To show that
\gamma
is an invariant measure, we need to show that for all continuous
f:I\to\mathbb{R}
, say of exponential decay at infinity, one has
\int_{I}P_{\mathbf{dif}}f\;\mathrm{d}\gamma=\int_{I}f\;\mathrm{d}\gamma.
To prove this, consider such
f
, say
|f(x)|\leq e^{-\theta|x|}
where
\theta\leq z_{0}/4
,
and define
\overline{f}:I^{k}\to\mathbb{R}
by
\overline{f}(\mathbf{x})=f(x_{1}-x_{2})
. To show that
\int_{I}P_{\mathbf{dif}}f\;\mathrm{d}\gamma=\int_{I}f\;\mathrm{d}\gamma,
it suffices to show that

(D.2)

\lim_{N\to\infty}N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[|(P^{\boldsymbol{b}}-P^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}})\overline{f}(\mathbf{R}_{s})|]=0.

To prove (
D.2
), we claim by the exponential decay assumption on
f
, one has the bound

(D.3)

|(P^{\boldsymbol{b}}-P^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}})\overline{f}(\mathbf{x})|\leq C\min\{d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{N}),e^{-\frac{\theta}{2}|x_{1}-x_{2}|}\}\leq Cd_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{N})^{1/2}e^{-\frac{\theta}{4}|x_{1}-x_{2}|}.

The second inequality is immediate from the first one using
\min\{u,v\}\leq u^{1/2}v^{1/2}
. To prove the first inequality, the upper bound of
Cd_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{N})
is clear simply from the definition of
d_{\mathrm{SRI}}
, since
|\overline{f}|\leq 1
. For the upper bound of
Ce^{-\frac{\theta}{2}|x_{1}-x_{2}|}
, we can show that both of the terms
P^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\overline{f}(\mathbf{x})
and
P^{\boldsymbol{b}}\overline{f}(\mathbf{x})
are bounded above by such a quantity. To show this, write the definition
P^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\overline{f}(\mathbf{x}):=\int_{I^{k}}f(y_{i}-y_{j})\boldsymbol{b}_{N}(\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}),
then split the integral into two parts:
\{\mathbf{y}:|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}|\leq\frac{1}{2}|x_{1}-x_{2}|\}
and
\{\mathbf{y}:|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}|>\frac{1}{2}|x_{1}-x_{2}|\}
, where
|\mathbf{x}|=\sum_{j=1}^{k}|x_{j}|
. On the first set, use the exponential decay bound on
f
to bound
|f(\mathbf{y})|\leq Ce^{-\frac{\theta}{2}|x_{1}-x_{2}|}
. On the second set, use the exponential moment bounds inherent in the definition of
\mathcal{E}_{k\times d}^{\mathrm{SRI}}(\Psi_{\sigma},M,F)
together with Markov’s inequality (and
|f|\leq 1
) to obtain an upper bound of
Ce^{-\frac{\sigma}{8}|x_{1}-x_{j}|}\leq Ce^{-\frac{\theta}{2}|x_{1}-x_{2}|}.

With (
D.3
) proved, the claim (
D.2
) follows immediately from Theorem
4.7
. Now let us show why (
D.2
) implies that the subsequential limit
\gamma
is indeed an invariant measure. We have
P^{\boldsymbol{b}}\overline{f}(\mathbf{R}_{s})=P_{\mathbf{dif}}f(R^{1}_{s}-R^{2}_{s})
so that

(D.4)

N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[\phi\big(N^{-1/2}X^{N}_{s})\big)P^{\boldsymbol{b}}\overline{f}(\mathbf{R}_{s})]\to\int_{I}P_{\mathbf{dif}}f\;\mathrm{d}\gamma

along the subsequence
N_{k_{j}}
. Moreover, by the Markov property and a summation by parts, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[\phi(N^{-1/2}X^{N}_{s})(P^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}-\mathrm{Id})\overline{f}(\mathbf{R}_{s})]=\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[\phi(N^{-1/2}X^{N}_{s})(\overline{f}(\mathbf{R}_{s+1})-\overline{f}(\mathbf{R}_{s}))]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[\phi(N^{-1/2}X_{Nt})\overline{f}(\mathbf{R}_{Nt})]-\phi(N^{-1/2}\mathbf{x}_{N})\overline{f}(\mathbf{x}_{N})

(D.5)

\displaystyle-\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[(\phi(N^{-1/2}X^{N}_{s+1})-\phi(N^{-1/2}X^{N}_{s}))\overline{f}(\mathbf{R}_{s+1})]

We claim that all three terms on the right side go to zero when multiplied by
N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}.
The first two terms go to zero because Theorem
4.7
implies that
N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[f(R^{1}_{Nt}-R^{2}_{Nt})]\to 0
, and because the assumption
N^{-1/2}|x^{1}_{N}-x^{2}_{N}|\not\to 0
guarantees that
N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}f(x^{1}_{N}-x^{2}_{N})\to 0
. The third term tends to zero because by conditioning at time
s
and then using Theorem
4.7
we have the bound

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[(\phi(N^{-1/2}X_{s+1})-\phi(N^{-1/2}X^{N}_{s}))\overline{f}(\mathbf{R}_{s+1})]

\displaystyle\leq\|\phi\|_{C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}N^{-1/2}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[|X^{N}_{s+1}-X^{N}_{s}|^{q^{\prime}}]^{1/q^{\prime}}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[\overline{f}(\mathbf{R}_{s})^{q}]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\phi\|_{C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}N^{-1/2}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[\overline{f}^{q}(\mathbf{R}_{s})]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq\|\phi\|_{C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}N^{-1/2}s^{-\frac{d}{2q}}\big(1+s^{-1/2}|x_{N}^{1}-x_{N}^{2}|)^{-K}

\displaystyle\leq\|\phi\|_{C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}N^{-1/2}s^{-\frac{d}{2q}}\big(1+cs^{-1/2}N^{1/2})^{-K}.

Here
q,q^{\prime}
are conjugate Holder exponents and this is valid for any
q
. In the last line, we used
|x^{1}_{N}-x^{2}_{N}|>cN^{1/2}
by assumption. Now we need to multiply by
N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}
and show that the last expression summed from
s=1
to
s=Nt
tends to 0. Taking
q<\frac{d}{d-1}
will show that it tends to 0 using a Riemann sum interpretation.

Since (
D.5
) has been shown to vanish, (
D.4
) thus shows that along the subsequence
N_{k_{j}}
we have that

(D.6)

N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[\phi\big(N^{-1/2}X^{N}_{s})\big)P^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\overline{f}(\mathbf{R}_{s})]\to\int_{I}f\;\mathrm{d}\gamma.

Combining (
D.4
) and (
D.6
) with (
D.2
), we do indeed see that
\int_{I}f\;\mathrm{d}\gamma=\int_{I}P_{\mathbf{dif}}f\;\mathrm{d}\gamma,
proving that
\gamma
is indeed an invariant measure.

Step 5.
Recall the measures
\gamma_{N}
from the previous step. Define the function
\mathfrak{f}:=(P_{\mathbf{dif}}-\mathrm{Id})u
where
u(x):=\mathrm{sign}(2-d)\cdot(1+|x|)^{2-d}
for
d\neq 2
and
u(x)=\log(1+|x|)
for
d=2
. Recall we defined the
unit normalization
of
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
as the one in which
\int_{I}\mathfrak{f}\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}=1.
Also recall that we define the dimension-dependent constants
c_{1}=1,c_{2}=2\pi,
and
c_{d}=d(d-2)/\Gamma(1+\frac{d}{2})
for
d\geq 3
.

We need to check that
\int_{I}\mathfrak{f}\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}<\infty
to ensure that the unit normalization of
\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
is well-defined. This will follow from showing that

(D.7)

|\mathfrak{f}(x)|\leq C|x|^{-d-1}

for
|x|>1
. A direct calculation with the Laplacian formula in polar coordinates reveals that

\Delta u(x)=|x|^{-1}(1+|x|)^{-2}
in
d=2
and
\Delta u(x)=(2-d)(1-d)|x|^{-1}(1+|x|)^{-d}
for
d\neq 2
and
x\neq 0
, so in particular we have the bound
|\Delta u(x)|\leq C_{d}|x|^{-d-1}
for
|x|>1
in all dimensions
d\geq 1
. The third-order derivatives also satisfy
|u_{ijk}(x)|\leq C|x|^{-d-1}.
From here and Lemma
A.2
and the fact that the covariance matrix of
\nu
is
\mathrm{Id}_{d\times d}
, one may show (very similarly to the proof of existence of Lyapunov functions in
d=2
above) by third order Taylor expansion that

|\mathfrak{f}(x)|\leq C|x|^{-d-1}

for
|x|>1
. This implies that
\mathfrak{f}
is indeed in
L^{1}(\pi^{\mathrm{inv}})
.

We will prove in this step that
\int_{I}\mathfrak{f}\;\mathrm{d}\gamma_{N}\to c_{d}\cdot\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}G(s,x_{1}-x_{2})G(s,y)\phi(y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}s
. If we can prove this, then the theorem would be proved, because by the uniqueness proved in Propositions
C.2
and
C.3
, together with the result of Step 4, this would mean that any subsequential limit
\gamma
of the sequence
\gamma_{N}
must be a constant multiple of
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
. But the convergence of
\int_{I}\mathfrak{f}\;\mathrm{d}\gamma_{N}
to the correct quantity uniquely identifies the constant as the one appearing in the theorem statement. If every subsequence of
\gamma_{N}
has a further subsequence converging to some fixed measure, then the sequence
\gamma_{N}
must itself converge to that fixed measure, thus completing the proof.

Letting
\overline{u}(\mathbf{x}):=u(x_{1}-x_{2})
and
\overline{f}_{N}(\mathbf{x}):=(P^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}-\mathrm{Id})\overline{u}
, both of which are functions on
I^{2}
, we claim that

(D.8)

|\mathfrak{f}(x_{1}-x_{2})-\overline{\mathfrak{f}}_{N}(\mathbf{x})|\leq d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{N})^{1/4}F(|x_{1}-x_{2}|)^{1/4}.

for some large enough constant
C>0
.
This is indeed fairly immediate from Lemma
A.2
and the definition of SRI chains, see (
A.2
) for the proof of a similar claim.

With (
D.8
) established, we find that

\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt-1}|\mathfrak{f}(R_{r}^{1}-R_{r}^{2})-\overline{\mathfrak{f}}_{N}(\mathbf{R}_{r})|\bigg]\leq\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{N})^{1/4}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt-1}F(|R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r}|)^{1/4}\bigg].

Using Theorem
4.7
and the conditions on
F
, we may immediately conclude that the above expectation is bounded above by
Cd_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{N})^{1/4}
and thus tends to zero. Thus, in order to complete this step, we need to calculate the limit

(D.9)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\bigg[N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt-1}\phi(N^{-1/2}X^{N}_{s})\overline{\mathfrak{f}}_{N}(\mathbf{R}_{r})\bigg],

where we remind the reader that
X^{N}_{r}:=\tfrac{1}{2}(R^{i}_{r}+R^{j}_{r}-2N^{-1}s_{N})
is a shorthand notation. Recalling that
\overline{\mathfrak{f}}_{N}=(P^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}-\mathrm{Id})\overline{u}
, we have by the Markov property and a summation by parts that

\displaystyle N

{}^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[\phi(N^{-1/2}X^{N}_{s})\overline{\mathfrak{f}}_{N}(\mathbf{R}_{s})]=N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[\phi(N^{-1/2}X^{N}_{s})(P^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}-\mathrm{Id})\overline{u}(\mathbf{R}_{s})]

\displaystyle=N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[\phi(N^{-1/2}X^{N}_{s})(\overline{u}(\mathbf{R}_{s+1})-\overline{u}(\mathbf{R}_{s}))]

\displaystyle=N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\bigg(\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[\phi(N^{-1/2}X_{Nt})\overline{u}(\mathbf{R}_{Nt})]-\phi(N^{-1/2}\mathbf{x}_{N})\overline{u}(\mathbf{x}_{N})

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;-\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[(\phi(N^{-1/2}X^{N}_{s+1})-\phi(N^{-1/2}X^{N}_{s}))\overline{u}(\mathbf{R}_{s})]

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;-\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[(\phi(N^{-1/2}X^{N}_{s+1})-\phi(N^{-1/2}X^{N}_{s}))(\overline{u}(\mathbf{R}_{s+1})-\overline{u}(\mathbf{R}_{s}))]\bigg).

Now, we need to take the limit of each of the four terms of the last expression. Let us first deal with the first two terms at once. For this, it will be useful to define the function

V_{t}(x):=\begin{cases}\mathbf{E}_{BM}[|B_{t}-x|],&d=1\\
\mathbf{E}_{BM}[\log|B_{t}-x|],&d=2\\
-\mathbf{E}_{BM}[|B_{t}-x|^{2-d}],&d\geq 3\end{cases}

where
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},t\geq 0
, and the expectation is with respect to the standard Brownian motion of rate 2 on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, started from the origin. Then by the invariance principle of Theorem
4.6
, and the assumptions on the sequence
\mathbf{x}_{N}
, we have that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}

\displaystyle N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\bigg(\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[\phi(N^{-1/2}X_{Nt})\overline{u}(\mathbf{R}_{Nt})]-\phi(N^{-1/2}\mathbf{x}_{N})\overline{u}(\mathbf{x}_{N})\bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}_{\boldsymbol{x}}^{BM^{\otimes k}}[\phi(\tfrac{1}{2}(B^{i}_{t}+B^{j}_{t}))V_{0}(B^{i}_{t}-B^{j}_{t})]-\phi(\tfrac{1}{2}(x_{1}+x_{2}))V_{0}(x_{1}-x_{2})

(D.10)

\displaystyle=\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}G(2t,y)\phi(\tfrac{1}{2}(y+x_{1}+x_{2}))\mathrm{d}y\bigg)V_{t}(x_{1}-x_{2})-\phi(\tfrac{1}{2}(x_{1}+x_{2}))V_{0}(x_{1}-x_{2}).

All expectations are with respect to a standard
2d
-dimensional Brownian motion started from
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},x_{2})
. In the third line we are using the independence of
B^{1}+B^{2}
from
B^{1}-B^{2}
, which implies that
\mathbf{E}_{BM^{\otimes k}}[\phi(\tfrac{1}{2}(B^{1}_{t}+B^{2}_{t}))V_{0}(B^{1}_{t}-B^{2}_{t})]=\mathbf{E}_{BM^{\otimes k}}[\phi(\tfrac{1}{2}(B^{1}_{t}+B^{2}_{t}))]\mathbf{E}_{BM^{\otimes k}}[V_{0}(B^{1}_{t}-B^{2}_{t})]=\mathbf{E}_{BM^{\otimes k}}[\phi(\tfrac{1}{2}(B^{1}_{t}+B^{2}_{t}))]V_{t}(x_{1}-x_{2})
. The reason that the invariance principle applies in this situation is because e.g. in
d\geq 3
we can write
N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\overline{u}(\mathbf{R}_{Nt})=(N^{-1/2}+N^{-1/2}|R^{1}_{Nt}-R^{2}_{Nt}|)^{2-d}
, and we can therefore use the fact that the sequence of functions
|N^{-1/2}+x|^{2-d}
converges for
x\neq 0
to the function
|x|^{2-d}=V_{0}(x)
. Notice that when
d\geq 2
, the function
V_{0}
does have a singularity at the origin, but fortunately the uniform integrability implied by Theorem
4.7
(which guarantees for instance that
\sup_{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}\big[\big|N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}(1+\big|R^{1}_{Nt}-R^{2}_{Nt}|)^{2-d}\big|^{q}\big]<\infty
for any
q\in[1,\frac{d}{d-2})
if
d\geq 3
) does imply convergence of the associated expectations.

Now we will take the limit of the third term
N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[(\phi(N^{-1/2}X^{N}_{s+1})-\phi(N^{-1/2}X^{N}_{s}))\overline{u}(\mathbf{R}_{s})]
. Apply a third-order Taylor expansion of
\phi
, and we see that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[(\phi(N^{-1/2}X^{N}_{s+1})-\phi(N^{-1/2}X^{N}_{s}))|\mathcal{F}_{s}]

\displaystyle=N^{-1}\Delta\phi(X_{s})+\mathbf{Er}_{N}(\phi,s),

where the error term satisfies

|\mathbf{Er}_{N}(\phi,s)|\leq C\|\phi\|_{C^{3}}N^{-3/2}+CN^{-1/2}\|\phi\|_{C^{2}}d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})^{1/4}F(R^{1}_{s}-R^{2}_{s})^{1/4}.

Indeed the first order term
N^{-1/2}\nabla\phi(X_{s})\bullet\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[X^{N}_{s+1}-X^{N}_{s}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]
can be absorbed into
\mathbf{E}_{N}
because
\boldsymbol{b}_{N}
is converging to a centered chain (for which the conditional expectation would be exactly zero), thus one has that
|\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[X^{N}_{s+1}-X^{N}_{s}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]|\leq Cd_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{0})^{1/4}F(|R^{1}_{s}-R^{2}_{s}|)^{1/4}
by e.g. (
A.2
). Likewise all of the “off-diagonal” second partials involving terms of the form
\partial_{12}\phi(X_{s}^{N})
would be absorbed into
\mathbf{Er}_{N}
for a very similar reason, noting that the covariance matrix of the base measure of the limiting chain
\boldsymbol{b}
is
\mathrm{Id}_{d\times d}
(see the proof of Proposition
C.2
above for a very similar calculation). The remainder terms involving third derivatives all come with a factor of
N^{-3/2}
.

Using Theorem
4.7
and the preceding bound, one may convince oneself that
\mathbf{Er}_{N}
does not contribute to the limit, so that by the invariance principle (Theorem
4.6
), one then has

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[(\phi(N^{-1/2}X^{N}_{s+1})-\phi(N^{-1/2}X^{N}_{s}))\overline{u}(\mathbf{R}_{s})]=\lim_{N\to\infty}N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}N^{-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[\Delta\phi(X_{s})\overline{u}(\mathbf{R}_{s})]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{E}_{BM^{\otimes 2}}[\Delta\phi(B^{1}_{s}+B^{2}_{s})V_{0}(B^{i}_{s}-B^{j}_{s})]\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}G(2s,y)(\Delta\phi)(\tfrac{1}{2}(y+x_{1}+x_{2}))\mathrm{d}y\bigg)V_{s}(x_{1}-x_{2})\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{s}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}G(2s,y)\phi(\tfrac{1}{2}(y+x_{1}+x_{2}))\mathrm{d}y\bigg)V_{s}(x_{1}-x_{2})\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}G(2t,y)\phi(\tfrac{1}{2}(y+x_{1}+x_{2}))\mathrm{d}y\bigg)V_{t}(x_{1}-x_{2})-\phi(\tfrac{1}{2}(x_{1}+x_{2}))V_{0}(x_{1}-x_{2})

(D.11)

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;-\int_{0}^{t}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}G(2s,y)\phi(\tfrac{1}{2}(y+x_{1}+x_{2}))\mathrm{d}y\bigg)(\partial_{s}V_{s})(x_{1}-x_{2})\mathrm{d}s.

In the third line we are using the independence of
B^{1}+B^{2}
from
B^{1}-B^{2}
, which implies that
\mathbf{E}_{BM^{\otimes 2}}[\phi(\tfrac{1}{2}(B^{1}_{t}+B^{2}_{t}))V_{0}(B^{1}_{t}-B^{2}_{t})]=\mathbf{E}_{BM^{\otimes 2}}[\phi(\tfrac{1}{2}(B^{1}_{t}+B^{2}_{t}))]\mathbf{E}_{BM^{\otimes 2}}[V_{0}(B^{1}_{t}-B^{2}_{t})]=\mathbf{E}_{BM^{\otimes 2}}[\phi(\tfrac{1}{2}(B^{1}_{t}+B^{2}_{t}))]V_{t}(x_{1}-x_{2})
since
G_{s}*V_{0}=V_{s}
. In the fifth line, we simply integrated by parts.

Next we argue that the fourth term goes to 0, i.e.,

\lim_{N\to\infty}N^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[(\phi(N^{-1/2}X^{N}_{s+1})-\phi(N^{-1/2}X^{N}_{s}))(\overline{u}(\mathbf{R}_{s+1})-\overline{u}(\mathbf{R}_{s}))].

The proof uses Hölder’s inequality is essentially verbatim with the “Term 4” argument in the proofs of Theorem
4.23
and
4.25
. We do not repeat it here.

Subtracting the results of (
D.10
) and (
D.11
), we have obtained that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\int_{I}\mathfrak{f}\;\mathrm{d}\gamma_{N}=\int_{0}^{t}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}G(2s,y)\phi(\tfrac{1}{2}(y+x_{1}+x_{2}))\mathrm{d}y\bigg)(\partial_{s}V_{s})(x_{1}-x_{2})\mathrm{d}s.

Now, simply note that
\partial_{s}V_{s}=c_{d}\cdot G(2s,\bullet)
for the universal constant
c_{d}
defined earlier. In particular, the subsequential limits of
\gamma_{N}
are unique, as it must be the unique scalar multiple
c\cdot\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
under which
c\int_{I}\mathfrak{f}\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}=c_{d}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{I}G(2s,y)\phi(\tfrac{1}{2}(y+x_{1}+x_{2}))G(2s,x_{1}-x_{2})\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}s.
Recalling that
\int_{I}\mathfrak{f}\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}=1
gives the result.∎

Proof of Theorem
4.17
.

The
d=1
statement follows from exactly the same proof as that of Theorem
4.16
, with no changes necessary. Thus, we focus on the
d=2
statement, which needs the appropriate modifications. In particula,r we need to explain why the correct factor is
\frac{1}{2}
multiplying
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
, which is the substance of the theorem. The proof starts similarly to that of Theorem
4.16
, using the same subsequence trick.

As in Step 1 in the proof of Theorem
4.16
, it suffices to show that one has

(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[f(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})]\to\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}f\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}

for a fixed function
f
, rather than a sequence
f_{N}
as in Assumption
4.14
. Consider the sequence of measures given by

\gamma_{N}(f):=(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[f(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})].

First we show that every subsequence
\gamma_{N_{k}}
has a further converging subsequence
\gamma_{N_{k_{j}}}
converging as
N\to\infty
to a measure
\gamma
which is a scalar multiple of the invariant measure
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
. As in the previous theorem, by convergence, we mean that for all continuous
f:I\to\mathbb{R}
such that
|f(x)|\leq H(|x|)
for some decreasing
H:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
such that
H(t)\leq Ae^{-bt}
with
A,b>0
, we have
\int_{I}f\;\mathrm{d}\gamma_{N}\to\int_{I}f\;\mathrm{d}\gamma
along this subsequence
\gamma_{N_{k_{j}}}
.

Consider a subsequence
\gamma_{N_{k}}
. Consider the sequence
\chi_{N}:=H(|x|)\gamma_{N}(\mathrm{d}x)
for any fixed
H:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
that is decreasing and satisfies
H(t)\leq Ae^{-bt}
with
A,b>0
. By the Banach-Alaoglu Theorem, there is a further subsequence
\gamma_{N_{k_{j}}}
of
\gamma_{N_{k}}
that converges. Let us call this subsequential limit
\gamma
. The fact that
\sup_{N}\|\chi_{N}\|_{TV}<\infty
(so that
\gamma
also has a finite total variation norm, and hence is a locally finite measure) is due to the fact that by Corollary
4.8
, one has

\displaystyle\|\chi_{N}\|_{TV}

\displaystyle=\int_{I}H(|x|)d\gamma_{N}=(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[H(|R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r}|)]\leq(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{N}C\cdot Ab^{-d}\cdot r^{-1}\leq C\cdot Ab^{-d}.

Next, we show that any subsequential limit
\gamma
must be an invariant measure. To prove this, we need to show that for all continuous
f:I\to\mathbb{R}
, say of exponential decay at infinity, one has
\int_{I}P_{\mathbf{dif}}f\;\mathrm{d}\gamma=\int_{I}f\;\mathrm{d}\gamma.
To prove this, consider
f
such that
|f(x)|\leq e^{-\theta|x|}
where
\theta\leq z_{0}/4
. We first show that

(D.12)

\lim_{N\to\infty}(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[|(P^{\boldsymbol{b}}-P^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}})f(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})|]=0.

Exactly as in (
D.3
), we have that

(D.13)

|(P^{\boldsymbol{b}}-P^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}})f(\mathbf{x})|\leq C\min\{d_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{N}),e^{-\frac{\theta}{2}|x_{i}-x_{j}|}\}\leq Cd_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{N})^{1/2}e^{-\frac{\theta}{4}|x_{i}-x_{j}|}.

We therefore have

\displaystyle(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[|(P^{\boldsymbol{b}}-P^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}})f(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})|]\leq(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[|Cd_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{N})^{1/2}e^{-\frac{\theta}{4}|R_{r}^{1}-R_{r}^{2}|}|].

It follows from Corollary
4.8
that
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[|e^{-\frac{\theta}{4}|R_{r}^{1}-R_{r}^{2}|}|]\leq Cr^{-1}
so we have

\displaystyle(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[|Cd_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{N})^{1/2}e^{-\frac{\theta}{4}|R_{r}^{1}-R_{r}^{2}|}|]

\displaystyle\leq(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{N}Cd_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{N})^{1/2}r^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq Cd_{\mathrm{SRI}}(\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{b}_{N})^{1/2}.

This last term goes to
0
as
N\to\infty
due to the assumption that
\boldsymbol{b}_{N}
converges to
\boldsymbol{b}
.

We have
P^{\boldsymbol{b}}f(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})=P_{\mathbf{dif}}f(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})
so that

(D.14)

(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[P^{\boldsymbol{b}}f(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})]\to\int_{I}P_{\mathbf{dif}}f\;\mathrm{d}\gamma

along the subsequence
N_{k_{j}}
.

Finally, by the Markov property, we have

\displaystyle(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[(P^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}-\mathrm{Id})f(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})]

\displaystyle=(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[(f(R^{1}_{r+1}-R^{2}_{r+1})-f(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r}))]

\displaystyle=(\log N)^{-1}\big(\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[f(R^{1}_{N+1}-R^{2}_{N+1})]-f(x^{1}_{N}-x^{2}_{N})\big).

This again goes to
0
by Corollary
4.8
, implying that along the subsequence
N_{k_{j}}
we have that

(D.15)

(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[P^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}f(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})]\to\int_{I}f\;\mathrm{d}\gamma.

Combining (
D.13
), (
D.14
), (
D.15
), we have
\int_{I}f\;\mathrm{d}\gamma=\int_{I}P_{\mathbf{dif}}f\;\mathrm{d}\gamma,
proving that
\gamma
is an invariant measure.

Finally, we show that all of these subsequential limits agree, in other words, the invariant measures have the same normalizing factors. To show this we take the particular choice
\mathfrak{f}=(P_{\mathbf{dif}}-\mathrm{Id})u(\mathbf{x})
where
u(\mathbf{x}):=\log(1+|x_{i}-x_{j}|).
In this case, the invariance principle of Theorem
4.6
easily implies that

\displaystyle(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{N-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[f(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})]

\displaystyle=(\log N)^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{N-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[\log(1+|R^{i}_{r+1}-R^{j}_{r+1}|)-\log(1+|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}|)]

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[\log(1+|R^{1}_{N}-R^{2}_{N}|)]-\log(1+|x^{i}_{N}-x^{j}_{N}|)}{\log N}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{\boldsymbol{b}_{N}}[\log(N^{-1/2}+N^{-1/2}|R^{1}_{N}-R^{2}_{N}|)]-\log(1+|x^{i}_{N}-x^{j}_{N}|)}{\log N}

(D.16)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle N\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}\frac{1}{2}.

In the last line, we used the invariance principle and the assumption that
|x^{i}_{N}-x^{j}_{N}|
remains bounded to conclude that the numerator remains bounded.

Note that
\frac{1}{2}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}\mathfrak{f}\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
when
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
is under the unit normalization.
This means that every subsequence of
\gamma_{N}
has a further subsequence converging to
\frac{1}{2}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
. Thus the sequence
\gamma_{N}
must itself converge to
\frac{1}{2}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
.
∎

Of course, the above proof is only valid in
d=2
, although at first glance, this logarithm trick appears to work in every dimension. However, for
d=1
,
\gamma_{N}
has no subsequential limits in the first place since (
D.7
) would not hold for
d=1
if we replaced
u(x)=1+|x|
with
\log(1+|x|)
. Similarly, for
d>2
the function
u
does not decay sufficiently fast at infinity to be integrable with respect to
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
, thus
d=2
is the only case where
\int_{I}(P_{\mathbf{dif}}-\mathrm{Id})\log(1+|\bullet|)\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
is actually guaranteed to exist.
